Quantitative concatenation for polynomial box norms
Noah Kravitz, Borys Kuca, James Leng

Abstract
. Using PET and quantitative concatenation techniques, we establish box-norm control with the “expected” directions for counting operators for general multidimensional polynomial progressions, with at most polynomial losses in the parameters. Such results are often useful first steps towards obtaining explicit upper bounds on sets lacking instances of given such progressions. In the companion paper [ 20 ] , we complete this program for sets in [ N ] 2 [N]^{2} lacking nondegenerate progressions of the form ( x , y ) , ( x + P ⁡ ( z ) , y ) , ( x , y + P ⁡ ( z ) ) (x,y),(x+P(z),y),(x,y+P(z)) , where P ∈ ℤ ⁡ [ z ] P\in\mathbb{Z}[z] is any fixed polynomial with an integer root of multiplicity 1 1 .



1.
Introduction

One of the starting points of arithmetic combinatorics is Roth’s Theorem
[
36
]
, which says that every subset of the natural numbers with positive upper Banach density contains a nontrivial
3
-term arithmetic progression
x,\;x+z,\;x+2z
. Szemerédi
[
40
]
famously extended this result to
\ell
-term arithmetic progressions for all
\ell
. The furthest-reaching generalization of this line of work is the Polynomial Szemerédi Theorem of Bergelson and Leibman
[
3
]
, which asserts that positive-upper-Banach-density subsets of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
always contain nontrivial “polynomial progressions” under certain weak local conditions on the polynomials involved.

The quantitative bounds from Roth’s original proof of his theorem have been improved many times, most recently by Kelley and Meka
[
18
]
. Szemerédi provided effective, albeit enormous, bounds for his theorem, and Gowers’ work
[
13
]
on obtaining more “reasonable” bounds inaugurated the field of higher-order Fourier analysis, where recent developments have led to improved bounds for Szemerédi’s Theorem
[
26
]
. The proof of Bergelson and Leibman
[
3
]
, by contrast, uses ergodic-theoretic methods and is completely ineffective. Obtaining “reasonable” bounds for the Bergelson–Leibman Theorem is a difficult problem that was highlighted by Gowers e.g. in
[
12
]
.

In an early development, Sárközy
[
37
,
38
]
obtained bounds for nonlinear polynomial progressions of length two. Following on Gowers’ breakthrough, Green
[
16
]
and Prendiville
[
34
]
later studied arithmetic progressions in which the difference is restricted to an image set of a fixed homogeneous polynomial.
In parallel to these developments, Bourgain and Chang
[
4
]
, Peluse
[
27
]
, and Dong, Li, and Sawin
[
6
]
examined
x,\;x+z,\;x+z^{2}
and other length-3 linearly independent polynomial patterns over
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
. This culminated in a spectacular work of Peluse
[
28
]
that introduced the by-now standard degree-lowering technique to obtain bounds for arbitrarily long polynomial progressions with linearly independent polynomials over the finite fields. Building on that, Peluse and Prendiville
[
31
]
gave an effective polynomial Szemerédi theorem for the progression
x,\;x+z,\;x+z^{2}
. Peluse
[
28
]
then extended this method to obtain reasonable bounds for polynomial progressions involving polynomials with distinct degrees. Quite a few quantitative polynomial Szemerédi theorems
[
2
,
17
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
33
,
39
]
have been proven in recent years, and our work
[
20
]
can be seen as progress in this direction.

Most arguments in this line of research have begun by using the PET
1
1

1

Some sources record that “PET” stands for “polynomial ergodic theorem” or “polynomial exhaustion technique”.
induction scheme of Bergelson
[
1
]
to show that the counting operator for the associated polynomial progression is bounded by an appropriate Gowers norm or, more generally, an appropriate box norm.
2
2

2

For the definitions of the concepts appearing in the introduction, including Gowers norms, box norms and multiplicative derivatives, we refer the reader to Section
3
. The details of the PET induction scheme are elucidated in Section
4
.
Green and Tao
[
15
]
showed that iterated applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the style of Gowers
[
11
,
13
]
suffice to give Gowers-norm-control for positive-complexity linear configurations in any number of dimensions. For
1
-dimensional polynomial progressions in the finite-field setting, a naïve application of the PET argument produces the required norm-control. For example, if the counting operator

\displaystyle\sum_{x,z\in{\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}}}f_{0}(x)f_{1}(x+z)f_{2}(x+z^{2})

is large for some
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}:{\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
(where
N
is a large prime), then a short PET argument gives that the Gowers norm
\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{U^{3}({\mathbb{Z}}/{\mathbb{N}}{\mathbb{Z}})}
is also large, with polynomial dependences. In the integer setting, by contrast, the PET argument alone does not suffice. If the counting operator

\displaystyle\sum_{x,z\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f_{0}(x)f_{1}(x+z)f_{2}(x+z^{2})

is large for some
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]:=\{1,\ldots,N\}
for some large
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then PET gives only the largeness of the quantity

\displaystyle\sum_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[N^{1/2}]}\sum_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}\in[N^{1/2}]}\sum_{x}\Delta_{h_{1}m_{1},h_{2}m_{2},(h_{1}+h_{2})m_{3}}f_{2}(x).

This expression is essentially an average of many “arithmetic box norms” along progressions with common differences

(1)

\displaystyle h_{1},h_{2},h_{1}+h_{2}.

For more complicated
1
-dimensional polynomial progressions, the common differences arising are
multilinear
polynomials in parameters
h_{1},h_{2},\ldots
(meaning that they are linear combinations of monomials
h_{1}^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots h_{r}^{\varepsilon_{r}}
with
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{r}\in\{0,1\}
; in other words, they are of degree at most 1 in each variable). At this point, it is desirable to “concatenate” these norms into a single Gowers norm of higher degree, so that one can use the numerous techniques involving the inverse theory of the Gowers norms.

The first such concatenation result was proven by Tao and Ziegler
[
41
]
. Their approach was infinitary, however, and gave no quantitative bounds on the losses incurred while passing from an average of box norms to a single concatenated Gowers norm. In
[
29
,
31
]
, Peluse and Prendiville developed a different approach that could provide quantitative bounds
3
3

3

See also
[
32
]
, in which Peluse, Prendiville and Shao extend the quantitative concatenation approach from
[
31
]
to obtain Gowers-norm-control for the configuration
(x_{1},x_{2}),\;(x_{1}+z,x_{2}),\;(x_{1},x_{2}+z^{2})
.
. They showed, among other things, that the averages of box norms arising from applying PET to
1
-dimensional polynomial progressions

(2)

\displaystyle x,\;x+P_{1}(z),\;\ldots,\;x+P_{\ell}(z)

can be concatenated into a single Gowers norm that control the counting operator for (
2
) with only polynomial losses. This concatenation result was instrumental in their proof of quantitative bounds for subsets of
[N]
lacking progressions (
2
) in the case where
P_{1},\ldots,P_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[z]
have distinct degrees.

The situation is even more complicated in higher dimensions. Consider, for example, the
multidimensional
polynomial pattern

(3)

(x_{1},x_{2}),(x_{1}+z^{2}+z,x_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2}+z^{2}+z).

This time, we
would like to show that if

(4)

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},z\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})f_{1}(x_{1}+z^{2}+z,x_{2})f_{2}(x_{1},x_{2}+z^{2}+z)

is large
for some
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{2}
, then so is
4
4

4

Naturally, we also want to obtain the largeness of certain averages of box norms of
f_{0},f_{1}
, with
\{{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\}
is replaced by
\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}
or
\{{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}
, respectively.

\displaystyle\sum_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{2t}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\Delta_{h_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\ldots,h_{t}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},h_{t+1}({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}),\ldots,h_{2t}({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})}f_{2}(x_{1},x_{2})

for some positive integer
t=O(1)
, where
{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
are the usual coordinate vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and
\Delta
refers to the symmetric multiplicative derivative (defined in (
19
) below). (From here, one can hope to use degree-lowering arguments to decrease
t
to its optimal value.) The usual PET argument gives control of (
4
) by an average of degree-
7
box norms of
f_{2}
in the directions

(5)

\displaystyle 2(h_{2}+h_{3})({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})+2h_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\ 2h_{2}({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})+2h_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\ 2h_{3}({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})+2h_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},

\displaystyle 2h_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\ 2(h_{2}+h_{3})({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}),\ 2h_{2}({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}),\ 2h_{3}({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}).

In contrast to the single-dimensional examples described above, this example requires us to concatenate an average of many box norms involving linear combinations of
two
principal directions
{\mathbf{e}}_{2},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
into a single box norm involving these two principal directions.
More generally, in the study of multidimensional polynomial progressions

(6)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}},\;{\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z),\;\ldots,\;{\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}(z)

for some choice of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
, one is confronted with the task of concatenating averages of box norms along

(7)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{1}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}),\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}),

for some
multilinear
polynomials
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}]
that arise from the application of PET to (
6
); the goal is to obtain control by a single box norm, which usually involves several principal directions.

Let us compare the directions in (
1
) with the directions in (
5
). The polynomial directions in (
1
), as examined by Peluse and Prendiville, are all multiples of the same principal direction in
{\mathbb{Z}}
. The polynomial directions in (
6
), by contrast, are linear combinations of the two principal directions
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1},{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
that come from the leading coefficients of the polynomials in (
3
) and their pairwise differences. Thus the problem of quantitative concatenation of box norms gains a layer of difficulty when one passes from the
1
-dimensional setting of Peluse and Prendiville to the multidimensional setting.

The goal of this paper is to prove a new quantitative concatenation result that is robust enough to handle the averages of polynomial box norms output by the PET argument for multidimensional polynomial progressions. The second author
[
22
]
recently obtained a similar result in the finite-field setting and used it to give bounds for subsets of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{D}
avoiding (multidimensional) polynomial progressions. Our work can be understood as an extension of his methods to the integer setting, which presents substantial new difficulties coming from the poor equidistribution behavior of polynomials over the integers. The main output of our work is the following box-norm-control for general multidimensional polynomial progressions. See (
20
) below for our definition of box norms.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
d,\ell,D\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
T>0
. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{d,\ell}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{d,\ell,D,T}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\delta>0
satisfy
N\geq C\delta^{-C}
, and let
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
be polynomials of the form
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}z^{i}
with coefficients of size at most
T
. Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

(8)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}}\E_{z\in[N^{1/d}]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}(z))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

(9)

\displaystyle\left\|f_{j}\right\|_{\{({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{jj^{\prime}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}d_{jj^{\prime}}})\cdot[\pm N^{d_{jj^{\prime}}/d}]:\;j^{\prime}\in[0,\ell],\;j^{\prime}\neq j\}^{t}}^{2^{\ell t}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D}

for all
j\in[0,\ell]
, where
d_{jj^{\prime}}=\deg({\mathbf{P}}_{j}-{\mathbf{P}}_{j^{\prime}})
and
{\mathbf{P}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
.

Informally, Theorem
1.1
asserts that every multidimensional polynomial progression can be controlled, with polynomial losses, by a single box norm of bounded degree along the “correct” boxes. Let us explain in words why the box norm in (
9
) is what one “should” expect, and specifically why it involves intervals of length
\asymp N^{d_{jj^{\prime}}/d}
in the directions
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{jj^{\prime}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}d_{jj^{\prime}}}
.
Consider the special case where
all of the
f_{i}
’s except for
f_{j},f_{j^{\prime}}
are the constant function
1
.
5
5

5

Obviously, such functions do not satisfy the assumption of being supported on
[N]^{D}
, but this example can be modified slightly to fit the assumptions of the more general Theorem
1.6
below.
Shifting
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{P}}_{j^{\prime}}(z)
, we find that (
8
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}}\E_{z\in[N^{1/d}]}f_{j^{\prime}}({\mathbf{x}})f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{j}(z)-{\mathbf{P}}_{j^{\prime}}(z))\geq\delta N^{D}.

The polynomial
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}-{\mathbf{P}}_{j^{\prime}}
has degree
d_{jj^{\prime}}
and leading coefficient
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{jj^{\prime}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}d_{jj^{\prime}}}
.
Applying the classical van der Corput inequality
d_{jj^{\prime}}
times removes the lower-order terms of
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}-{\mathbf{P}}_{j^{\prime}}

and (more or less) results in the inequality

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}}\E_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{d_{jj^{\prime}}}\in[N^{1/d}]}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}})f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+(d!/{(d-d_{jj^{\prime}})!})({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{jj^{\prime}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}d_{jj^{\prime}}})h_{1}\cdots h_{d_{jj^{\prime}}})\\
\gg_{d_{jj^{\prime}}}\delta^{O_{d_{jj^{\prime}}}(1)}N^{D}.

One can use iterated applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to replace the monomial
h_{1}\cdots h_{d_{jj^{\prime}}}
with a single variable ranging over
[N^{d_{jj^{\prime}}/d}]
, and this morally gives the largeness of a box norm of
f_{j}
along the progression
({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{jj^{\prime}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}d_{jj^{\prime}}})\cdot[\pm N^{d_{jj^{\prime}}/d}]
. The large multiplicity
t
appearing in Theorem
1.1
is an artifact of the large number of applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in both the PET induction scheme and the concatenation arguments.

For
D>1
, the results of Theorem
1.1
and the more general Theorem
1.6
below are completely novel (in the integer setting). For
D=1
, they recover Peluse’s concatenation result
[
29
, Theorem 6.1]
and also address some configurations that are not covered by Peluse’s work.

Example 1.2
.

As an example of a 1-dimensional configuration not covered by
[
29
, Theorem 6.1]
, take
x,\;x+z^{2},\;x+2z^{2},\;x+2z^{2}+z
. If

\displaystyle\Bigl|\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E_{z\in[N^{1/2}]}f_{0}(x)f_{1}(x+z^{2})f_{2}(x+2z^{2})f_{3}(x+2z^{2}+z)\Bigr|\geq\delta N,

then Theorem
1.1
(combined with standard properties of box norms listed in Lemma
3.5
) gives a positive integer
t=O(1)
for which

\displaystyle\left\|f_{0}\right\|_{U^{t}([\pm N])}^{2^{t}},\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{U^{t}([\pm N])}^{2^{t}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{U^{t}([\pm N^{1/2}])}^{2^{t}},\left\|f_{3}\right\|_{U^{t}([\pm N^{1/2}])}^{2^{t}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N.

The disparity in the scales of these Gowers norms comes from the polynomials
2z^{2},\;2z^{2}+z
having the same leading coefficient: Since the difference of these polynomials is a linear polynomial that ranges over an interval of length
\asymp N^{1/2}
rather than
\asymp N
, the shifting parameters in the Gowers norms controlling
f_{2}
and
f_{3}
must be at the smaller scale
N^{1/2}
. For configurations like this in which several polynomials have the same leading coefficients,
[
29
, Theorem 6.1]
does not give any nontrivial box-norm-control, so our result is novel in this case. We remark as a side note that such restricted Gowers norms are often difficult to use in applications because the inverse theorem for the
U^{t}([\pm N^{1/2}])
-norm involves correlations with
(t-1)
-step nilsequences on short intervals of length
\asymp N^{1/2}
, with no discernible global (i.e., scale-
N
) structure.

When the polynomials
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
have distinct degrees, the conclusion of Theorem
1.1
radically simplifies.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
d,\ell,D\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
T>0
. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{d,\ell}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{d,\ell,D,T}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\delta>0
satisfy
N\geq C\delta^{-C}
, let
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{D}}
be nonzero vectors of size at most
T
, and let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[z]
be polynomials with coefficients of size at most
T
and degrees

\displaystyle 1\leq\deg P_{1}<\cdots<\deg P_{\ell}=d.

Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}}\E_{z\in[N^{1/d}]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{1}P_{1}(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}P_{\ell}(z))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f_{\ell}\right\|_{U^{t}({\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}\cdot[\pm N])}^{2^{t}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D}.

Example 1.4
.

In particular, Corollary
1.3
recovers the Gowers-norm-control obtained by Peluse and Prendiville in
[
31
]
: It shows that if

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E_{z\in[N^{1/2}]}f_{0}(x)f_{1}(x+z)f_{2}(x+z^{2})\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N,

then
\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{U^{t}([\pm N])}^{2^{t}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N
for some positive integer
t=O(1)
.

Example 1.5
.

Corollary
1.3
also recovers the Gowers-norm-control obtained by Peluse, Prendiville and Shao in
[
32
]
: It shows that if

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E_{z\in[N^{1/2}]}f_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})f_{1}(x_{1}+z,x_{2})f_{2}(x_{1},x_{2}+z^{2})\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N,

then
\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{U^{t}({\mathbf{e}}_{2}\cdot[\pm N])}^{2^{t}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{2}
for some positive integer
t=O(1)
.

It is often desirable for technical reasons to consider polynomial progressions with “
W
-tricked” polynomials whose coefficients grow (in a controlled way) with the parameter
N
. We in fact prove the following more general version of Theorem
1.1
which is flexible enough to accommodate
W
-tricks.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
d,\ell,D\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
T>0
. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{d,\ell}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{d,\ell,D,T}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
K,N,V\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\delta>0
satisfy

C\delta^{-C}\leq K\leq T(N/V)^{1/d},

and let
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
be polynomials of the form
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}z^{i}
with coefficients at most
V
.
6
6

6

In the sense that for every
j,i
, each coordinate of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}
is at most
V
in absolute value, or equivalently that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}\in[\pm V]^{D}
.

Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}(z))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

(10)

\displaystyle\left\|f_{j}\right\|_{\{({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{jd_{jj^{\prime}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{j^{\prime}d_{jj^{\prime}}})\cdot[\pm K^{d_{jj^{\prime}}}]:\;j^{\prime}\in[0,\ell],\;j^{\prime}\neq j\}^{t}}^{2^{\ell t}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D}

for all
j\in[0,\ell]
, where
d_{jj^{\prime}}=\deg({\mathbf{P}}_{j}-{\mathbf{P}}_{j^{\prime}})
and
{\mathbf{P}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
.

When
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}
are all
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
-multiples of a single integer polynomial, Theorem
1.6
reduces to the following.

Corollary 1.7
.

Let
d,\ell,D\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
T>0
. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{d,\ell}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{d,\ell,D,T}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
{\mathbf{v}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{{D}}
be nonzero vectors of size at most
T
(with
{\mathbf{v}}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
), and let
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[z]
be a degree-
d
polynomial with leading coefficient
\beta_{d}
.
Then for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}}\E_{z\in[(N/\beta_{d})^{1/d}]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{1}P(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{v}}_{\ell}P(z))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f_{j}\right\|_{\{\beta_{d}({\mathbf{v}}_{j}-{\mathbf{v}}_{j^{\prime}})\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}]:\;j^{\prime}\in[0,\ell],\;j^{\prime}\neq j\}^{t}}^{2^{\ell t}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D}

for all
j\in[0,\ell]
.

Example 1.8
.

If
D=1
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{j}=j
, Corollary
1.7
tells us that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E_{z\in[(N/\beta_{d})^{1/d}]}f_{0}(x)f_{1}(x+P(z))\cdots f_{\ell}(x+\ell P(z))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N

implies that

\displaystyle\min_{j\in[0,\ell]}\left\|f_{j}\right\|^{2^{t}}_{U^{t}(\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}])}\gg_{d,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}N

for some positive integer
t=O_{d,\ell}(1)
, where we recall that
\beta_{d}
is the leading coefficient of
P
. This improves on the Gowers-norm-control obtained by Prendiville in
[
34
]
and recovers the global Gowers-norm-control used by Peluse, Sah and Sawhney in
[
33
]
.

Example 1.9
.

If
\ell=D=2
and
{\mathbf{v}}_{j}={\mathbf{e}}_{j}
, Corollary
1.7
tells us that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{x_{1},x_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\E_{z\in[(N/\beta_{d})^{1/d}]}f_{0}(x_{1},x_{2})f_{1}(x_{1}+P(z),x_{2})f_{2}(x_{1},x_{2}+P(z))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{2}

implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f_{0}\right\|_{({\mathbf{e}}_{1}\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}])^{t},({\mathbf{e}}_{2}\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}])^{t}}^{2t}

\displaystyle\gg_{d}\delta^{O_{d}(1)}N^{2}

\displaystyle\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{({\mathbf{e}}_{1}\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}])^{t},(({\mathbf{e}}_{1}-{\mathbf{e}}_{2})\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}])^{t}}^{2t}

\displaystyle\gg_{d}\delta^{O_{d}(1)}N^{2}

\displaystyle\left\|f_{2}\right\|_{({\mathbf{e}}_{2}\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}])^{t},(({\mathbf{e}}_{2}-{\mathbf{e}}_{1})\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}])^{t}}^{2t}

\displaystyle\gg_{d}\delta^{O_{d}(1)}N^{2}

for some positive integer
t=O(1)
. This result serves as the starting point for our companion paper
[
20
]
.

Our new concatenation result (the key ingredient in the proof of Theorem
1.6
) is the following.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
d,r,s,D\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
d\leq r
. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{r,s}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{r,s,D}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
H,M,N,V\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\delta>0
satisfy

\displaystyle C^{-1}\delta^{-C}\leq H\leq M\quad\textrm{and}\quad H^{d}MV\leq N,

and let
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}]
be multilinear polynomials
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum\limits_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}
of degree at most
d
and with coefficients at most
V
. Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M],\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{t},\ldots,E_{s}^{t}}^{2^{st}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D},

where

\displaystyle E_{j}=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j{\underline{u}}}\cdot[\pm H^{|{\underline{u}}|+1}].

1.1.
Further applications

The original motivation for this work was obtaining quantitative bounds for sets avoiding polynomial corners (see the companion paper
[
20
]
). In the time since this paper appeared as a preprint, however, it has already found rather unexpected applications to other problems in number theory, ergodic theory, and harmonic analysis. First, Green and Sawhney
[
14
]
used our concatenation results to obtain an asymptotic for the number of pairs of primes
(p,q)
such that
p^{2}+nq^{2}
is also prime (for any fixed
n\equiv 0,4\pmod{6}
). In the course of their work, they extended our arguments from Section
6
to a variant of box norms defined with general probability measures (instead of multisets) on
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
. Second, Donoso, Koutsogiannis, Sun, Tsinas, and the second author
[
8
,
9
]
adapted our concatenation argument to an ergodic-theoretic setting and used it to obtain new seminorm estimates, limiting formulas, and joint ergodicity and recurrence results for multiple ergodic averages of commuting transformations along Hardy sequences of polynomial growth. Lastly, Kosz, Mirek, Peluse, and Wright
[
19
]
applied our result in their work on establishing pointwise almost-everywhere convergence of multiple ergodic averages along distinct-degree polynomials.

For the initial stages of the project, BK was supported by the ELIDEK grant No: 1684. JL is suppored by the NSF Graduate Research Fellowship Grant No. DGE-2034835.

2.
Proof strategy

Because the proofs of our main results are rather technical, we will begin with an informal discussion of how our techniques apply to a few simplified examples.

The proof of Theorem
1.6
begins with PET induction, a complexity-reduction argument that allows us to control the counting operator of a polynomial progression by an average of box norms. While PET arguments are now standard and have been carried out in many places (see, e.g.,
[
1
,
3
,
7
,
29
,
34
]
), we need to keep careful track of the structure of the polynomial differencing parameters
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}
that are output by PET. In general, the input of PET is the counting operator lower bound

\displaystyle{\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}}\E_{z\in[N^{1/d}]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}(z))}\geq\delta N^{D},

for some polynomials
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
of degree at most
d
and some
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}
supported on
[N]^{D}
. The output of PET is then that the average of box norms

(11)

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm N^{1/d}]^{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{{{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm N^{1/d}]},\ldots,{{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm N^{1/d}]}}^{2^{s}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{D}

is also large. (Here and elsewhere, we allow all
O(1)
terms to depend on
d,\ell
.) Two crucial properties of this output are that the polynomials
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}]
appearing above are multilinear and that their coefficients are
O(1)
-multiples of the leading coefficients of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}
and of their pairwise differences. This PET argument is carried out in detail in Section
4
.

Sections
5
–
8
are then devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.10
, our main concatenation result, which we will focus on in the remainder of this sketch. To differentiate between the ranges of the various parameters, let us rewrite (
11
) as

(12)

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{{{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]},\ldots,{{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]}}^{2^{s}}\gg\delta N^{D},

where we have set
H:=M:=N^{1/d}
and replaced
\delta^{O(1)}
with
\delta
. (It will later be convenient to be able to consider slightly different values of
H,M
.)

We begin with the very simplest example, namely, where
d=2
,
D=s=r=1
, and
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}(h)=h
, so that our hypothesis is

\E_{h\in[\pm H]}\left\|f\right\|^{2}_{h\cdot[\pm M]}\gg\delta N.

Ignoring smoothing terms, we can expand this as

\E_{h\in[\pm H]}\E_{m\in[\pm M]}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}f(x)\overline{f(x+hm)}.

If the quantity
hm
ranged over
[\pm HM]
uniformly as
h,m
ranged, then we could replace
hm
by a new variable
t
and conclude that
\left\|f\right\|_{[\pm HM]}^{2}\gg\delta N
. This is of course not the case, but our goal will be to reach a similar situation. Exchanging the order of summation and applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality twice, we obtain

\E_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}\in[\pm H]}\E_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4}\in[\pm M]}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f(x)\overline{f(x+h_{1}m_{1}+h_{2}m_{2}+h_{3}m_{3}+h_{4}m_{4})}\gg\delta^{4}N.

An elementary number-theoretic argument (see Corollary
7.2
below) tells us that the quantity
h_{1}m_{1}+h_{2}m_{2}+h_{3}m_{3}+h_{4}m_{4}
assumes each value
t\in[\pm 4HM]
at most
O(H^{3}M^{3})
times as
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4}
range; in other words, this quantity is nearly uniformly distributed, in the sense of being very anti-concentrated. The idea behind the lemma is that for most fixed choices of the
h_{i}
’s, the expression is well-distributed as the
m_{i}
’s range; more precisely, this is the case unless
\gcd(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
is big or all of the
h_{i}
’s are small, and there are very few such “bad” choices.
Together with the triangle inequality, this fact implies (after some work) that

\E_{t\in[\pm 4HM]}\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f(x)\overline{f(x+t)}\mathclose{}\right|\gg\delta^{O(1)}N.

Using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to remove the absolute value gives

\E_{t\in[\pm 4HM]}\sum_{x,s\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f(x)\overline{f(x+t)}\overline{f(x+s)}f(x+s+t)\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{2}.

Since
f
is supported on
[N]
, we can restrict the
s
-variable to
[-N,N]
(say), and renormalizing gives

\left\|f\right\|^{4}_{[\pm 4HM],[\pm N]}=\E_{t\in[\pm 4HM]}\E_{s\in[\pm N]}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}}f(x)\overline{f(x+t)}\overline{f(x+s)}f(x+s+t)\gg\delta^{O(1)}N.

So we have successfully concatenated the differencing sets
[\pm H],[\pm M]
into a single differencing set
[\pm 4HM]
, at the cost of introducing an additional differencing over
[\pm N]
. Using monotonicity properties of box norms (see Lemma
3.5
below), we can “trim”
[\pm 4HM],[\pm N]
and replace each of them with a copy of
[\pm HM]
.

For our next example, take
d=3
,
r=2
,
D=s=1
, and
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}(h_{1},h_{2})=h_{1}h_{2}
, so that our hypothesis is

\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\left\|f\right\|^{2}_{h_{1}h_{2}\cdot[\pm M]}\gg\delta N.

Expanding the left-hand side and using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to duplicate the differencing parameters, as in the first example, gives

\E_{h_{11},\ldots h_{14}\in[\pm H]}\E_{h_{21},\ldots h_{24}\in[\pm H]}\E_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{4}\in[\pm M]}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}\\
f(x)\overline{f(x+h_{11}h_{21}m_{1}+h_{12}h_{22}m_{2}+h_{13}h_{23}m_{3}+h_{14}h_{24}m_{1})}\gg\delta^{4}N.

If the quantity
h_{11}h_{21}m_{1}+h_{12}h_{22}m_{2}+h_{13}h_{23}m_{3}+h_{14}h_{24}m_{1}
were nearly uniformly distributed on its range
[\pm H^{2}M]
, then we could replace it by a single new variable ranging over
[\pm H^{2}M]
and conclude as in the first example. But this quantity fails to be sufficiently anti-concentrated, essentially because it is too often divisible by squares of small primes. The solution to this problem is to apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality several more times in order to replace each term
h_{2j}m_{j}
with the expression

h_{2j1}m_{j1}+h_{2j2}m_{j2}+h_{2j3}m_{j3}+h_{2j4}m_{j4}

(at the cost of degrading the power of
\delta
, which we do not care about). As the variables range, this expression is nearly uniformly distributed on
[\pm 4HM]
(as in the previous example), and so with an acceptably small loss we can replace it by a new variable
t_{j}
that ranges over
[\pm 4HM]
. In turn, the expression

h_{11}t_{1}+h_{12}t_{2}+h_{13}t_{3}+h_{14}t_{4}

is nearly uniformly distributed on
[\pm 16H^{2}M]
, and we conclude as in the first example.

This strategy of constructing large “expanding” polynomials with nested equidistribution properties suffices to handle the general case where
s=1
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}
is a monomial. When
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}
is not a monomial, we must show that several multilinear expressions are simultaneously nearly uniformly distributed. For example, with
d=3
,
r=D=2
,
s=1
, and
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}(h_{1},h_{2})=h_{1}h_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}+h_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{2}=(h_{1}h_{2},h_{1})
, our hypothesis

\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\left\|f\right\|^{2}_{(h_{1}h_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}+h_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{2})\cdot[\pm M]}\gg\delta N

leads to the bound

(13)

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{i}\in[\pm H]:\\
i\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{ij}\in[\pm H]:\\
i,j\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{ij}\in[\pm M]:\\
i,j\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\\
f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f\Bigl({\mathbf{x}}+\Bigl(\sum_{i\in[4]}h_{i}\sum_{j\in[4]}h_{ij}m_{ij}\Bigr){\mathbf{e}}_{1}+\Bigl(\sum_{i\in[4]}h_{i}\sum_{j\in[4]}m_{ij}\Bigr){\mathbf{e}}_{2}\Bigr)}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{2}

after the usual Cauchy–Schwarz maneuvers, so we wish to show that the
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
- and
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
-coefficients are simultaneously equidistributed. The first step is showing that for each
i\in[4]
, the expressions

\sum_{j\in[4]}h_{ij}m_{ij},\quad\sum_{j\in[4]}m_{ij}

are simultaneously equidistributed. Dropping the
i
’s from the indices, we need to show that for each pair
(n_{0},n_{1})\in[\pm 4M]\times[\pm 4HM]
, the system of equations

m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}+m_{4}=n_{0},\quad h_{1}m_{1}+h_{2}m_{2}+h_{3}m_{3}+h_{4}m_{4}=n_{1}

has at most
O(H^{3}M^{2})
solutions. (Here
H^{3}M^{2}=H^{4}M^{4}/(HM\cdot M)
.) The new idea here is that we can solve for
m_{1}
in the second equation and make a substitution into the first equation, which (after regrouping) yields

(h_{2}-h_{1})m_{2}+(h_{3}-h_{1})m_{3}+(h_{4}-h_{1})m_{4}=n_{1}-h_{1}n_{0}.

Our previous arguments imply that for each of the
O(H)
choices for
h_{1}
, this equation has at most
O(H^{2}M^{2})
solutions
(h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},m_{2},m_{4},m_{4})
. Such a solution can be extended to at most one choice of
m_{1}
(sometimes there are no choices for
m_{1}
because we constrained it to lie in
[\pm M]
), so in total we have at most
O(H^{3}M^{2})
solutions, as desired.

Using the first step, we replace
\sum_{j\in[4]}h_{ij}m_{ij},\sum_{j\in[4]}m_{ij}
with new variables
t_{i},t^{\prime}_{i}
ranging over
[\pm 4HM],[\pm 4M]
, respectively, and it remains to show that the expressions

\sum_{i\in[4]}h_{i}t_{i},\quad\sum_{i\in[4]}h_{i}t^{\prime}_{i}

are simultaneously equidistributed. These expressions are individually identical to the expressions that we discussed above, but they are coupled in the sense that the variables
h_{i}
appear in both expressions. It turns out that the few “bad” choices of
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}
are now too costly because they lead to concentration in both expressions. The solution to this problem is to eliminate the bad choices before counting solutions: We can throw out the bad tuples
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
in (
13
) at the cost of degrading the implicit constant in the inequality.

To apply this type of multiscale analysis to multilinear expressions whose degrees differ by more than
1
, we introduce dummy expressions at the intermediate degrees and then run the above argument for pairs of expressions with consecutive degrees. The genericity assumption on the
h
’s is an essential technical element. The details, which are quite delicate, are carried out in Section
7
.

Further difficulties arise for
s>1
. In the
s=1
case, we iteratively used the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to double certain variables. When
s>1
, this maneuver also increases the number of multilinear expressions that must be simultaneously equidistributed—which in turn potentially requires further applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to replace these new expressions by ones with good equidistribution properties. Using arguments originally developed by the second author
[
22
]
for concatenation in the finite-field setting, we show in Section
6
that this process can be satisfactorily controlled. This part of our proof uses no information about the “polynomial” nature of our box-norm parameters, and it ends up being convenient to carry out the argument in the more general setting of averages of box norms

(14)

\displaystyle\E_{i\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}}^{2^{s}},

where
H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}
for
i\in I
are arbitrary finite multisets of a countable abelian group
G
that are contained in a bounded dilate of the support of
f
. We also believe that this level of generality might be useful for future extensions of our concatenation argument to other settings.

The key idea for handling (
14
), elucidated in Lemma
6.1
, is to use the Cauchy–Schwarz and Gowers–Cauchy–Schwarz inequalities in order to control (
14
) by

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1i_{0}}-H_{1i_{1}},H_{2i_{0}},H_{2i_{1}}\ldots,H_{si_{0}},H_{si_{1}}}^{2^{2s-1}};

we see that the multiset
H_{1i}
got replaced by a potentially much better distributed multiset
H_{1i_{0}}-H_{1i_{1}}
at the cost of doubling the number of remaining multisets. Iterating this procedure many times, we can control (
14
) by an analogous expression in which each multiset
H_{ji}
is replaced by many iterated multisets of the form
H_{ji_{1}}+\cdots+H_{ji_{\ell}}
. In the first example discussed above, this process corresponds to replacing
hm
by
h_{1}m_{1}+h_{2}m_{2}+h_{3}m_{3}+h_{4}m_{4}
, and we have already established that the latter expression enjoys better equidistribution properties.

The last step of our argument, described in Section
8
, specializes this general concatenation result to our “polynomial” setting and shows how it can be massaged to lead to the types of systems of multilinear equations described above. The results from Section
6
allow us to replace the boxes
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]
in (
12
) by iterated sums

(15)

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}=1}^{\ell}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1i_{1}}\cdots h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}})\cdot[\pm M].

Each such box generates a system of multilinear equations, corresponding to the monomials in the polynomial
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}
. The arguments from Section
7
then give that if
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum\limits_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}
, then (
15
) is uniformly distributed in the box
\sum\limits_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j{\underline{u}}}\cdot[\pm H^{|{\underline{u}}|+1}]
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.10
.

In Section
9
, we combine the conclusions of Theorem
1.10
with the output of PET to prove Theorem
1.6
. In doing so, we use the important observation from PET that the coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}
are
O(1)
-multiples of the leading coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}
and their pairwise differences.

Figure
1
provides a visual overview of the main steps of our argument.

2.1
)

8.1
)

Equidistribution for multilinear systems (Proposition
7.5
)

1.10
)

4.9
)

1.6
)

1.6
.

2.1.
Comparison with previous approaches

Our argument deviates in several ways from previous concatenation arguments in the literature: the quantitative concatenation arguments of Peluse
[
29
]
and Peluse and Prendiville
[
31
]
for
1
-dimensional polynomial progressions over
{\mathbb{Z}}
(see also Prendiville’ exposition
[
35
]
); the second author’s quantitative concatenation arguments for multidimensional polynomial progressions over finite fields
[
22
]
; and the original qualitative argument of Tao and Ziegler
[
41
]
, together with its later development in the context of ergodic theory (e.g.,
[
7
]
).

One difference between our argument and the approach of Peluse and Prendiville
[
29
,
31
,
35
]
lies in the order of carrying out two major maneuvers, namely, duplicating variables using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and replacing a multilinear polynomial like
h_{1}m_{1}+h_{2}m_{2}+h_{3}m_{3}+h_{4}m_{4}
by a new variable using equidistribution properties. As described in the sketch above, our argument clearly separates these two operations: First, we apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality many times to convert the original average (
12
) into one where each arithmetic progression
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]
can be replaced by a generalized arithmetic progression
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}}_{1})\cdot[\pm M]+\cdots+{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}}_{\ell})\cdot[\pm M]
(interpreted as a multiset). Only then do we show that the massive system of multilinear forms arising out of this procedure is equidistributed in its range. In
[
29
,
31
,
35
]
, these two steps are intertwined: Applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality are followed by equidistribution results for intermediate systems of bilinear forms, then more applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and so on. While the approach of Peluse and Prendiville has the advantage of using “simpler” equidistribution inputs (only systems of bilinear forms appear), our approach has the advantages of clearly separating the aforementioned two steps. The first step outlined above culminates in a quantitative concatenation result for averages of arbitrary box norms in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
which more closely resembles the “Bessel inequalities” of Tao and Ziegler
[
41
]
(see also its iterated version in
[
7
, Corollary 3.7]
). Say that a multiset is
symmetric
if the multiplicity of
{\mathbf{x}}
equals the multiplicity of
-{\mathbf{x}}
for all
{\mathbf{x}}
.

Corollary 2.1

(Iterated concatenation of box norms)
.

Let
A,D,s,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
\ell
be a power of 2. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{\ell,s}(1)
and positive reals
C=O_{\ell,s,D}(1)
,
C^{\prime}=O_{\ell,s}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
I
be a finite indexing set, and let
H_{ji}\subseteq[\pm AN]^{D}
be a symmetric
multiset for each
i\in I
and
j\in[s]
. Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

\displaystyle\E_{i\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

(16)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t}\in I\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\{H_{jk_{i_{1}}}+\cdots+H_{jk_{i_{\ell}}}:\ j\in[s],\ 1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{\ell}\leq t\}}^{2^{d}}\geq\frac{1}{C}A^{-C^{\prime}D}\delta^{C}N^{D},

where
d
is the degree of the box norms on the left-hand side.

Another important point of difference is the choice of induction scheme. In
[
29
]
, Peluse begins the induction with degree-
2
box norms as the base case (see
[
29
, Lemma 5.5]
), and she treats degree-
1
box norms separately (
[
29
, Lemma 5.4]
). The strategy in
[
31
,
35
]
is organized the same way.
Here, in order to concatenate averages of degree-
2
box norms, Peluse and Prendiville apply an inverse theorem for
2
-dimensional box norms and then remove the resulting structured functions using their periodicity properties. For us, degree-
1
box norms serve as the base case, which we analyze using a simple application of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. Consequently, our approach is more elementary and requires neither inverse theory nor periodicity properties. This strategy first appeared in the aforementioned work of the second author
[
22
]
over finite fields.

Furthermore, dealing with multidimensional progressions forces us to keep careful track—more so than in
[
29
]
—of how polynomial coefficients change during the PET procedure. Compared with the approach of
[
29
]
, our coefficient-tracking scheme
7
7

7

Our scheme is strongly inspired by a similar scheme from
[
7
]
, but it improves on the latter in that we are able to identify fewer (and more relevant) properties that need to be tracked throughout PET.
records more information about lower-degree terms of polynomials. It is precisely this extra information that allows Theorems
1.1
and
1.6
to extend Peluse’s result even in the single-dimensional setting (see Example
1.2
).

Lastly, whereas Peluse and Prendiville use basic Fourier analysis on several occasions, our argument is entirely carried out on the physical side. In particular, we do not use Fourier analysis in replacing equidistributed expressions like
h_{1}m_{1}+h_{2}m_{2}+h_{3}m_{3}+h_{4}m_{4}
by a new variable.

The relationship between our argument and the recent quantitative concatenation result over the finite fields by the second author
[
22
]
is more complex. As already mentioned, our concatenation argument consists of two big steps: handling averages of box norms (
14
) along arbitrary multisets by means of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and establishing equidistribution results for systems of multilinear equations. The first of these steps is largely based on an analogous argument from
[
22
]
, and its adaptation to the integer setting requires only minor technical modifications. The two approaches deviate in the second step, in which we replace each monomial
h_{1}\cdots h_{r}m
by a new variable. This task is much simpler in the finite-field setting because the monomial expression in question is already very uniformly distributed; the integer setting requires several new ideas.

The differences between quantitative concatenation arguments (whether ours or those from
[
22
,
28
,
30
]
) and the results of Tao and Ziegler
[
41
]
run more deeply. Our main goal is to obtain an effective inequality between two averages of box norms. By contrast, Tao and Ziegler’s main result compares different algebras of structured functions naturally related to various box norms. Specifically, they show that the intersection of the algebras of dual functions related to two different box norms can be embedded into a single algebra of dual functions arising from some (higher-degree) box norm. This leads to a (completely ineffective) “comparison” result between averages of box norms. Tao and Ziegler make ample use of the Gowers–Cauchy–Schwarz inequality but also employ infinitary arguments that are hard to translate into the quantitative, finitary world.
Thus, even though they obtain better control on the degree of the “concatenated” box norms, their conclusion is purely qualitative and hence unsuitable for quantitative applications.

3.
Definitions and preliminaries

3.1.
Basic notation

We write
{\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{N}}_{0},{\mathbb{Z}},{\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}
for the sets of positive integers, nonnegative integers, integers, real numbers, and complex numbers (respectively). For reals
a<b
, we set
[a,b]=\{\left\lceil a\right\rceil,a+1,\ldots,\left\lfloor b\right\rfloor\}
, abbreviating
[1,N]
as
[N]
and
[-N,N]
as
[\pm N]
.

To facilitate the reading of the paper, we fix the uses of various letters. The letter
d
denotes the maximum degree of polynomials under consideration;
D
is the dimension of the ambient space
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
in which we work;
K
is the length of the interval from which the polynomial variable
z
is taken;
\ell
is the length of the polynomial family, and later on it also denotes the number of summands in concatenated box norms;
N
refers to the size of the support of the functions;
r
is the number of parameters
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
, and
H
concerns their range;
s,t
denote the degrees of box norms appearing in the arguments; and
V
bounds the size of polynomial coefficients.

We write
{\mathbf{x}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{D})
for an element of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
(for some fixed
D\in{\mathbb{N}}
) and
x
for an element of
{\mathbb{Z}}
. When the context is clear, we often write a sum over
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
or
{\mathbb{Z}}
as
\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}
or
\sum_{x}
instead of
\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}}
or
\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

We usually write
{\underline{h}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r})
for a tuple of length
r
, and we denote its
\ell^{1}
-norm by
|{\underline{h}}|:=|h_{1}|+\cdots+|h_{r}|
. For
{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{r}
, we let
\supp({\underline{u}}):=\{i\in[r]:\;u_{i}>0\}
and get the monomial
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}:=h_{1}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{r}^{u_{r}}
. Given
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}
and
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, we also set
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{\epsilon}}:=(h_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\ldots,h_{r}^{\epsilon_{r}})
, where
h_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}:=h_{i}
if
\epsilon_{i}=0
and
h_{i}^{\epsilon_{i}}:=h_{i}^{\prime}
if
\epsilon_{i}=1
.
While there is a potential for confusion here, the meaning of the notation should be clear from the context.

We frequently work with multisets.

Definition 3.1

(Multisets)
.

For a finite multiset
X
, we write
|X|
for the total number of elements in
X
, counted with multiplicity. We use
\supp(X)
to denote the set of elements appearing in
X
with strictly positive multiplicity. With regard to containment of multisets, we write
X\subseteq Y
if the set
\supp(X)
is contained in the set
\supp(Y)
, and we denote
X\leq Y
if each element of
\supp(X)
has multiplicity at least as large in
Y
as in
X
. Lastly, for multisets
X,Y
, we define the sumset
X+Y
to be the Minkowski sum of
X,Y
with multiplicities: The multiplicity of the element
z
in
X+Y
is
|\{(x,y)\in X\times Y:\;z=x+y\}|
. Equivalently, the weight function of
X+Y
is the convolution of the weight functions of
X,Y
. Note that
|X+Y|=|X|\cdot|Y|
. The difference set
X-Y
is defined analogously.

Definition 3.2

(Indicator function)
.

We use
\mathbf{1}(E)
to denote the indicator function of an event
E
. For instance,
\mathbf{1}(a+b=c)
is the function (of
a,b,c
) that equals
1
when
a+b=c
and equals
0
when
a+b\neq c
. For a multiset
E
, we also abbreviate
\mathbf{1}(x\in\supp(E))
by
\mathbf{1}_{E}(x)
.

Let
X
be a nonempty multiset. If
X
is finite, we let
\E_{x\in X}:=\frac{1}{|X|}\sum_{x\in X}
be the average over
X
(with elements counted with multiplicity).

We call a function
f:X\to{\mathbb{C}}

1
-bounded
if
\left\|f\right\|_{\infty}:=\sup\limits_{x\in X}|f(x)|\leq 1
.

We use the following standard asymptotic notation. If
f,g:X\to{\mathbb{C}}
, with
g
taking positive real values, we denote
f=O(g)
,
f\ll g
,
g\gg f
, or
g=\Omega(f)
if there exists
C>0
such that
|f(x)|\leq Cg(x)
for all
x\in X
. If the constant
C
depends on a parameter, we record this dependence with a subscript. Lastly, we write
f\asymp g
if
f\ll g
and
f\gg g
.

We let
{\mathcal{C}}:{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
denote the complex conjugation operator
{\mathcal{C}}z=\overline{z}
.

3.2.
Van der Corput inequalities

If
G
is a countable abelian group and
E\subseteq G
is a finite and nonempty multiset, we define the
Fejér kernel

\displaystyle\mu_{E}(h):=\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in E}\mathbf{1}({h=h_{1}-h_{2}}),

and also
\mu_{E}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r})=\mu_{E}(h_{1})\cdots\mu_{E}(h_{r})
.
Summing over
h
, we easily observe that
\sum_{h}\mu_{E}(h)=1
. Moreover, if there is some
C>0
such that every element of
E
has multiplicity at most
C\frac{|E|}{|\supp(E)|}
,
then we also have the pointwise bound
0\leq\mu_{E}(h)\leq\mathbf{1}_{E-E}(h)\cdot\frac{C}{|\supp(E)|}
.
8
8

8

The lower bound is obvious; for the upper bound, write

\displaystyle\mu_{E}(h)=\mathbf{1}_{E-E}(h)\cdot\frac{1}{|E|^{2}}\sum_{h_{1}\in E}\sum_{h_{2}\in E}\mathbf{1}(h_{2}=h_{1}+h),

and notice that the sum over
h_{2}
is bounded by the maximum multiplicity of an element of
E
.

When
E=[\pm H]
, we use the shorthand notation

\displaystyle\mu_{H}(h):=\mu_{[\pm H]}(h)=\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\mathbf{1}_{h=h_{1}-h_{2}}=\frac{1}{2H+1}\mathopen{}\left(1-\frac{|h|}{2H+1}\mathclose{}\right)_{+}.

This definition differs slightly from those in
[
29
,
31
,
30
,
34
]
in that we take the ranges of
h_{1},h_{2}
to be
[\pm H]
rather than
[H]
. Making the range of
h_{1},h_{2}
symmetric around the origin will be convenient for us later.

We record several versions of the standard van der Corput inequality.
The proof of the following basic version is standard (see, e.g.,
[
34
, Lemma 3.2]
).

Lemma 3.3

(van der Corput inequality, version 1)
.

Let
f:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be
1
-bounded, and let
H,K\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then:

(i)

(Symmetric van der Corput inequality)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f(z)\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{K+2H}{K}\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[\pm H]}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{z\in([K]-h)\cap([K]-h^{\prime})}f(z+h)\overline{f(z+h^{\prime})}.

(ii)

(Asymmetric van der Corput inequality)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f(z)\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{K+2H}{K}\sum_{h}\mu_{H}(h)\frac{1}{K}\sum_{z\in[K]\cap([K]-h)}f(z)\overline{f(z+h)}.

Of course, it does not make sense to take
H\geq K
since
[K]\cap([K]-h)
is empty whenever
|h|\geq K
.

In applications, we will use the following corollary of Lemma
3.3
.

Lemma 3.4

(van der Corput inequality, version 2)
.

Let
0<\delta\leq 1
and
H,K\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfy
H\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}K
. If
f:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is a
1
-bounded function satisfying

(17)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f(z)\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta,

then

\displaystyle\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[\pm H]}\E_{z\in[K]}f(z+h)\overline{f(z+h^{\prime})}\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}

and

\displaystyle{\sum_{h}\mu_{H}(h)\E_{z\in[K]}f(z)\overline{f(z+h)}}\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}.

Proof.

We prove only the second inequality since the proof of the first is almost identical.
Since
K-|[K]\cap([K]-h)|\leq|h|\leq H\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}K
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{h}\mu_{H}(h)\sum_{z\in[K]}f(z)\overline{f(z+h)}-\sum_{h}\mu_{H}(h)\sum_{z\in[K]\cap([K]-h)}f(z)\overline{f(z+h)}\mathclose{}\right|\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}K.

It then follows Lemma
3.3
and the assumption (
17
) that

\displaystyle{\sum_{h}\mu_{H}(h)\E_{z\in[K]}f(z)\overline{f(z+h)}}\geq\frac{K}{K+2H}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f(z)\mathclose{}\right|^{2}-\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4},

as desired.
∎

3.3.
Box norms

In this section, we summarize
the theory of box norms on an arbitrary countable
9
9

9

If the group
G
were uncountable, then one would typically prefer to replace the counting measure with some more natural choice, which could raise issues of measurability. Our interest in concatenation comes from
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
, so we restrict our attention to countable groups in order not to get sidetracked by such considerations.
abelian group
G
; throughout, we write
\sum_{x}:=\sum_{x\in G}
. Given
h,h^{\prime}\in G
and a finitely supported function
f:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we define
the (multiplicative) discrete derivatives

\Delta_{h}f(x):=f(x)\overline{f(x+h)}
and
\Delta^{\prime}_{(h,h^{\prime})}f(x):=f(x+h)\overline{f(x+h^{\prime})}
. Similarly, compositions of such discrete derivatives are given by

(18)

\displaystyle\Delta_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}}f(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{h_{1}}\cdots\Delta_{h_{s}}f(x)=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f(x+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}),

(19)

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime})}f(x)

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime})}\cdots\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime})}f(x)=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f(x+(\underline{1}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{h}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}^{\prime}),

where again
{\mathcal{C}}z=\overline{z}
for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
We will later also use the notation

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s};({\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime})}:=\Delta^{\prime}_{(\alpha_{1}h_{1},\alpha_{1}h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(\alpha_{s}h_{s},\alpha_{s}h_{s}^{\prime})}

for
{\underline{h}},{\underline{h}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
and
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}\in G
.
For finite multisets
E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}\subseteq G
, we define the
box norm
of
f
along
E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}
to be

(20)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}:=\mathopen{}\left(\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime}\in E_{s}}\sum_{x}\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime})}f(x)\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s}}.

We can equivalently express this as

(21)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}=\mathopen{}\left(\sum_{x,h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}}\mu_{E_{1}}(h_{1})\cdots\mu_{E_{s}}(h_{s})\Delta_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}}f(x)\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s}}

after shifting
x\mapsto x-h^{\prime}_{1}-\cdots-h_{s}^{\prime}
and removing the extraneous averages over
h^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,h^{\prime}_{s}
. If a multiset
E
repeats
s
times, we often write
E^{s}
instead of listing it
s
times, and we also write
\left\|f\right\|_{U^{s}(E)}=\left\|f\right\|_{E^{s}}
, calling it the degree-
s

Gowers norm
of
f
along
E
.

For functions
f_{\underline{\epsilon}}
indexed by
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}
, we also define the
Gowers–Cauchy–Schwarz inner product
(or
box inner product
) along
E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}
to be

\displaystyle\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\rangle_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}

\displaystyle:=\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime}\in E_{s}}\sum_{x}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}(x+(\underline{1}-{\underline{\epsilon}})\cdot{\underline{h}}+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{x,h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}}\mu_{E_{1}}(h_{1})\cdots\mu_{E_{s}}(h_{s})\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f_{\underline{\epsilon}}(x+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}).

The classical
Gowers--Cauchy--Schwarz inequality
10
10

10

Even though this result is usually stated only for sets, exactly the same proof works for multisets.
then states that

(22)

\displaystyle\langle(f_{\underline{\epsilon}})_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\rangle_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}\leq\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}}\left\|f_{\underline{\epsilon}}\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}.

Box norms satisfy a number of well known properties, listed in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5

(Properties of box norms)
.

Let
\delta>0
, let
d,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
G
be a countable abelian group,
and let
E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}\subseteq G
be finite and nonempty multisets. Let
f:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
be 1-bounded and supported on a finite nonempty set
B
. Then the following properties hold:

(i)

(Inductive formula) For any
k\in[s]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s}}=\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{k},h_{k}^{\prime}\in E_{k}}\left\|\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{k},h_{k}^{\prime})}f\right\|_{E_{k+1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s-k}}.

(ii)

(Permutation invariance) For any permutation
\sigma:[s]\to[s]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}=\left\|f\right\|_{E_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,E_{\sigma(s)}}.

(iii)

(Monotonicity) If
|\supp(B-E_{s})|\leq C|B|
for some
C>0
, then

(23)

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s-1}}^{2^{s-1}}\geq\delta|B|\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta^{2}|B|/C.

(iv)

(Enlarging the sets) If
s\geq 2
and
E^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq G
is a finite multiset satisfying
E^{\prime}_{i}\geq E_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
, then

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}\leq\mathopen{}\left(\frac{|E_{1}^{\prime}|\cdots|E_{s}^{\prime}|}{|E_{1}|\cdots|E_{s}|}\mathclose{}\right)^{1/2^{s-1}}\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime}}.

(v)

(Trimming the dimensions of boxes)
11
11

11

Although in this and the next two properties we have taken a single parameter
d
, setting some
H_{ij}
’s to be
0
lets us apply these results to the case where different boxes
E_{i}
have different dimensions.
Let
1\leq d^{\prime}_{i}\leq d
for
i\in[s]
, and define the multisets

\displaystyle E_{i}=\sum_{j\in[d_{i}]}\beta_{ij}\cdot[\pm H_{ij}]\quad\textrm{and}\quad E_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}_{i}]}\beta_{ij}\cdot[\pm H_{ij}]

for some
\beta_{ij}\in G
and
H_{ij}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta|B|\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime}}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta^{2^{s}}|B|.

(vi)

(Trimming the lengths of boxes) Let

\displaystyle E_{i}=\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{ij}\cdot[\pm H_{ij}]\quad\textrm{and}\quad E_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{ij}\cdot[\pm\kappa_{ij}H_{ij}]

for some
\beta_{ij}\in G
,
\kappa_{ij}>0
, and
H_{ij}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta|B|\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime}}^{2^{s}}\gg_{d,s}\delta^{2^{s}}\min(1,\delta^{2^{s}}/\kappa)^{2ds}|B|

for
\kappa=\max_{i,j}\kappa_{ij}.

(vii)

(Passing to sub-arithmetic progressions)
Suppose that

\displaystyle E_{i}=\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{ij}\cdot[\pm H_{ij}]\quad\textrm{and}\quad E_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in[d]}q_{ij}\beta_{ij}\cdot[\pm H_{ij}/q_{ij}]

for some
\beta_{ij}\in G
and
q_{ij},H_{ij}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. There exists a positive real
C=O_{d,s}(1)
such that if
q_{ij}\leq C\delta^{2^{s}}H_{ij}
for all
i,j
, then

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta|B|\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime}}^{2^{s}}\gg_{d,s}\delta|B|.

In applications, we will take
G={\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
,
B=[N]^{D}
, and
E_{i},E^{\prime}_{i}
will be generalized arithmetic progressions of bounded dimension (interpreted appropriately as multisets).

Proof.

The first two properties are standard, and we omit the proofs. For the third property, we expand the definition of
\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s-1}}
and introduce an extra averaging over
E_{s}
to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s-1}}^{2^{s-1}}=\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime}\in E_{s-1}}\sum_{x}\E_{h_{s}\in E_{s}}\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime})}f(x+h_{s}).

The term
f(x+h_{1}+\cdots+h_{s})
in
\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime})}f(x+h_{s})
vanishes unless
x+h_{1}+\cdots+h_{s-1}\in B-E_{s}
, so we can introduce the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{B-E_{s}}(x+h_{1}+\cdots+h_{s-1})
into the expression above without altering its value. Applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality then gives

\displaystyle\delta^{2}|B|^{2}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s-1}}^{2^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime}\in E_{s-1}}\sum_{x}\mathbf{1}_{B-E_{s}}(x+h_{1}+\cdots+h_{s-1})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime}\in E_{s}}\sum_{x}\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s},h_{s}^{\prime})}f(x)

\displaystyle\leq C|B|\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s}},

and the claim follows.

For the fourth property, we write

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s}}=\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime}\in E_{s-1}}\sum_{x}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{h_{s}\in E_{s}}\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime})}f(x+h_{s})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}.

Using the assumption
s\geq 2
and nonnegativity, we can extend the averages over
E_{1},\ldots,E_{s-1}
to
E_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,E_{s-1}^{\prime}
and upper-bound the previous expression by

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(\frac{|E_{1}^{\prime}|\cdots|E_{s-1}^{\prime}|}{|E_{1}|\cdots|E_{s-1}|}\mathclose{}\right)^{2}\cdot\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots\E_{h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime}\in E_{s-1}^{\prime}}\sum_{x}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{h_{s}\in E_{s}}\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime})}f(x+h_{s})\mathclose{}\right|^{2}.

We can replace average over
E_{s}
with an average over
E_{s}^{\prime}
using the same maneuver.

To prove the fifth property, we start with the case
s=1
. Dropping the index
i
,
we can write

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E}^{2}=\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h^{\prime}_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d]}\end{subarray}}f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}^{\prime}\mathclose{}\right)\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d]}\end{subarray}}\overline{f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}\mathclose{}\right)}.

Using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to double the variables
(h_{j})_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}
, we bound

\left\|f\right\|_{E}^{4}\leq|B|\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d^{\prime}+1,d]}\end{subarray}}\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d^{\prime}]}\end{subarray}}f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}+1,d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}+\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}\beta_{j}h_{j}\mathclose{}\right)\\
\overline{f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}+1,d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}+\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}\beta_{j}h_{j}^{\prime}\mathclose{}\right)}.

Shifting
x\mapsto x-\sum\limits_{j\in[d^{\prime}+1,d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}
, we can ignore the contribution of
j\in[d^{\prime}+1,d]
altogether, and we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq|B|\cdot\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d^{\prime}]}\end{subarray}}f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}\beta_{j}h_{j}\mathclose{}\right)\overline{f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d^{\prime}]}\beta_{j}h_{j}^{\prime}\mathclose{}\right)}=|B|\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{E^{\prime}}^{2}.

We now consider the case
s>1
. The induction formula for box norms, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and the
s=1
case give

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime}\in E_{s-1}}\left\|\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime})}f\right\|_{E_{s}}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq|B|\cdot\E_{h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in E_{1}}\cdots\E_{h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime}\in E_{s-1}}\left\|\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{s-1},h_{s-1}^{\prime})}f\right\|_{E^{\prime}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=|B|\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s-1},E^{\prime}_{s}}^{2^{s}}.

Performing the same procedure with
E_{s-1}
in place of
E_{s}
gives

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s+2}}\leq|B|^{2}\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s-1},E^{\prime}_{s}}^{2^{s+1}}\leq|B|^{3}\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s-2},E^{\prime}_{s-1},E^{\prime}_{s}}^{2^{s}},

and continuing in this manner gives

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{4^{s}}\leq|B|^{2^{s}-1}\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{E^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,E^{\prime}_{s}}^{2^{s}},

from which the result promptly follows.

To prove the sixth property, we first set

\displaystyle E^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{ij}\cdot[\pm\varepsilon_{ij}H_{ij}]

for parameters
\varepsilon_{ij}>0
to be chosen later.
As in the proof of the fifth property, we begin with the
s=1
case and drop the index
i
. Note that for every
\varepsilon>0
and
1
-bounded function
g:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we have

(24)

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E_{h\in[\pm H]}g(h)-\E_{h\in[\pm H]}\E_{h^{\prime}\in[\pm\varepsilon H]}g(h+h^{\prime})\mathclose{}\right|\leq 2\varepsilon.

Hence we can approximate

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d]}\end{subarray}}f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}^{\prime}\mathclose{}\right)\overline{f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}\mathclose{}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d]}\end{subarray}}f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}^{\prime}\mathclose{}\right)\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h^{\prime\prime}_{j}\in[\pm\varepsilon_{j}H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d]}\end{subarray}}\overline{f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}(h_{j}+h^{\prime\prime}_{j})\mathclose{}\right)}+O_{d}(\varepsilon|B|),

where
\varepsilon:=\max_{j}\varepsilon_{j}
.
By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in all variables except
h^{\prime\prime}_{j}
, we get

\left\|f\right\|_{E}^{4}\leq|B|\cdot\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}],\\
h^{\prime}_{j},h^{\prime\prime}_{j}\in[\pm\varepsilon_{j}H_{j}]:\;{j\in[d]}\end{subarray}}f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}(h_{j}+h^{\prime}_{j})\mathclose{}\right)\\
\overline{f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}(h_{j}+h^{\prime\prime}_{j})\mathclose{}\right)}+O_{d}(\varepsilon|B|^{2}).

Shifting the variable
x\mapsto x-\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}h_{j}
gives

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq|B|\cdot\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h^{\prime}_{j},h^{\prime\prime}_{j}\in[\pm\varepsilon_{j}H_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d]}\end{subarray}}f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}h^{\prime}_{j}\mathclose{}\right)\overline{f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}h^{\prime\prime}_{j}\mathclose{}\right)}+O_{d}(\varepsilon|B|^{2})

\displaystyle=|B|\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{E^{\prime\prime}}^{2}+O_{d}(\varepsilon|B|^{2}).

Iterating this argument for higher-degree norms as in the proof of (
v
) gives

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{4^{s}}\leq|B|^{2^{s}-1}\cdot\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{\prime\prime},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime\prime}}^{2^{s}}+O_{d,s}(\varepsilon|B|^{2^{s}}),

where
\varepsilon:=\max_{i,j}\varepsilon_{ij}
, so

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1},\ldots,E_{s}}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta|B|\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{\prime\prime},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime\prime}}^{2^{s}}\geq(\delta^{2^{s}}-C_{d,s}\varepsilon)|B|

for some constant
C_{d,s}>0
.
If the
\kappa_{ij}
’s are sufficiently small, namely, if
\kappa:=\max_{i,j}\kappa_{ij}\leq\delta^{2^{s}}/(2C_{d,s})
,
then we apply the above bound directly with
\varepsilon_{ij}=\kappa_{ij}
. Otherwise, we first decrease the lengths of boxes, setting
\varepsilon_{ij}=\min(\delta^{2^{s}}/(2C_{d,s}),\kappa_{ij})
for all
i,j
,
and then use (
iv
) to enlarge the boxes from
E^{\prime\prime}_{i}
to
E^{\prime}_{i}
, which gives

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime}}^{2^{s}}\geq\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[s],\;j\in[d]\end{subarray}}\frac{\varepsilon_{ij}^{2}}{\kappa_{ij}^{2}}\mathclose{}\right)\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{\prime\prime},\ldots,E_{s}^{\prime\prime}}^{2^{s}}

\displaystyle\gg_{d,s}\delta^{2^{s}}\cdot(\delta^{2^{s}}/\kappa)^{2ds}|B|.

Combining these two cases yields the desired bound.

For the last property, we first observe that for positive integers
H,q,u
, the sets
[\pm H]
and
\bigcup\limits_{u=0}^{q-1}(u+q\cdot[\pm H/q])
differ in at most
2q
“boundary” elements. Hence, if
(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
1
-bounded sequence of complex numbers, then

\mathopen{}\left|\E_{h\in[\pm H]}x_{h}-\E_{u\in[0,q-1]}\E_{h\in[\pm H/q]}x_{qh+u}\mathclose{}\right|\\
\leq\frac{2q}{2H+1}+\mathopen{}\left|\frac{1}{2H+1}-\frac{1}{(2\left\lfloor H/q\right\rfloor+1)q}\mathclose{}\right|(2\left\lfloor H/q\right\rfloor+1)q\leq\frac{3q+1}{2H+1}.

For the proof proper of the last property, we start with the case
s=1
.
Approximating each box
\beta_{ij}\cdot[\pm H_{ij}]
by the union of boxes
\beta_{ij}\cdot(u_{j}+q_{j}\cdot[\pm H_{j}/q_{j}])
indexed by
u_{j}\in[0,q_{j}-1]
and applying the estimate above, we get

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E}^{2}

\displaystyle=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{j},u_{j}^{\prime}\in[0,q_{j}-1]:\\
j\in[d]\end{subarray}}\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{j},h^{\prime}_{j}\in[\pm H_{j}/q_{j}]:\\
{j\in[d]}\end{subarray}}f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}(q_{j}h_{j}+u_{j})\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\overline{f\mathopen{}\left(x+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}(q_{j}h_{j}^{\prime}+u_{j}^{\prime})\mathclose{}\right)}+O_{d}(\varepsilon|B|)

for
\varepsilon:=\max_{j\in[d]}\frac{q_{j}}{H_{j}}
.
We pigeonhole in
u_{j},u_{j}^{\prime}
and apply
the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to get

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E}^{4}\leq\left\|f\Bigl(\cdot+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}u_{j}\Bigr)\right\|_{E^{\prime}}^{2}\cdot\left\|f\Bigl(\cdot+\sum_{j\in[d]}\beta_{j}u_{j}^{\prime}\Bigr)\right\|_{E^{\prime}}^{2}+O_{d}(\varepsilon|B|^{2}).

The translation-invariance of box norms then gives
\left\|{f}\right\|_{E^{\prime}}^{2}\geq(\delta-O_{d}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}))|B|
. The statement for general
s
follows inductively, using the inductive formula for box norms.
∎

4.
PET induction scheme

In this section, we begin the study of the counting operators

(25)

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}(z))

associated with families
{\mathcal{P}}=({\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell})
of essentially distinct polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
(where “essentially distinct” means that all polynomials and their pairwise differences are nonconstant). For instance, in this notation the configuration

\displaystyle(x_{1},x_{2}),\;(x_{1}+z^{2}+z,x_{2}),\;(x_{1},x_{2}+z^{2}+z)

can be rewritten as
{\mathbf{x}},\;{\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{e}}_{1}(z^{2}+z),\;{\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}(z^{2}+z)
and corresponds to the family
{\mathcal{P}}=({\mathbf{e}}_{1}(z^{2}+z),{\mathbf{e}}_{2}(z^{2}+z))
.

We present here the standard PET induction scheme which allows us to bound the counting operator (
25
) by an average of box norms. These box norms can be seen as “local” in the sense that the differencing parameters lie in arithmetic progressions of length
\asymp N^{1/d}
, where
d=\max_{j}\deg{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
. This is the first step in showing that multidimensional polynomial progressions are controlled by a single “global” box norm. Combining arguments from
[
7
,
42
]
, we obtain a precise description of the directions of the box norms; in particular, we show that they come from the coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
and their differences. We start with a simple example in Section
4.1
that will motivate the argument in the general case. In Section
4.2
, we set up the formalism that we need for this purpose and derive various properties of the polynomial families that appear in the PET induction scheme. We then assemble bits and pieces from Section
4.2
in order to prove Proposition
4.9
, the main result of this section.

The material in this subsection is not novel, and various parts of it can be found in previous works on the subject (in
[
7
,
29
,
34
,
42
]
,
inter alia
).

4.1.
Example of PET

Consider the counting operator

\displaystyle\Lambda(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}):=\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}z^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}z)f_{2}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}z^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}z),

and suppose that

\displaystyle\Lambda(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2})\geq\delta N^{2}

for some
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{2}
. To avoid degenerate cases, we assume that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}\neq\textbf{0}
, and that
({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})\neq(\textbf{0},\textbf{0})
.

Our goal in this section is to work out the PET argument for this specific example before delving into it more abstractly. The goal of the PET argument is to pass from the counting operator for a polynomial progression to an average of box norms of one of the functions in the configuration (say,
f_{1}
). This is achieved in a finite number of steps, in each of which we apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (to remove terms that do not depend on
z
), the van der Corput inequality (to double the variable
z
) and a change of variables (to bring the progression into a form where some functions are independent of
z
). That this procedure ends in finite time follows from the fact that at each step, we reduce the (appropriately measured) complexity, or
type
, of the polynomial progression.

For this particular example, we will need three applications of the aforementioned procedure to reduce the progression to an average of box norms of
f_{1}
.

Step 1

We apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in
{\mathbf{x}}
; since
f_{0}
is independent of
z
and has
L^{2}
-norm at most
N
, we can obtain the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}z^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}z)f_{2}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}z^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}z)\mathclose{}\right|^{2}\geq\delta^{2}N^{2}.

For the
\Omega(\delta^{2}N^{2})
values of
{\mathbf{x}}\in[N]^{2}
for which the absolute value above is at least
\delta^{2}N^{2}/2
, we then apply the van der Corput inequality (Lemma
3.4
) for some
H\ll\delta^{8}K
; this has the effect of doubling
z
and gives

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},h_{1}}\mu_{H}(h_{1})\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}z^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}z)\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}(z+h_{1})^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}(z+h_{1}))}\\
f_{2}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}z^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}z)\overline{f_{2}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}(z+h_{1})^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}(z+h_{1}))}\gg\delta^{2}N.

We now want to bring the configuration into a form where one copy of
f_{2}
has an argument independent of
z
; then this copy of
f_{2}
can be removed at the subsequent step of the argument. Shifting
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}z^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}z
, we obtain

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},h_{1}}\mu_{H}(h_{1})\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z)\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}h_{1})}\\
f_{2}({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f_{2}({\mathbf{x}}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}z+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}h_{1})}\gg\delta^{2}N^{2}.

This new progression (or, rather, family of progressions indexed by
h_{1}
) may not look simpler than the original one, but it is: Whereas the original progression involved two quadratic
12
12

12

in
z
(the degree of a polynomial in this section always refers to the degree in
z
)
polynomials with distinct leading coefficients
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}
, the new progression involves two quadratic polynomials with the same leading coefficient
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}
(which is nonzero by assumption), as well as a linear polynomial. The decrease in the number of quadratic polynomials with distinct leading coefficients is what makes this progression “less complex” than the original one.

Step 2

Once again, we apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in
{\mathbf{x}},h_{1}
. This time, this has the effect of removing one of the two instances of
f_{2}
, giving
