\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},h_{1}}\mu_{H}(h_{1})\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z)\right.\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}h_{1})}\\
\left.\overline{f_{2}({\mathbf{x}}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}z+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}h_{1})}\vphantom{\E_{z\in[K]}}\right|^{2}\gg\delta^{4}N^{2}.

The van der Corput inequality (Lemma
3.4
) then gives

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},h_{1},h_{2}}\mu_{H}(h_{1},h_{2})\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z)\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2})^{2}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})(z+h_{2}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}h_{1})}\\
{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2})^{2}+(2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})(z+h_{2})+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}h_{1})}\\
\overline{\Delta_{2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}h_{2}}f_{2}({\mathbf{x}}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}z+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}h_{1})}\gg\delta^{4}N^{2}.

We want to make the remaining instances of
f_{2}
independent of
z
. To this end we shift
13
13

13

If
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}=\textbf{0}
, then this term can be removed before applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, and we can proceed directly to the change of variables performed in Step 3 below.

{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}z
, which gives

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},h_{1},h_{2}}\mu_{H}(h_{1},h_{2})\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z)\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2})^{2}+(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{1}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{2}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2})^{2}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{3}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
\overline{\Delta_{2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}h_{2}}f_{2}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{a}}_{4}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\gg\delta^{4}N^{2}

for some polynomials
{\mathbf{a}}_{j}
in
h_{1},h_{2}
, the exact form of which is irrelevant for us. Even though the configuration has increased in length (this is an unfortunate aspect of the PET argument), it has become less complex in the sense that all of the quadratic polynomials now have the same leading coefficient, and there is no linear polynomial left (as contrasted with the previous step, where we still had one polynomial linear in
z
). We are ready for the last step, in which we will remove
f_{2}
altogether.

Step 3

Proceeding as before, we apply the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in
{\mathbf{x}},h_{1},h_{2}
to get

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},h_{1},h_{2}}\mu_{H}(h_{1},h_{2})\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z)\right.\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2})^{2}+(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{1}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{2}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
\left.\vphantom{\E_{z\in[K]}}{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2})^{2}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{3}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\right|^{2}\gg\delta^{8}N^{2}.

An application of Lemma
3.4
then gives

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}}\mu_{H}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z)\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{3})^{2}+(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})(z+h_{3}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2})^{2}+(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{1}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2}+h_{3})^{2}+(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})(z+h_{3})+{\mathbf{a}}_{1}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{2}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{3})^{2}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})(z+h_{3})+{\mathbf{a}}_{2}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2})^{2}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{3}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(z+h_{2}+h_{3})^{2}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})(z+h_{3})+{\mathbf{a}}_{3}(h_{1},h_{2}))}\\
\gg\delta^{8}N^{2}.

Shifting

{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}-(-2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z,

we end up with

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}}\mu_{H}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{3}z+{\mathbf{a}}_{1}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{2}z+{\mathbf{a}}_{2}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}))}{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(h_{2}+h_{3})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{3}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}z+{\mathbf{a}}_{4}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}))}{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{3}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{5}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}))}\\
{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{2}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{6}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}))}\\
\overline{f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(h_{2}+h_{3})+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})z+{\mathbf{a}}_{7}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}))}\gg\delta^{8}N^{2}.

for some polynomials
{\mathbf{a}}_{j}
in
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
. The important point about this new expression is that it is an average, indexed by
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})
, of counting operators for the
8
-point linear (in
z
) configurations

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}},\;{\mathbf{x}}+2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{3}z,\;{\mathbf{x}}+2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{2}z,\;{\mathbf{x}}+2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(h_{2}+h_{3})z,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}z,\;{\mathbf{x}}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{3}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})z,\;{\mathbf{x}}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{2}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})z,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}+(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(h_{2}+h_{3})+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})z;

as such, each of these configurations can be controlled individually by a certain degree-
7
box norm as in Lemma
4.4
below, using Gowers’ original argument for controlling arithmetic progressions by Gowers norms
[
11
,
13
]
. Gowers’ argument, which we abstain from showing here, consists of
7
more applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz and van der Corput inequalities, together with changes of variables, and its output is the norm-control

\displaystyle\sum_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}}\mu_{H}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})\left\|f_{1}\right\|^{2^{7}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{3}\cdot[\pm M],\;2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{2}\cdot[\pm M],\\
2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(h_{2}+h_{3})\cdot[\pm M],\;2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}\cdot[\pm M],\\
(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{3}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})\cdot[\pm M],\\
(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{2}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})\cdot[\pm M],\\
(2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(h_{2}+h_{3})+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1})\cdot[\pm M]\end{subarray}}\gg\delta^{2^{10}}N^{2}

for any
M\ll\delta^{2^{10}}K
. The important points about this average of box norms are that the directions

\displaystyle 2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{3},\;2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{2},\;2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(h_{2}+h_{3}),

\displaystyle 2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1},\;2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{3}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1},\;2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})h_{2}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1},

\displaystyle 2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})(h_{2}+h_{3})+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h_{1}

in the average of box norms above depend only on the leading coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{2}
, and that they are linear polynomials in
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
(for a general polynomial family
{\mathcal{P}}
, the directions will be multilinear polynomials in
h_{i}
of degree one smaller than the degree of
{\mathcal{P}}
). Understanding these directions for a general polynomial family is an important (even if tedious) task, on which we embark now.

4.2.
Setting up the induction scheme

In order to carry out the PET argument in general, we first need to set up some notation that allows us to talk about general multidimensional polynomial progressions. Let
d,D,\ell,r,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
{\underline{h}}:=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r})
be a tuple of length
r
. All of the polynomial families that we shall consider will take the following form.

Definition 4.1

(Normal polynomial family)
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s})
be a family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z,{\underline{h}}]
of degree at most
d
. We call
{\mathcal{Q}}

normal
if the polynomials
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s}
are nonzero and distinct,
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}
has highest degree (with respect to the variable
z
), and
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(0,{\underline{h}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s}(0,{\underline{h}})
are the zero polynomial for all
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}
(equivalently,
z
divides all monomial terms of each
{\mathbf{Q}}_{i}(0,{\underline{h}})
).

During the PET argument, we will subject
{\mathcal{P}}
to an iterated application of the following operation.

Definition 4.2

(van der Corput operation)
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z,{\underline{h}}]
. Given
m\in[s]
, we define the new polynomial family

\displaystyle\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}:=

\displaystyle(\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m},\ldots,\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m},{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m},\ldots,{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m})^{*},

where
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{\mathbf{Q}}(z,{\underline{h}}):={\mathbf{Q}}(z+h_{r+1},{\underline{h}})-{\mathbf{Q}}(h_{r+1},{\underline{h}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{D}[z,h,h_{r+1}]

and the
∗
operation removes all zero polynomials and all but the first copy of each polynomial that appears multiple times.

We refer to the operation
{\mathcal{Q}}\mapsto\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
as a
van der Corput operation
on
{\mathcal{Q}}
since the new family
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
will arise from
{\mathcal{Q}}
via an application of the van der Corput inequality. It is through this operation that we obtain a new polynomial family from
{\mathcal{Q}}
at each step of the PET induction scheme.

We caution the reader that
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
need not be normal even if
{\mathcal{Q}}
is, since
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
need not be the highest-degree polynomial (in
z
) in
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
(the other conditions for normality are easily seen to be met). For instance, if
{\mathcal{Q}}=(z^{2},2z^{2})
, then
\partial_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}=(2hz,z^{2}+4zh,z^{2})
, and clearly the first polynomial is lower-degree in
z
than the other two. However, in ensuing applications, we will always choose the index
m
to maximize the degree of
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
among the elements of
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
.
14
14

14

This condition is explicitly assumed in Proposition
4.7
and ensured in Proposition
4.8
below.
For instance, we will never pass from the
{\mathcal{Q}}
as above to
\partial_{1}{\mathcal{Q}}
, but rather to the normal family
\partial_{2}{\mathcal{Q}}=(-z^{2}+2hz,-z^{2},4hz)
.

The following lemma shows how we can use the Cauchy–Schwarz and van der Corput inequalities, together with changes of variables, to pass from the family
{\mathcal{Q}}
to the family
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
at each step of PET.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
d,D,m,\ell,r,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z,{\underline{h}}]
of degree at most
d
. Suppose that the degree of
Q_{1}
as a polynomial in
z
exceeds
1
.
Let
\delta>0
and
H,K,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
be parameters satisfying

H\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}K
. Then for any
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
and any
{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{s}
, the lower bound

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(z,{\underline{h}}))\cdots f_{s}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}_{s}(z,{\underline{h}}))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{h_{r+1}}\mu_{H}(h_{r+1})\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{\mathbf{Q_{0}}}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot\prod_{{\mathbf{Q}}\in\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}}f_{{\mathbf{Q}}}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}))\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}N^{D}

for some
1
-bounded functions
f_{\mathbf{Q}}:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
(depending on
{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}
) with
f_{\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}}=f_{1}
.

The property
f_{\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}}=f_{1}
is crucial for letting us keep track of the function
f_{1}
over the course of the PET procedure.

In forthcoming applications, we will compare weighted averages over
{\underline{h}}
of the inequalities considered in Lemma
4.3
, hence the seemingly redundant presence of
{\underline{h}}
in the statement of the lemma.

Proof.

From the triangle inequality we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\mathopen{}\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(z,{\underline{h}}))\cdots f_{s}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}_{s}(z,{\underline{h}}))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}.

By the popularity principle, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E_{z\in[K]}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(z,{\underline{h}}))\cdots f_{s}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}_{s}(z,{\underline{h}}))\mathclose{}\right|\geq(\delta/2)N^{D}.

for at least
(\delta/2)N^{D}
values
{\mathbf{x}}\in[N]^{D}
.
Applying the asymmetric version of the van der Corput inequality from Lemma
3.4
to each such value
{\mathbf{x}}
,
we deduce, for arbitrary
H\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}K
, that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\sum_{h_{r+1}}\mu_{H}(h_{r+1})\E_{z\in[K]}\prod_{j\in[s]}\mathopen{}\left(f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}}))\overline{f_{j}}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(z+h_{r+1},{\underline{h}}))\mathclose{}\right)\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}N^{D}.

Swapping the order of summation, shifting
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}})-{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(h_{r+1},{\underline{h}})
, and swapping the order of summation again, we deduce that

(26)

\displaystyle\sum_{h_{r+1}}\mu_{H}(h_{r+1})\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}\prod_{j\in[s]}\big(f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{R}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}))\overline{f_{j}}({\mathbf{x}}+\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1})\big)\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}N^{D},

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1})

\displaystyle:={\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}})-{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(h_{r+1},{\underline{h}}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1})

\displaystyle:={\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(z+h_{r+1},{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}})-{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(h_{r+1},{\underline{h}}).

To show that (
26
) indeed takes the form stipulated by the lemma, we observe that up to subtracting terms depending only on
{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}
, each polynomial
\mathbf{R}_{j},\mathbf{R}_{j}^{\prime}
is one of

(27)

\displaystyle\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{2}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m},\ldots,\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m},{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m},\ldots,{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{s}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}

or
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
. Hence we can group all the terms in (
26
) whose polynomials equal some particular
{\mathbf{Q}}\in\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}\cup\{\mathbf{0}\}
up to terms depending only on
{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}
. Specifically, if

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{Q}}

\displaystyle:=\{j\in[s]:\;\mathbf{R}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1})-{\mathbf{Q}}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1})\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}]\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{Q}}

\displaystyle:=\{j\in[s]:\;\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1})-{\mathbf{Q}}(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1})\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}]\},

then we set

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{Q}}({\mathbf{x}}):=\prod_{j\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{Q}}}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+(\mathbf{R}_{j}-{\mathbf{Q}})(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}))\cdot\prod_{j\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{Q}}}\overline{f_{j}}({\mathbf{x}}+(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}_{j}-{\mathbf{Q}})(z,{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}));

notice that this depends on
{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}
but not
z
.
Hence the inequality takes the claimed form, with each
f_{\mathbf{Q}}
being a product of some
f_{j}
’s and their conjugates shifted by polynomials in
{\underline{h}},h_{r+1}
. We remark that this grouping of terms corresponds to applying the operation
*
when obtaining the polynomial family
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
from
{\mathcal{Q}}
.

What remains to be shown is that
f_{\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}}={f_{1}}
. To this end, we make an important observation that each polynomial in (
27
) is distinct from
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
. This can be seen on a case-by-case basis:

(i)

\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
is distinct from
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{j}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
for
j\neq 1
because
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}
is distinct from
{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}
, which is a consequence of the normality of
{\mathcal{Q}}
;

(ii)

\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
is distinct from
{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{j}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
for
j\neq 1
because the
h_{r+1}
-free parts are simply
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}
, which are distinct by the normality of
{\mathcal{Q}}
;

(iii)

\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
is distinct from
{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
because
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}
is nonlinear in
z
.
15
15

15

Note that in the linear case,
{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
and
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}
would be the same because the
\sigma
operation would remove entirely the shift by
h_{r+1}
.

Consequently, when we group terms above, we do not group any term with
f_{1}
. Hence
f_{\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}}=\overline{f_{1}}
, since
\mathbf{R}_{1}^{\prime}=\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}
.
The complex conjugate can be removed by conjugating the left-hand side of (
26
) and redefining other
f_{\mathbf{Q}}
’s accordingly.
∎

We deal with the linear case differently.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\ell,D\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
be nonzero vectors and set
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{0}:=\mathbf{0}
. Let
\delta>0
and
K,M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
be parameters satisfying
M\leq\frac{\delta^{2^{\ell}}}{16^{2^{\ell}-1}}K
. Then for any
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the lower bound

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot\prod_{j\in[\ell]}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}z)\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}\in[\pm M]^{\ell}}\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\Delta^{\prime}_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{0},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j+1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell};({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}})\geq\frac{\delta^{2^{\ell}}}{16^{2^{\ell}-1}}N^{D}

for each
j\in[\ell]
.

We remark that the result needs no bounds on the size of the vectors
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
, for if they are comparable with
N
, then the result essentially degenerates. For instance, if we consider the 1-dimensional progression
x,\;x+\frac{N}{10}z
and set
K=N
, then the constraints on the support of
f_{0},f_{1}
force the summand to vanish whenever
z\geq 10
. In that case the only permissible
\delta
’s for which the lower bound holds are themselves of size
\asymp 1/N
, and the conclusion becomes trivial.

Proof.

Since the hypothesis of the lemma is invariant under permutations of the functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{\ell}
, it suffices to consider the case
j=1
. We proceed by induction on
\ell
. The popularity principle yields at least
(\delta/2)N^{D}
values of
{\mathbf{x}}\in[N]^{D}
for which

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\E_{z\in[K]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}z)\mathclose{}\right|\geq(\delta/2)N^{D}.

The symmetric van der Corput inequality from Lemma
3.4
gives

\displaystyle\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\Delta^{\prime}_{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}m_{1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}m_{1}^{\prime})}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}z)\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}N^{D}

as long as
M\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}K
.
We then translate
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell}z
(temporarily swapping the order of
{\mathbf{x}}
and
z
for that purpose), so that

\displaystyle\E_{m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}\prod_{j\in[\ell]}\Delta^{\prime}_{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}m_{1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}m_{1}^{\prime})}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})z)\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{16}N^{D}.

This immediately gives the claimed result in the case
\ell=1
. For
\ell>1
, we apply the induction hypothesis (separately for each
({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})
) to the shorter progression

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}},\;{\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell})z,\;\ldots,\;{\mathbf{x}}+({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell-1}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell})z.

and the functions
\Delta^{\prime}_{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}m_{1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}m_{1}^{\prime})}f_{j}
in place of
f_{j}
upon observing that

({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell})-({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\ell})={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}.

The final lower bound and a threshold on
M
follow from a rather straightforward computation.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{P}}=({\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
. Our next goal is to describe the properties of
{\mathcal{P}}
that are preserved by the van der Corput operation. Let
d=\deg{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
; by normality,
\deg p_{j}\leq d
for all
j\in[\ell]
.
We denote the coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
by
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}z^{i}
, and we also set
{\mathbf{P}}_{0}:=\mathbf{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0i}:=\mathbf{0}
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z,{\underline{h}}]
of degree at most
d
, and write

(28)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}({\underline{h}})z^{i}

for some
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[{\underline{h}}]
. In other words, the
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}
’s are the coefficients of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}
as a polynomial in
z
, and they are themselves polynomials in
{\underline{h}}
. For
j,j^{\prime}\in[0,s]
, also define

d_{jj^{\prime}}:=\max\{i\in[d]:\;{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}({\underline{h}})\neq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j^{\prime}i}({\underline{h}})\}

to be the degree of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{j^{\prime}}
as a polynomial in
z
,
where we set
{\mathbf{Q}}_{0}:=\mathbf{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{0i}:=\mathbf{0}
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
We want to show that if
{\mathcal{Q}}
is a family obtained from
{\mathcal{P}}
at some stage of the PET induction scheme, then the polynomials
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}
preserve several useful properties.

Definition 4.5

(Descendent family of polynomials)
.

A normal family
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s})
of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z,{\underline{h}}]

descends from

{\mathcal{P}}
if for each
j\in[s]
, the polynomials
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}
in (
28
) can be expressed as
16
16

16

The multinomial coefficient

\displaystyle{{{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}+i}\choose{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}}}}=\frac{(u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}+i)!}{u_{1}!\cdots u_{r}!\cdot i!}

is the coefficient of
{\underline{h}}^{{\underline{u}}}z^{i}=h_{1}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{r}^{u_{r}}z^{i}
in
(z+h_{1}+\cdots+h_{r})^{i+u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}}
.

(29)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-i\end{subarray}}{{{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}+i}\choose{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}}}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+i)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+i)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}

for some indices
w_{{\underline{u}}},w_{j{\underline{u}}}\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

(Coefficients of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}
)
w_{1{\underline{u}}}=1
for all
{\underline{u}}
.

(ii)

(Multilinearity) For each
j{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\in[0,s]}}
, the polynomial
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}
(the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}
as a polynomial in
z
) is multilinear in
{\underline{h}}
.

(iii)

(Leading coefficients) For each
j{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\in[0,s]}}
and multidegree
{\underline{u}}
, the vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})}
is nonzero only if it is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
.

Example 4.6
.

Consider the polynomial family
{\mathcal{P}}=({\mathbf{P}}_{1},{\mathbf{P}}_{2})
given by

{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z)={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}z^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}z\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\mathbf{P}}_{2}(z)={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}z^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}z,

as studied in Section
4.1
. Like we did there, we assume that
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}\neq\mathbf{0}
. Also let
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{2},{\mathbf{Q}}_{3})
, where

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(z,h)=({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+(2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}h,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}(z,h)=({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22})z^{2}+({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21})z,\quad{\mathbf{Q}}_{3}(z,h)=2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}hz+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h^{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21}h.

The family
{\mathcal{Q}}
consists of polynomials from
\partial_{2}{\mathcal{P}}
after chopping off the terms independent from
z
, and it has been obtained from
{\mathcal{P}}
in the first step of the PET argument outlined in Section
4.1
. We claim that
{\mathcal{Q}}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
. Observe first that

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{11}(h)

\displaystyle=2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}h+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{12}(h)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{21}(h)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{21},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{22}(h)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{31}(h)

\displaystyle=2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}h,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{32}(h)

\displaystyle=\textbf{0},

and so
d_{10}=2
(since
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{12}\neq\mathbf{0}
),
d_{12}=1
(since
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{12}={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{22}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{11}\neq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{21}
) and
d_{13}=2
(since
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{12}\neq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{32}
).
An inspection of the coefficients of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}(h)
shows that they are indeed of the form (
29
); for instance,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{11},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{12}
equal

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{11}(h)=2({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{11}2}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{1}2})h+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{10}1}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{0}1}\quad\textrm{and}\quad{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{12}(h)={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{10}2}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{0}2}

with
w_{11}=w_{10}=1
,
w_{1}=0
,
w_{0}=2
. This in particular implies the property (
i
) from Definition
4.5
. The (multi)linearity property (
ii
) of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}
’s and their differences is immediate. Lastly, the special form (
iii
) of the coefficients is a more detailed version of the observation that the leading coefficients of the polynomials
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{12}
,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{11}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{21}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{32}
(which are themselves “leading coefficients” of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1},{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{2}
,
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{3}
understood as polynomials in
z
) are all scalar multiples of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{12}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}

The rationale behind recording the particular properties listed in Definition
4.5
is as follows. The normality of the family ensures that the progression does not become degenerate
17
17

17

A degenerate family would be one in which the difference of some two polynomials is independent of
z
; such a family is “bad” because successive applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz and van der Corput inequalities would not allow us to separate the functions corresponding to these polynomials.
and that the polynomial with index
1
continues to have the highest degree (which is essential for the function originally evaluated at
{\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z)
not to disappear at an intermediate step of PET).
The general form of the polynomials
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}
ensures that the coefficients of the
{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}
’s and their pairwise differences are bounded multiples of the coefficients of the
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
’s and their differences. The property (
i
) guarantees that we do not lose information about the coefficients of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
while carrying out PET.
The multilinearity of the polynomials
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}
given in (
ii
) will be crucial in our concatenation argument since, as we show in Section
7
, bounding the number of solutions to systems of multilinear equations is
much
simpler than obtaining bounds for general systems of polynomial equations. Lastly, property (
iii
) ensures that the coefficients of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}
come from only the
leading
coefficients of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}
, and so the end product of PET will not depend on the lower-degree coefficients.

We note several consequences of Definition
4.5
:

(i)

If
{\mathcal{Q}}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
, then the formulas (
28
) and (
29
) jointly ensure that the polynomials in
{\mathcal{Q}}
cannot have their total degree in
z,h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
greater than
d
.

(ii)

{\mathcal{P}}
descends from itself: Since
r=0
, the only choice of
{\underline{u}}
is
\underline{0}
, and one can set
w_{j\underline{0}}=j
and
w_{\underline{0}}=0
for each
j\in[\ell]
.

(iii)

The multilinearity in (
ii
) tells us that only
{\underline{u}}
’s in
\{0,1\}^{r}
can contribute to
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}
, so the multinomial coefficients simplify and we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-d_{1j}\end{subarray}}\frac{(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})!}{d_{1j}!}\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}.

(iv)

As a special case of (
iii
), if the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
differs from the leading coefficients of
{\mathbf{P}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}
, then for each
j
, the polynomial
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}
is a multilinear, homogeneous polynomial of degree
d-d_{1j}
,
and the expression for the polynomials
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}
further simplifies to

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|=d-d_{1j}\end{subarray}}\frac{d!}{d_{1j}!}\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}d}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}.

(v)

If the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
is the same as the leading coefficient of some
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
for
2\leq j\leq\ell
, then the polynomial
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}
ceases to be homogeneous and of degree
d-d_{1j}
; for instance, if
{\mathcal{P}}=(z^{2}+z,z^{2})
, then
\partial_{2}{\mathcal{P}}=((2h+1)z,2hz,z)
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
, but
2h+1
fails to be homogeneous and the constant polynomial
1
occurring as the coefficient of the polynomial
z
in
\partial_{2}{\mathcal{P}}
has degree
0<1=d-d_{12}
.

The descendent family obtained during the van der Corput operation admits more properties: For instance, the coefficients
w_{j{\underline{u}}},w_{\underline{u}}
can be shown to satisfy
w_{j{\underline{u}}}=w_{j{\underline{u}}^{\prime}}
and
w_{\underline{u}}=w_{{\underline{u}}^{\prime}}
whenever
\supp({\underline{u}})=\supp({\underline{u}}^{\prime})
(here,
\supp({\underline{u}}):=\{i\in[r]:\;u_{i}>0\}
; this property has been observed in
[
10
, Definition 5.5 (P3)]
and used in this paper in the proof of
[
10
, Proposition 5.7]
). However, the use of this observation has been superseded in our paper by the use of property (
ii
) from Definition
4.5
; this property previously appeared implicitly in
[
29
,
42
]
.

The following lemma ascertains that the van der Corput operation preserves the abovementioned properties inherited by
{\mathcal{Q}}
from
{\mathcal{P}}
by virtue of descending from
{\mathcal{P}}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
d,D,\ell,r,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
{\mathcal{P}}=({\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell})
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
with coefficients given by
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}z^{i}
.
Suppose that the family
{\mathcal{Q}}=({\mathbf{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s})
of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z,{\underline{h}}]
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then the family
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
also descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
for each
m\in[s]
for which
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}

has the highest degree (as a polynomial in
z
) among the elements of
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Proof.

We first note that the family
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
is normal: The application of the
*
operation ensures that the polynomials of the family are nonzero and distinct, and the assumption in the proposition ensures that the first polynomial in the family has the highest degree (in
z
).

We now check that
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfies the three properties of a descendent family.
Since
{\mathcal{Q}}
is a descendent family, the polynomials
{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}
satisfy the formulas (
28
) and (
29
) for some indices
w_{j{\underline{u}}},w_{\underline{u}}
with
w_{1{\underline{u}}}=1
.
Using the binomial theorem, we can expand

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(z+h_{r+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-i\end{subarray}}{{{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}+i}\choose{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}}}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+i)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+i)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}(z+h_{r+1})^{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-i\end{subarray}}{{{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}+i}\choose{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}}}}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+i)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+i)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}\sum_{l=0}^{i}{{{i}\choose{l}}}h_{r+1}^{i-l}z^{l}.

Changing the order of summation of
i
and
l
, setting
u_{r+1}:=i-l
, and using the identity

\displaystyle{{{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r+1}+l}\choose{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r+1}}}}={{{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}+u_{r+1}+l}\choose{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}}}}{{{u_{r+1}+l}\choose{l}}},

we obtain

{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(z+h_{r+1})=\sum_{l=0}^{d}z^{l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{r+1},\\
|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}\leq d-l\end{subarray}}{{{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r+1}+l}\choose{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r+1}}}}\\
({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+l)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+l)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}h_{r+1}^{u_{r+1}}.

Hence

(30)

{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(z+h_{r+1})-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}(z)=\sum_{l=0}^{d}z^{l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{r+1},\\
|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}\leq d-l\end{subarray}}{{{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r+1}+l}\choose{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r+1}}}}\\
({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+l)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+l)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}h_{r+1}^{u_{r+1}},

where we set
w_{j({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}:=w_{j{\underline{u}}}
and

\displaystyle w_{({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}:=\begin{cases}w_{\underline{u}},\;&u_{r+1}>0,\\
w_{m{\underline{u}}},\;&u_{r+1}=0.\end{cases}

In particular, we have
w_{1({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}=w_{1{\underline{u}}}=1
by the induction hypothesis, and so
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
satisfies property (
i
).

To show properties (
ii
) and (
iii
),
we need to compare
\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}(z,{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}})
with other polynomials in
\partial_{m}{\mathcal{Q}}
and examine the leading coefficients of these differences (as polynomials in
z
).
There are three cases to investigate. First, we check (
ii
) and (
iii
) for differences of the form

(31)

(\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}(z,{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}}))-(\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}}))\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1j}}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}({\underline{h}}))(z+h_{r+1})^{i}-({\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(h_{r+1},{\underline{h}})-{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(h_{r+1},{\underline{h}})),

where the terms
{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}})
cancel out.
The leading coefficient of this expression (interpreted as a polynomial in
z
) is
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}({\underline{h}})
, the same as the leading coefficient of the polynomial
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m}
, and this is multilinear by assumption. For (
iii
), we need to show that the nonzero vectors

{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+d_{1j})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+d_{1j})}

are leading coefficients of the polynomials
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{j({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}}
. If
u_{r+1}=0
, then this follows immediately from the formula

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+d_{1j})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+d_{1j})}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j})}

and the assumption on
{\mathcal{Q}}
, while if
u_{r+1}=1
, then

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+d_{1j})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}(|{\underline{u}}|+u_{r+1}+d_{1j})}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j}+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1j}+1)}=\mathbf{0}

because the leading coefficient
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}({\underline{h}})
is independent of
h_{r+1}
.

Second, we let
d_{1}
denote the degree of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}
in
z
and expand

(32)

\displaystyle(\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}(z,{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}}))-({{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}(z,{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1i}({\underline{h}})\mathopen{}\left((z+h_{r+1})^{i}-z^{i}\mathclose{}\right)-{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}(h_{r+1},{\underline{h}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1i}({\underline{h}}){{{i}\choose{l}}}h_{r+1}^{i-l}z^{l}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{l=1}^{d_{1}-1}z^{l}\sum_{i=l+1}^{d_{1}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1i}({\underline{h}}){{{i}\choose{l}}}h_{r+1}^{i-l};

the leading coefficient here is
d_{1}h_{r+1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})
, corresponding to
l=d_{1}-1,i=d_{1}
. The polynomial
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})
is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}
, so it is multilinear by assumption, and hence
d_{1}h_{r+1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})
is multilinear as well. Thus (
ii
) holds. To show property (
iii
), we first observe that the coefficient of
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}
in
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})
is
\frac{(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})!}{d_{1}!}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})})
(where we use implicitly property (
i
)). Therefore, the coefficient of
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}h_{r+1}
in
d_{1}h_{r+1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})
is

\frac{(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})!}{(d_{1}-1)!}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})})\\
=\frac{(|({\underline{u}},1)|+(d_{1}-1))!}{(d_{1}-1)!}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|({\underline{u}},1)|+(d_{1}-1))}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{({\underline{u}},1)}(|({\underline{u}},1)|+(d_{1}-1))}).

By the induction hypothesis, if
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})}
is nonzero, then it is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}}
, and hence

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|({\underline{u}},1)|+(d_{1}-1))}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{({\underline{u}},1)}(|({\underline{u}},1)|+(d_{1}-1))}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+d_{1})}

is also nonzero and the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{({\underline{u}},1)}}={\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{{\underline{u}}}}
.

It remains to consider

\displaystyle(\sigma_{h_{r+1}}{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{1}(z,{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}}))-({{\mathbf{Q}}}_{j}(z,{\underline{h}})-{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{m}(z,{\underline{h}}))

for
j\neq 1
; we split this case into two subcases. If
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})\neq{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1}}({\underline{h}})
, then the leading coefficient equals
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}({\underline{h}})={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1}}({\underline{h}})
. This is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}
, so it is multilinear by assumption, and property (
iii
) holds for the same reason as it held for (
31
).

Otherwise,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1}}({\underline{h}})
, in which case the leading coefficient equals

(33)

\displaystyle d_{1}h_{r+1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})+{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1(d_{1}-1)}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j(d_{1}-1)}({\underline{h}}),

where

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1(d_{1}-1)}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j(d_{1}-1)}({\underline{h}})=\begin{cases}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}({\underline{h}}),\;&\textrm{if}\quad d_{1j}=d_{1}-1,\\
\mathbf{0},\;&\textrm{if}\quad d_{1j}<d_{1}-1.\end{cases}

The term
d_{1}h_{r+1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})
is multilinear by the same argument as in the previous case. The term
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1(d_{1}-1)}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j(d_{1}-1)}({\underline{h}})
is trivially multilinear when
d_{1j}<d_{1}-1
, and its multilinearity follows from the assumption on
{\mathcal{Q}}
when
d_{1j}=d_{1}-1
(since then it is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}
). Hence (
33
) is multilinear as well.

It remains only to prove property (
iii
) in this very last case, namely, to show that if the coefficient of the monomial
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}h_{r+1}^{u_{r+1}}
in (
33
) is nonzero, then it is an appropriate scalar multiple of the leading coefficient of the polynomial
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{j({\underline{u}},u_{r+1})}}={\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
. If
u_{r+1}=1
, then the coefficient of
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}h_{r+1}^{u_{r+1}}
in (
33
) is the same as in
d_{1}h_{r+1}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1}}({\underline{h}})
, and the claim follows by the same reasoning as in the case of (
32
). Otherwise,
u_{r+1}=0
, and the coefficient
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}h_{r+1}^{u_{r+1}}
in (
33
) comes fully from
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1d_{1j}}({\underline{h}})-{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{jd_{1j}}({\underline{h}})
. The result then follows by the same argument as one used to prove property (
iii
) for (
31
).
∎

The next result ensures that we reach a family of linear polynomials after a bounded number of van der Corput operations; this fact is the foundation of all PET induction schemes.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
d,D,r,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
{\mathcal{P}}
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
of degree at most
d
, and let
{\mathcal{Q}}
be a normal family of polynomials in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z,{\underline{h}}]
of length
s
that descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
. There exist a nonnegative integer
r^{\prime}=O_{d,s}(1)
and positive integers
m_{1},\ldots,m_{r^{\prime}}
such that the family
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}:=\partial_{m_{i}}\cdots\partial_{m_{1}}{\mathcal{Q}}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
for each
i\in[r^{\prime}]
and, moreover, the elements of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{r^{\prime}}
are linear in
z
.

We remark that
r^{\prime}
is independent of the dimension
D
of the underlying space.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}:=({\mathbf{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s})
. As usual, we expand each
{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}
as in (
28
) and say that its leading coefficient is
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[{\underline{h}}]
for the largest
i\in[d]
with
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{ji}\neq\mathbf{0}
. Let
d^{\prime}
be the maximum degree of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{j},\ldots,{\mathbf{Q}}_{s}
as a polynomial in
z
; since
{\mathcal{Q}}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
and is normal, necessarily
1\leq d^{\prime}\leq d
. For
l\in[d^{\prime}]
, let
w_{l}
denote the number of distinct leading coefficients of the degree-
l
polynomials in
{\mathcal{Q}}
. We then define
w=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{d^{\prime}})
to be the
type
of
{\mathcal{Q}}
, and we order the types colexicographically.
18
18

18

That is, we have
w>w^{\prime}
if
w_{l}>w^{\prime}_{l}
for the largest
l
with
w_{l}\neq w^{\prime}_{l}
.

Since the family
{\mathcal{Q}}
is normal and has positive length, we have
w>(0,\ldots,0)
; let
l
be the smallest index for which
w_{l}\neq 0
. We define a parameter
m_{1}
as follows.
If
l<d^{\prime}
, then we choose an arbitrary
m_{1}\in[s]
with
\deg{\mathbf{Q}}_{m_{1}}=l
. If
l=d^{\prime}
(i.e., all of the polynomials in
{\mathcal{Q}}
have degree
d^{\prime}
as polynomials in
z
) and
w_{d^{\prime}}>1
, then we choose an arbitrary
m_{1}\in[s]
such that
{\mathbf{Q}}_{m_{1}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}
have different leading coefficients. Finally, if
l=d^{\prime}
and
w_{d^{\prime}}=1
(i.e., all of the polynomials have the same leading term), then we choose
{m_{1}}=1
. A standard calculation shows that in each case the new family
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}=\partial_{m_{1}}{\mathcal{Q}}
has type

w^{\prime}=(w^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{l-1},w_{l}-1,w_{l+1},\ldots,w_{d^{\prime}})<w,

for some
0\leq w^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,w^{\prime}_{l-1}\leq 2s
, and the polynomial
\partial_{h}{\mathbf{Q}}_{1}-{\mathbf{Q}}_{m_{1}}
has the maximum degree among the polynomials in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
. It follows from Proposition
4.7
that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
.

We now iterate this algorithm. Since each decreasing sequence of types
w^{0}>w^{1}>w^{2}>\cdots
with
w^{0}=w
eventually reaches a type
w^{r^{\prime}}=(w^{r^{\prime}}_{1},0,\ldots,0)
for some
r^{\prime}=O_{d^{\prime},s}(1)=O_{d,s}(1)

and
w^{r^{\prime}}_{1}=O_{r^{\prime},s}(1)=O_{d,s}(1)
, the algorithm terminates after
O_{d,s}(1)
steps, and we obtain the conclusion of the proposition.
∎

We remark that in the existing literature, the type of a polynomial family (as defined in the proof of Proposition
4.8
) is often expressed as a matrix rather than a (row) vector, with different coordinates in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
giving rise to different rows in the matrix (see e.g.
[
3
,
5
,
10
]
). This bookkeeping is excessive, however; as the above proof illustrates, the type-reduction proceeds in the same way regardless of
D
, and the number of steps does not depend on
D
. The argument is even more robust: The number of steps would in fact be the same if we considered polynomial families over any integral domains with characteristic either equal to
0
or sufficiently large in terms of the degrees of the polynomials and the order of their coefficients.
19
19

19

In the small-characteristic case, there may be additional degeneracies. For instance, a term
\beta_{d}z^{d}
may reduce eventually to a term
\beta_{d}dz^{d-1}h
, and if the underlying ring has characteristic dividing
\beta_{d}d
, then this term vanishes, which requires a more refined treatment. Other complications arise if the underlying ring has zero divisors. See
[
2
]
for an example of a PET argument dealing with the latter issue.

In the example discussed in Section
4.1
, the original family has type
(0,2)
(or
(1,1)
if
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{22}=\textbf{0}
; we ignore this case for now). The new families obtained in steps 1, 2, and 3 have types
(1,1)
,

(0,1)
and
(7,0)
(respectively).

4.3.
Bounds by averages of box seminorms

Having presented the PET induction scheme in detail, we are ready now to show that the counting operator (
25
) is controlled by an average of box norms whose directions can be described in terms of the coefficients of the polynomials appearing in the multidimensional polynomial progression and the coefficients of differences of these polynomials. We no longer assume that
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
has the highest degree among
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}
.

Proposition 4.9

(PET bound for polynomials)
.

Let
d,\ell,D\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
T>0
. There exist positive integers
r,s=O_{d,\ell}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{d,\ell,D,T}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
\delta>0
and
H,K,M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfy
H,M\leq T\delta^{-T}K
. Let
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[z]
be essentially distinct polynomials with degrees at most
d
and coefficients
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}z^{i}
.
Then for any
1
-bounded functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
, the lower bound

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}(z))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{{{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})}\cdot[\pm M],\ldots,{{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]}}^{2^{s}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D},

where
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[{\underline{h}}]
are nonzero multilinear polynomials depending only on
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}
, and they take the form

(34)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-1\end{subarray}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}

for some indices
w_{j{\underline{u}}}\in[0,\ell]
(with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}:=\mathbf{0}
). Moreover, each nonzero vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
from (
34
) is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
.

We typically apply Proposition
4.9
with
K\asymp_{d}(N/V)^{1/d}
for
V:=\max_{j,i}|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{ji}|
, as this provides the maximum possible range for which
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}(z)\in[\pm N]^{D}
for all
j\in[\ell]
and
z\in[K]
. Since we do not place any special assumptions on
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
, the role of
f_{1}
in the conclusion can be replaced by any of the functions
f_{0},\ldots,f_{\ell}
(with an appropriate modification of the formulas for (
34
)). For
f_{2},\ldots,f_{\ell}
, this is obvious by symmetry, whereas for
f_{0}
this follows after shifting
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{P}}_{m}
for some arbitrary
m\in[\ell]
and observing (as in the proof of Proposition
4.9
below) that the resulting collections of polynomials (
35
) are identical.

Proof.

We will proceed under the assumption that
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
has the largest degree among the
{\mathbf{P}}_{j}
’s; at the end we will describe the necessary modifications in the general case.

Proposition
4.8
provides
r=O_{d,\ell}(1)
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{r}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such the families
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\partial_{m_{i}}\cdots\partial_{m_{1}}{\mathcal{P}}
all descend from
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}
is linear in
z
. Let

\displaystyle{\mathbf{b}}_{1}({\underline{h}})z,\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{s}({\underline{h}})z

be the elements of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}
. Since the van der Corput operation at most doubles the length of a polynomial family, we have
s:=|{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}|\leq 2^{r}|{\mathcal{P}}|\ll_{d,\ell}1
. Applying Lemma
4.3
inductively to each of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{r-1}
(with
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}:={\mathcal{P}}
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}}\mu_{H}({\underline{h}}){\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{s}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{b}}_{j}({\underline{h}})z)}\gg_{d,D,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}N^{D}.

The power of
\delta
depends only on the number of iterations of Lemma
4.3
; in particular, it is independent of
D
.

Since the family
{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
, the polynomials
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{s}
are all distinct. Lemma
4.4
then implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\underline{h}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}}\mu_{H}({\underline{h}})\E_{{\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime}\in[\pm M]^{s}}\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\Delta^{\prime}_{{{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})},\ldots,{{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})};({\underline{m}},{\underline{m}}^{\prime})}f_{1}({\mathbf{x}})\gg_{d,D,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}N^{D},

where
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}
are the polynomials
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}-{\mathbf{b}}_{2},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{1}-{\mathbf{b}}_{s}
. Since
\mu_{H}({\underline{h}})\leq\frac{\mathbf{1}_{[-2H,2H]^{r}}({\underline{h}})}{(4H+1)^{r}}
pointwise and the inner average is nonnegative (by virtue of being an average of box norms), the inequality in the conclusion of the proposition follows after we replace
H
with
H/2
.

That the polynomials
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}
are nonzero is a consequence of the distinctness of
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{b}}_{s}
, which in turn is a consequence of the tuple
{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}
being normal. The form of
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}
is then a consequence of the properties (
i
)-(
iii
) from Definition
4.5
.

We now address the case where
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}
does not have the largest degree. Then some other
{\mathbf{P}}_{m}
has the largest degree, and we can translate
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{P}}_{m}(z)
so that

\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}(z))\\
=\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{P}}_{m}(z))\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}f_{j}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{j}(z)-{\mathbf{P}}_{m}(z)).

We set

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathbf{P}}}_{j}=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{P}}_{j}-{\mathbf{P}}_{m},\;&j\neq m,\\
-{\mathbf{P}}_{m},\;&j=m,\end{cases}

so that
\tilde{{\mathbf{P}}}_{1}
has the largest degree among the
\tilde{{\mathbf{P}}}_{j}
’s and we can apply the argument above (after exchanging
f_{0}
and
f_{m}
). The desired result now follows from the observation that

(35)

\displaystyle\{{\mathbf{P}}_{1},{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{j}:j\in[2,\ell]\}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\{\tilde{{\mathbf{P}}}_{1},\tilde{{\mathbf{P}}}_{1}-\tilde{{\mathbf{P}}}_{j}:j\in[2,\ell]\}

are identical as sets and hence these families of polynomials have the same leading coefficients.
∎

5.
Model example: concatenation of degree-
1
box norms

To illustrate some of the ideas that go into the proof of Theorem
1.10
, we start with a model concatenation result for averages of degree-
1
box norms.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
d,D,r\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
d\leq r
, and let
T>0
. There exists a positive real
C=O_{r,D,T}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
\delta>0
and
H,M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfy

(36)

\displaystyle C\delta^{-C}\leq H\leq M\quad\textrm{and}\quad H^{d}M\leq N,

and let
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})=\sum\limits_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}]
be a multilinear polynomial of degree
d
with coefficients of size at most
T
. Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

(37)

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]}^{2}\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{[\pm N]^{D},E}^{4}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D},

where

(38)

\displaystyle E=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}\cdot[\pm H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M].

We observe that the multiset
E
is a generalized arithmetic progression spanned by the coefficients
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}
, and that the length of the interval corresponding to the coefficient
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}
is of the same order of magnitude as the maximum value of the monomial
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}m
. Moreover, if
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}=\textbf{0}
, i.e., the monomial
{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}
does not appear in the polynomial
{\mathbf{C}}
, then the corresponding term does not contribute to the set
E
.

Example 5.2
.

To illustrate the content of Proposition
5.1
with a specific example, we consider the polynomial

{\mathbf{C}}(h_{1},h_{2})={\mathbf{e}}_{1}h_{1}h_{2}+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{1}=(h_{1}h_{2},h_{1}),

where
D=2
,
H=M=N^{1/3}
; for simplicity, we take
N
to be a perfect cube.
Proposition
5.1
tells us that if

\displaystyle\sum_{x_{1},x_{2},m}\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm N^{1/3}]}\mu_{N^{1/3}}(m)f(x_{1},x_{2})\overline{f(x_{1}+h_{1}h_{2}m,x_{2}+h_{1}m)}\geq\delta N^{2},

or equivalently

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbf{x}},m}\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm N^{1/3}]^{2}}\mu_{N^{1/3}}(m)f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f({\mathbf{x}}+({\mathbf{e}}_{1}h_{1}h_{2}+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{1})m)}\geq\delta N^{2},

then

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{{\mathbf{n}}_{1},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{1}\in[\pm N]^{2}}\E_{{\mathbf{n}}_{2},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{2}\in[\pm N]\times[\pm N^{2/3}]}\Delta^{\prime}_{({\mathbf{n}}_{1},{\mathbf{n}}_{1}^{\prime}),\;({\mathbf{n}}_{2},{\mathbf{n}}^{\prime}_{2})}f({\mathbf{x}})\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{2}.

Writing
{\mathbf{n}}_{2}=(n_{21},n_{22})
and
{\mathbf{n}}_{2}^{\prime}=(n_{21}^{\prime},n_{22}^{\prime})
, we see that the variables
n_{21},n_{21}^{\prime}
correspond to the monomial
h_{1}h_{2}
(equivalently, to the coefficient
{\underline{u}}=(1,1)
) and hence run along the coefficient
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
of
h_{1}h_{2}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
; the variables
n_{22},n_{22}^{\prime}
correspond to the monomial
h_{1}
(equivalently, to
{\underline{u}}=(1,0)
) and therefore run along the coefficient
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
of
h_{1}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
.

In applications, we usually want to discard the directions that correspond to lower-degree terms in the original polynomial, such as the direction
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
corresponding to the index
(1,0)
in the example above. We accomplish this using Lemma
3.5
, and obtain the following streamlined version of Proposition
5.1
.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
d,D,r\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
d\leq r
, and let
T>0
. There exists a positive real
C=O_{r,D,T}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\delta>0
satisfy
N\geq C\delta^{-C}
, and let
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})=\sum\limits_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}]
be a multilinear polynomial of degree
d
with coefficients of size at most
T
. Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the lower bound

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm N^{1/(d+1)}]^{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm N^{1/(d+1)}]}^{2}\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}\cdot[\pm N])^{2}}^{4}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D}

for each
{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
with
|{\underline{u}}|=d
and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}\neq\textbf{0}
.

Proof.

We let all the constants depend on
r,D,T
(and
d
, which however is bounded in terms of
r
).
Proposition
5.1
applied with
H=M=N^{1/(d+1)}
gives

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{[\pm N]^{D},E}^{4}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{D},

where
E
is as in (
38
). Since the coefficients
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}
have size
O(1)
, we can find vectors
{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
(with
k<D
) of size
O(1)
such that the set

\displaystyle\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}:\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r},\;|{\underline{u}}|=d,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}\neq\textbf{0}\}\cup\{{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{v}}_{k}\}

generates a subgroup in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
of index
O(1)
. Hence there exists a positive real
C=O(1)

such that

\displaystyle C\cdot[\pm N/C]^{D}\subseteq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|=d\end{subarray}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}\cdot[\pm CN]+\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\mathbf{v}}_{i}\cdot[\pm CN],

and so by successively applying properties (
vii
), (
iv
), (
v
) and (
vi
) of Lemma
3.5
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E^{\prime},E}^{4}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{D}

for

\displaystyle E^{\prime}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|=d\end{subarray}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}\cdot[\pm N].

To conclude from here, we pick a single
{\underline{u}}
and apply Lemma
3.5
(
v
).
∎

Corollary
5.3
allows us to upgrade the conclusion of Example
5.2
to

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{n_{1},n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2},n_{2}^{\prime}\in[\pm N]}\Delta^{\prime}_{(n_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1},n^{\prime}_{1}{\mathbf{e}}_{1}),\;(n_{2}{\mathbf{e}}_{1},n_{2}^{\prime}{\mathbf{e}}_{1})}f({\mathbf{x}})\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{2}.

5.1.
Sketch of the proof of Proposition
5.1

We now outline the proof of Proposition
5.1
in order to present the logic behind our concatenation argument in a relatively simple setting. This is an expanded version of the overview given in Section
2
. The argument gets considerably more complicated in the setting of box norms of degree greater than
1
, so the reader may find the simplified argument useful for grasping the main ideas. In the sketch, we use as a black box some technical results that will be derived in later sections.

Suppose that (
37
) holds. Expanding the definition of the box norm gives us

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\E_{m,m^{\prime}\in[\pm M]}\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\Delta^{\prime}_{({\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m,{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m^{\prime})}f({\mathbf{x}})\geq\delta N^{D}.

Shifting
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m
, replacing
m^{\prime}-m
by
m
, and changing the order of summation, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\sum_{m}\mu_{M}(m)\overline{f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m)}\geq\delta N^{D}.

We will use several applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to replace the polynomial
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m
by one with better equidistribution properties: Specifically, we want to replace it by a polynomial in a larger number of variables that does not assume any particular value more than a constant multiple of the “expected” number of times. Each application of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, which increases the number of variables, can be understood as a “smoothing” of the polynomial differencing parameter.

Step 1: Replacing
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m
by a polynomial with better equidistribution properties.

Applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in
{\mathbf{x}}
, we double the
{\underline{h}},m
variables and obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\E_{{\underline{h}}_{1},{\underline{h}}_{2}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu_{M}(m_{1},m_{2})f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{1})m_{1})\overline{f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{2})m_{2})}\geq\delta^{2}N^{D},

then we shift
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{1})m_{1}
to get

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\E_{{\underline{h}}_{1},{\underline{h}}_{2}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}}\mu_{M}(m_{1},m_{2})\overline{f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{2})m_{2}-{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{1})m_{1})}\geq\delta^{2}N^{D}.

Applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in
{\mathbf{x}}
again gives

\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{h}}_{1},{\underline{h}}_{2},\\
{\underline{h}}_{3},{\underline{h}}_{4}\in[\pm H]^{r}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},m_{2},\\
m_{3},m_{4}\end{subarray}}\mu_{M}(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4})\\
f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{2})m_{2}-{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{1})m_{1})\overline{f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{4})m_{4}-{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{3})m_{3})}\geq\delta^{4}N^{D},

and, after changing variables
{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto{\mathbf{x}}-({\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{2})m_{2}-{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{1})m_{1})
,
m_{2}\mapsto-m_{2},m_{3}\mapsto-m_{3}
, we obtain

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{h}}_{1},{\underline{h}}_{2},\\
{\underline{h}}_{3},{\underline{h}}_{4}\in[\pm H]^{r}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1},m_{2},\\
m_{3},m_{4}\end{subarray}}\mu_{M}(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4})\\
\sum_{x}f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{1})m_{1}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{2})m_{2}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{3})m_{3}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{4})m_{4}\mathclose{}\right)}\geq\delta^{4}N^{D}.

Thus, at the cost of increasing the power of
\delta
in our lower bound, we have replaced
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m
by the polynomial

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in[4]}{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{i})m_{i}={\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{1})m_{1}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{2})m_{2}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{3})m_{3}+{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}}_{4})m_{4},

which is more uniformly distributed in its range than the original polynomial
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m
.

Our strategy will be to fix values of the
{\underline{h}}_{i}
’s and then obtain equidistribution as the
m_{i}
’s range. However, the polynomial that we just obtained does not yet have all of the necessary equidistribution properties for carrying out this strategy.
The issue is roughly that when the components of the
{\underline{h}}_{i}
’s are small or have large common factors, the polynomial will concentrate on values that are small or have “highly-divisible” components. For instance, if
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})={\mathbf{e}}_{1}h_{1}h_{2}+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{1}
(as in Example
5.2
), then the new polynomial

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{1}(h_{11}h_{21}m_{1}+h_{12}h_{22}m_{2}+h_{13}h_{23}m_{3}+h_{14}h_{24}m_{4})

\displaystyle+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}(h_{11}m_{1}+h_{12}m_{2}+h_{13}m_{3}+h_{14}m_{4})

is not always equidistributed in its “natural” range
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\cdot[\pm 4H^{2}M]+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\cdot[\pm 4HM]
. Indeed, when the
h_{ij}
’s are small, so are the
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
- and
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
-coordinates. More subtly, the
{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
-coordinate must be a multiple of
\gcd(h_{11},h_{12},h_{13},h_{14})
, and this leads to concentration when the
\gcd
is large. Analogously, the
{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
-coordinate is concentrated on a small subset of its natural range when
\gcd(h_{11}h_{21},h_{12}h_{22},h_{13}h_{23},h_{14}h_{24})
is large.

A simple fix for each of these problems separately would be to throw out a small set of “bad” tuples of
{\underline{h}}_{i}
’s that prevents equidistribution in one coordinate or the other. However, the task of ensuring
simultaneous
equidistribution of the two coordinates is complicated by the presence of
h_{1i},m_{i}
’s in both coefficients. We eventually resolve this issue by combining two maneuvers. The first maneuver is duplicating the variables in both coordinates via several more applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality; this will allow us to “decouple” the coefficients. The second maneuver is throwing out a few bad
{\underline{h}}_{i}
’s to prevent the aforementioned issues of concentration. (The notion of “badness” for
{\underline{h}}_{i}
’s will look more complicated after we have carried out the first maneuver.) Let us now turn to the details.

We use more applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to increase the number of the variables
h_{2i},\ldots,h_{ri}
and uniformize the polynomial further; after enough duplications of variables using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we will be able to show that the resulting polynomial is equidistributed in its natural range for a generic choice of the
h
’s.
Writing
{\underline{h}}_{i}=(h_{1i},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{i})
, we now apply the Cauchy–Schwarz
inequality twice in
{\mathbf{x}}
and
h_{1i}

to obtain

\E_{(h_{i_{1}})_{i_{1}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{4}}\E_{(\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{i_{1}i_{2}})_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{16(r-1)}}\sum_{(m_{i_{1}i_{2}})_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[4]}}\mu_{M}((m_{i_{1}i_{2}})_{i_{1},i_{2}})\\
\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f\Bigl({\mathbf{x}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2}\in[4]\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{C}}(h_{1i_{1}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{i_{1}i_{2}})m_{i_{1}i_{2}}\Bigr)}\geq\delta^{16}N^{D}.

We continue in this manner: Applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality twice in the variables

{\mathbf{x}},h_{1i_{1}},h_{2i_{1}i_{2}},\ldots,h_{(l-1)i_{1}\cdots i_{l-1}}

for each
l=3,4,\ldots,r
(as in the
l=2
step just illustrated; the
l=1
step appeared at the very beginning of the proof), and bounding the Fejér kernels pointwise, we arrive at the bound

\E_{(h_{1i_{1}})_{i_{1}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{4}}\cdots\E_{(h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{4^{r}}}\E_{(m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}\in[\pm 2M]^{4^{r}}}\\
\Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f\Bigl({\mathbf{x}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{C}}(h_{1i_{1}},\ldots,h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}})m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\Bigr)}\Bigr|\gg_{r}\delta^{4^{r}}N^{D}.

Step 2: Applying the equidistribution properties of the new polynomial.

We have now replaced
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})m
by the massive polynomial

(39)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{C}}(h_{1i_{1}},\ldots,h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}})m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\\
=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\in[4]\end{subarray}}h_{1i_{1}}^{u_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{2}\in[4]\end{subarray}}h_{2i_{1}i_{2}}^{u_{2}}\cdots\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}}^{u_{r}}m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}.

For instance, if
{\mathbf{C}}({\underline{h}})={\mathbf{e}}_{1}h_{1}h_{2}+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}h_{1}
(as in Example
5.2
), then the polynomial (
39
) equals

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[4]}h_{1i_{1}}h_{2i_{1}i_{2}}m_{i_{1}i_{2}}+{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[4]}h_{1i_{1}}m_{i_{1}i_{2}}.

The crucial point is that for a generic choice of
h
’s, the subpolynomials of (
39
) corresponding to different
{\underline{u}}
’s are simultaneously well distributed in their ranges. This result on simultaneous equidistribution is one of the new inputs of this paper, and we outsource its exact statement and proof to Proposition
7.5
. For now, however, let us briefly explain how this good distribution property of the polynomial (
39
) arises from its “multiscale” structure. For a generic choice of
h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}}
, the polynomial

\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}},\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}}m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\Bigr)

is well distributed in its range; this allows us to essentially replace both sums by new variables
t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}0}
and
t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}1}
ranging over
[\pm 4M],[\pm 4HM]
respectively.
Then for a generic choice of
h_{(r-1)i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}}
, the polynomial

\displaystyle\Bigl(\sum_{i_{r-1}\in[4]}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}0},\sum_{i_{r-1}\in[4]}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}1},\sum_{i_{r-1}\in[4]}h_{(r-1)i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}0},\sum_{i_{r-1}\in[4]}h_{(r-1)i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}1}\Bigr)

is well distributed in its range
[\pm 16M]\times[\pm 16HM]\times[\pm 16HM]\times[\pm 16H^{2}M]
, and so on. (We could obtain the same conclusion by summing over the indices in triples rather than quadruples, but we work with quadruples because each application of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality doubles the number of variables.)
Because of this iterative scheme, it suffices to to show that (
39
) is well distributed in its range for
r=1
, and this can be accomplished using elementary counting arguments.

For
l\in[r]
and
\eta>0
, define the set

{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\eta}=\{(h_{li_{1}\cdots i_{l}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{4^{l}}:\;|h_{li_{1}\cdots i_{l}}-h_{li^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots i^{\prime\prime}_{l}}|\geq\eta H,\\
\gcd(h_{li_{1}\cdots i_{l}}-h_{li^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots i^{\prime\prime}_{l}},h_{li^{\prime}_{1}\cdots i^{\prime}_{l}}-h_{li^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots i^{\prime\prime}_{l}})\leq\eta^{-1}\\
\textrm{for {all} distinct}\;(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}),\;(i^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime}_{l}),\;(i^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,i^{\prime\prime}_{l})\in[4]^{l}\}.

This is a set of tuples
(h_{li_{1}\cdots i_{l}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}
whose coordinates are not too close to each other and do not have large common factors. It is not difficult to show that for a small
\eta>0
, a generic tuple in
[\pm H]^{4^{l}}
belongs to this set.
Indeed, taking
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}:={\mathcal{H}}_{l,c\delta^{1/c}}
for sufficiently small
c=c(r)>0
, we have by Lemma
7.8
that all but a
O_{r}(\delta^{4^{r}})
-proportion of the elements of
[\pm H]^{4^{l}}
lie in
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}
, and hence

\E_{(h_{1i_{1}})_{i_{1}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{4}}\cdots\E_{(h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{4^{r}}}\E_{(m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}\in[\pm 2M]^{4^{r}}}\\
