\prod_{l\in[r]}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{l}(h_{(li_{1}\cdots i_{l})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}}})}\cdot\Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f\Bigl({\mathbf{x}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}{\mathbf{C}}(h_{1i_{1}},\ldots,h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}})m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\Bigr)}\Bigr|\gg_{r}\delta^{4^{r}}N^{D}.

We have thus excluded the
h^{\prime}s
whose coefficients are close to each other or have large common factors.

For
(n_{\underline{u}})_{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2^{r}}
, let

{\mathcal{N}}((n_{\underline{u}})_{{\underline{u}}})=\E_{(h_{1i_{1}})_{i_{1}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{4}}\cdots\E_{(h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}\in[\pm H]^{4^{r}}}\E_{(m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}\in[\pm 2M]^{4^{r}}}\\
\prod_{l\in[r]}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{l}((h_{li_{1}\cdots i_{l}})_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}})}\cdot\prod_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}\in[4]\end{subarray}}h_{1i_{1}}^{u_{1}}\cdots\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}h_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}}^{u_{r}}m_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}=n_{\underline{u}}\Bigr),

so that

(40)

\displaystyle\sum_{(n_{\underline{u}})_{\underline{u}}}{\mathcal{N}}((n_{\underline{u}})_{\underline{u}})\cdot\Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f\Bigl({\mathbf{x}}+\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}n_{\underline{u}}\Bigr)}\Bigr|\gg_{r}\delta^{4^{r}}N^{D}.

The quantity
{\mathcal{N}}((n_{\underline{u}}))
is the normalized count of
\mathopen{}\left({\underline{h}}_{1i_{1}},\ldots,{\underline{h}}_{ri_{1}\cdots i_{r}},{\underline{m}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\mathclose{}\right)
such that for each
{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, the subpolynomial associated with
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}
in (
39
) equals
n_{\underline{u}}
. The key equidistribution result on the polynomial (
39
) is the assertion that the normalised count
{\mathcal{N}}
satisfies the pointwise bound

(41)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}((n_{\underline{u}})_{\underline{u}})\ll_{r}\delta^{-O_{r}(1)}M^{-2^{r}}H^{-r2^{r-1}}.

This bound is a special case of Proposition
7.5
to be derived later on.

Let us explain why the bound (
41
) is of the right order of magnitude. Note first that
{\mathcal{N}}((n_{\underline{u}})_{\underline{u}})=0
unless each
|n_{\underline{u}}|\leq C_{\underline{u}}H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M
for some
C_{\underline{u}}>0
. Defining

\displaystyle E_{0}=\left\{(n_{\underline{u}})_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}:\;n_{\underline{u}}\in[-C_{\underline{u}}H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M,C_{\underline{u}}H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M],\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\right\},

we observe that

\displaystyle|E_{0}|\asymp_{r}\prod_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}(H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M)=M^{2^{r}}H^{r2^{r-1}}

and note that the exponents agree with those in (
41
). Combining these observations with (
40
) and (
41
), we get

\displaystyle\E_{(n_{\underline{u}})_{\underline{u}}\in E_{0}}\Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f\Bigl({\mathbf{x}}+\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}n_{\underline{u}}\Bigr)}\Bigr|\gg_{r}\delta^{O_{r}(1)}N^{D}.

Defining

\displaystyle E_{1}=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\underline{u}}\cdot[\pm C_{\underline{u}}H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M],

we have

\displaystyle\E_{{\mathbf{n}}\in E_{1}}\Bigl|\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f\mathopen{}\left({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{n}}\mathclose{}\right)}\Bigr|\gg_{r}\delta^{O_{r}(1)}N^{D}.

Using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to remove the absolute value and recalling that
f
is supported on
[N]^{D}
, we obtain

\E_{{\mathbf{n}}\in E_{1}}\E_{{\mathbf{s}}\in[\pm N]^{D}}\sum_{\mathbf{x}}f({\mathbf{x}})\overline{f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{s}})f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{n}})}f({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{s}}+{\mathbf{n}})\gg_{r}\delta^{O_{r}(1)}N^{D}.

Two further applications of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to double
{\mathbf{n}},{\mathbf{s}}
let us introduce Fejér kernels, and we get

\left\|f\right\|^{4}_{E_{1},[\pm N]^{D}}\gg_{r}\delta^{O_{r}(1)}N^{D}.

To conclude the proof, we use Lemma
3.5
(
vi
) to replace
E_{1}
by
E
.

6.
Concatenation of averages of higher-degree seminorms

In the previous section, we showed how to concatenate an average of degree-
1
box norms with polynomial directions. The argument becomes significantly more complicated when we deal with higher-degree box norms. In the degree-
1
setting, applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality (as in the proof of Proposition
5.1
) did not cause the degree of the box norm to increase. This pleasant property ceases to hold in the setting of higher-degree box norms; rather, each application of the Cauchy–Schwarz and Gowers-Cauchy–Schwarz inequalities will involve passing to norms of much higher degree. The objective of the present section is to carry out this higher-degree argument, along the lines of the prior work of the second author
[
22
]
. As noted in the proof outline, this stage of the concatenation arguments works for any box norms, not necessarily those that come out of PET and involve polynomials. To emphasize the robustness of this part of the concatenation argument, we phrase the results in this section in a more abstract setting of an average of box norms on an arbitrary countable abelian group
G
. We will thus deal with expressions of the form

\displaystyle\E_{i\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}}^{2^{s}},

where
I
is a finite indexing set and

H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}\subseteq G
are finite nonempty multisets for each
i\in I
. In our applications, the set
I
will be
{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}
, and the multisets
H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}
for
i\in I
will be the sets

{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M],\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]

for
{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}
.

The following lemma will serve as the basis for the concatenation argument. It is an adaptation of
[
22
, Lemma 2.2]
from
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{D}
to more general countable abelian groups.

Lemma 6.1
.

For every
s\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a positive real
C=O_{s}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
A,\delta>0
,
let
G
be a countable abelian group, let
I
be a finite indexing set, and let
H_{i},K_{1i},\ldots,K_{si}\subseteq G
be finite multisets for each
i\in I
. Suppose that
B\subseteq G
is a set
satisfying

\displaystyle|\supp(B+B-B)|\leq A|B|.

Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f
supported on
B
, the bound

\displaystyle{\E_{i\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{i},K_{1i},\ldots,K_{si}}^{2^{s+1}}}\geq\delta|B|

implies that

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{K_{1i_{0}},\ldots,K_{si_{0}},K_{1i_{1}},\ldots,K_{si_{1}},H_{i_{1}}-H_{i_{0}}}^{2^{2s+1}}\geq\frac{1}{C}A^{-C}\delta^{2^{2s+1}}|B|.

In applications, we will take
B=[N]^{D}
and
A=3^{D}
since

\supp(B+B-B)=[2-N,2N-1]^{D}.

Proof.

Let
K_{i}:=K_{1i}\times\cdots\times K_{si}
.
The alternative formula (
21
) for box norms yields

\displaystyle\E_{i\in I}\sum_{x}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in K_{i}\end{subarray}}\sum_{h\in H_{i}}\mu_{K_{i}}({\underline{k}})\mu_{H_{i}}(h)\Delta_{{\underline{k}},h}f(x)\geq\delta|B|.

We expand

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\underline{k}},h}f(x)=f(x)\overline{f(x+h)}\Delta_{{\underline{k}}}^{*}\Delta_{h}f(x),

where
\Delta_{{\underline{k}}}^{*}f(x)=\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus\{0\}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f(x+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{k}})
,
and change
the order of summation to get

\displaystyle\sum_{x}f(x)\E_{i\in I}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in K_{i}\end{subarray}}\sum_{h\in H_{i}}\mu_{K_{i}}({\underline{k}})\mu_{H_{i}}(h)\overline{f(x+h)}\Delta^{*}_{{\underline{k}}}\Delta_{h}f(x)\geq\delta|B|.

An application of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in
x
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}_{0}\in K_{i_{0}},\\
{\underline{k}}_{1}\in K_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{0}\in H_{i_{0}},\\
h_{1}\in H_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\mu_{K_{i_{0}}}({\underline{k}}_{0})\mu_{K_{i_{1}}}({\underline{k}}_{1})\mu_{H_{i_{0}}}(h_{0})\mu_{H_{i_{1}}}(h_{1})\overline{f(x+h_{0})}f(x+h_{1})

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}_{{\underline{k}}_{0}}\Delta_{h_{0}}f(x)\overline{\Delta^{*}_{{\underline{k}}_{1}}\Delta_{h_{1}}f(x)}\geq\delta^{2}|B|.

We crucially observe that for each fixed
i_{0},i_{1}\in I
and
h_{0}\in H_{i_{0}},h_{1}\in H_{i_{1}}
, the average

(42)

\displaystyle\sum_{x}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}_{0}\in K_{i_{0}},\\
{\underline{k}}_{1}\in K_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\mu_{K_{i_{0}}}({\underline{k}}_{0})\mu_{K_{i_{1}}}({\underline{k}}_{1})f(x+h_{0})\overline{f(x+h_{1})}\Delta^{*}_{{\underline{k}}_{0}}\Delta_{h_{0}}f(x)\overline{\Delta^{*}_{{\underline{k}}_{1}}\Delta_{h_{1}}f(x)}

is a box inner product of
1
-bounded functions along
K_{1i_{0}},\ldots,K_{si_{0}},K_{1i_{1}},\ldots,K_{si_{1}}
, with
f(x+h_{0})\overline{f(x+h_{1})}
being the term with multi-index
\underline{0}
. Moreover, since
f
is supported on
B
, the summand in (
42
) vanishes unless

\displaystyle x+h_{j},\;x+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{j}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{j},\;x+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{j}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{j}+h_{j}\in B\quad\textrm{for all}\quad j\in\{0,1\},\;{\underline{\epsilon}}_{j}\neq\underline{0}.

In particular, if all of these elements lie in
B
, then we also have

x+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{0}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{0}+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{1}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{1}+h_{0}=(x+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{0}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{0}+h_{0})+(x+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{1}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{1}+h_{1})-(x+h_{1})\in B+B-B

for any
{\underline{\epsilon}}_{0},{\underline{\epsilon}}_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus{\{0\}}
,
and so we can introduce the term

(43)

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\epsilon}}_{0},{\underline{\epsilon}}_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\setminus{\{0\}}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{B+B-B-h_{0}}(x+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{0}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{0}+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{1}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{1})

into (
42
) without changing the value of the expression. The purpose of inserting this term is to ensure that if we view (
42
) as a box inner product along
K_{1i_{0}},\ldots,K_{si_{0}},K_{1i_{1}},\ldots,K_{si_{1}}
, then the functions evaluated at
x+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{0}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{0}+{\underline{\epsilon}}_{1}\cdot{\underline{k}}_{1}
with
{\underline{\epsilon}}_{0},{\underline{\epsilon}}_{1}\neq\underline{0}
have support of size bounded by
|\supp(B+B-B)|
, and hence by
A|B|
by our assumption.

We now apply the Gowers-Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to (
42
) weighed by the product (
43
) to obtain

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\sum_{x}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}_{0}\in K_{i_{0}},\\
{\underline{k}}_{1}\in K_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\mu_{K_{i_{0}}}({\underline{k}}_{0})\mu_{K_{i_{1}}}({\underline{k}}_{1})\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{0}\in H_{i_{0}},\\
h_{1}\in H_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{{\underline{k}}_{0},{\underline{k}}_{1}}(f(x+h_{0})\overline{f(x+h_{1})})\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{2^{2s+1}}|B|,

or equivalently

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}_{0},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}_{0}\in K_{i_{0}},\\
{\underline{k}}_{1},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}_{1}\in K_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{0}\in H_{i_{0}},\\
h_{1}\in H_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\Delta^{\prime}_{({\underline{k}}_{0},{\underline{k}}_{0}^{\prime}),({\underline{k}}_{1},{\underline{k}}_{1}^{\prime})}(f(x)\overline{f(x+h_{1}-h_{0})})\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{2^{2s+1}}|B|

after we expand the kernels
\mu_{K_{i_{0}}},\mu_{K_{i_{1}}}
and change variables
x\mapsto x-h_{0}
. Using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to double the variables
h_{0},h_{1}
yields

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\sum_{x}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}_{0},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}_{0}\in K_{i_{0}},\\
{\underline{k}}_{1},{\underline{k}}^{\prime}_{1}\in K_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{0},h_{0}^{\prime}\in H_{i_{0}},\\
h_{1},h_{1}^{\prime}\in H_{i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\Delta^{\prime}_{({\underline{k}}_{0},{\underline{k}}_{0}^{\prime}),({\underline{k}}_{1},{\underline{k}}_{1}^{\prime}),(h_{1}-h_{0},h_{1}^{\prime}-h_{0}^{\prime})}f(x)\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{2^{2s+2}}|B|.

This expression is now an average (indexed by
i_{0},i_{1}
) of box norms along
K_{i_{0}},K_{i_{1}},H_{i_{1}}-H_{i_{0}}
. The inductive formula for box norms gives the claimed bound

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{K_{1i_{0}},\ldots,K_{si_{0}},K_{1i_{1}},\ldots,K_{si_{1}},H_{i_{1}}-H_{i_{0}}}^{2^{2s+1}}\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{2^{2s+2}}|B|.

We reiterate our convention that
H_{i_{1}}-H_{i_{0}}
denotes the Minkowski sum counted with multiplicity and in particular is itself a multiset.
∎

By repeatedly applying Lemma
6.1
, we derive the following concatenation result, whose proof follows closely that of
[
22
, Proposition 2.3]
.

Proposition 6.2

(Concatenation of box norms, version I)
.

For every
s\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a positive real
C=O_{s}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
A,\delta>0
,
let
G
be a countable abelian group, let
I
be a finite indexing set, and let
H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}\subseteq G
be finite multisets for each
i\in I
. Suppose that
B\subseteq G
is a set
satisfying

\displaystyle|\supp(B+B-B)|\leq A|B|.

Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f
supported on
B
, the bound

(44)

\displaystyle{\E_{i\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}}^{2^{s}}}\geq\delta|B|

implies that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in I,\\
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{H_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}-H_{ji_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ j\in[s]\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\\
\mathrm{with}\ (\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{s-j})=(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon^{\prime}_{s-j}),\ \epsilon_{s+1-j}=1,\ \epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1-j}=0\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{d}}\geq\frac{1}{C}A^{-C}\delta^{C}|B|,

where
d
is the degree of the box norms on the left-hand side.

Since the indices
{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}
are always distinct, the multiset
H_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}-H_{ji_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}
is usually more uniformly distributed than both of the multisets
H_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}},H_{ji_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}
.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
6.2
relies on a gradual concatenation of the “unconcatenated” multisets
H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}
using Lemma
6.1
. We repeatedly use the inductive formula for box norms in order to reinterpret the average in such a way that successive applications of Lemma
6.1
concatenate the multisets
H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}
one by one.

Starting with (
44
), we apply Lemma
6.1
to bound

(45)

\displaystyle\E_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1i_{0}},\ldots,H_{(s-1)i_{0}},H_{1i_{1}},\ldots,H_{(s-1)i_{1}},H_{si_{1}}-H_{si_{0}}}^{2^{2s-1}}\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}|B|.

Instead of having
s
unconcatenated multisets indexed by
i
, we now have
s-1
unconcatenated multisets indexed by
i_{0}
and another
s-1
unconcatenated multisets indexed by
i_{1}
, together with the concatenated multiset
H_{si_{1}}-H_{si_{0}}
. Even though the total number of multisets has nearly doubled, we have made progress because the number of unconcatenated multisets for each index
i_{0},i_{1}
decreased by
1
.

At the next stage of the argument, we will apply Lemma
6.1
twice to concatenate
H_{(s-1)i_{0}}
and then
H_{(s-1)i_{1}}
. As a consequence, the two indices
i_{0},i_{1}
will be replaced by four indices
i_{00},i_{01},i_{10},i_{11}
, and for each of them we will have exactly
s-2
unconcatenated multisets.
We will continue in this manner: At each stage, the number of indices
i_{\underline{\epsilon}}
will double, but the number of unconcatenated multisets with each index
i_{\underline{\epsilon}}
will decrease by
1
.
Eventually, after
s
steps, we will be left with
2^{s-1}
unconcatenated multisets
H_{1i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
, and
2^{s-1}
applications of Lemma
6.1
will allow us to concatenate them all without producing any new unconcatenated multisets. This will finish the argument.

To illustrate this general strategy, let us see in detail what happens at the second stage, i.e., after we have obtained the bound (
45
). Using the induction formula for box norms, we can express (
45
) as

\E_{i_{1}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1i_{1}},h_{1i_{1}}^{\prime}\in H_{1i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\cdots\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{(s-1)i_{1}},h_{(s-1)i_{1}}^{\prime}\in H_{(s-1)i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\\
\E_{i_{0}\in I}\left\|\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1i_{1}},h_{1i_{1}}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{(s-1)i_{1}},h_{(s-1)i_{1}}^{\prime})}f\right\|_{H_{1i_{0}},\ldots,H_{(s-1)i_{0}},H_{si_{1}}-H_{si_{0}}}^{2^{s}}\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}|B|.

For each fixed
i_{1},h_{1i_{1}},h_{1i_{1}}^{\prime},\ldots,h_{(s-1)i_{1}},h_{(s-1)i_{1}}^{\prime}
, we apply Lemma
6.1
separately to each average over
i_{0}
, obtaining

\E_{i_{1}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1i_{1}},h_{1i_{1}}^{\prime}\in H_{1i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\cdots\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{(s-1)i_{1}},h_{(s-1)i_{1}}^{\prime}\in H_{(s-1)i_{1}}\end{subarray}}\E_{i_{00},i_{01}\in I}\\
\left\|\Delta^{\prime}_{(h_{1i_{1}},h_{1i_{1}}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{(s-1)i_{1}},h_{(s-1)i_{1}}^{\prime})}f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{1i_{00}},\ldots,H_{(s-2)i_{00}},H_{1i_{01}},\ldots,H_{(s-2)i_{01}},\\
H_{(s-1)i_{01}}-H_{(s-1)i_{00}},H_{si_{1}}-H_{si_{00}},H_{si_{1}}-H_{si_{01}}\end{subarray}}^{2^{2s-1}}\hskip-17.07164pt\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}|B|.

Using the inductive formula for box norms and swapping the indices of
\Delta^{\prime}
and the box norm, we can rearrange this to get

\E_{i_{00},i_{01}\in I}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1i_{00}},h_{1i_{00}}^{\prime}\in H_{1i_{00}},\\
h_{1i_{01}},h_{1i_{01}}^{\prime}\in H_{1i_{01}}\end{subarray}}\cdots\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{(s-1)i_{00}},h_{(s-1)i_{00}}^{\prime}\in H_{(s-1)i_{00}},\\
h_{(s-1)i_{01}},h_{(s-1)i_{01}}^{\prime}\in H_{(s-1)i_{01}}\end{subarray}}\\
\E_{i_{1}\in I}\left\|\Delta^{\prime}_{\begin{subarray}{c}(h_{1i_{00}},h_{1i_{00}}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{(s-2)i_{00}},h_{(s-2)i_{00}}^{\prime})\\
(h_{1i_{01}},h_{1i_{01}}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{(s-2)i_{01}},h_{(s-2)i_{01}}^{\prime}),\\
(h_{(s-1)i_{01}}-h_{(s-1)i_{00}},h^{\prime}_{(s-1)i_{01}}-h^{\prime}_{(s-1)i_{00}})\end{subarray}}f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{1i_{1}},\ldots,H_{(s-1)i_{1}},\\
H_{si_{1}}-H_{si_{00}},H_{si_{1}}-H_{si_{01}}\end{subarray}}^{2^{s+1}}\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}|B|.

By Lemma
6.1
applied separately to each average over
i_{1}\in I
, we have

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{00},i_{01},\\
i_{10},i_{11}\in I\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1i_{00}},h_{1i_{00}}^{\prime}\in H_{1i_{00}},\\
h_{1i_{01}},h_{1i_{01}}^{\prime}\in H_{1i_{01}}\end{subarray}}\cdots\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{(s-1)i_{00}},h_{(s-1)i_{00}}^{\prime}\in H_{(s-1)i_{00}},\\
h_{(s-1)i_{01}},h_{(s-1)i_{01}}^{\prime}\in H_{(s-1)i_{01}}\end{subarray}}\\
\left\|\Delta^{\prime}_{\begin{subarray}{c}(h_{1i_{00}},h_{1i_{00}}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{(s-2)i_{00}},h_{(s-2)i_{00}}^{\prime})\\
(h_{1i_{01}},h_{1i_{01}}^{\prime}),\ldots,(h_{(s-2),i_{01}},h_{(s-2),i_{01}}^{\prime}),\\
(h_{(s-1)i_{01}}-h_{(s-1)i_{00}},h^{\prime}_{(s-1)i_{01}}-h^{\prime}_{(s-1)i_{00}})\end{subarray}}f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{1i_{10}},\ldots,H_{(s-2)i_{10}},H_{1i_{11}},\ldots,H_{(s-2)i_{11}},\\
H_{(s-1)i_{11}}-H_{(s-1)i_{10}},H_{si_{10}}-H_{si_{00}},H_{si_{11}}-H_{si_{00}},\\
H_{si_{10}}-H_{si_{01}},H_{si_{11}}-H_{si_{01}}\end{subarray}}^{2^{2s+1}}\hskip-56.9055pt\\
\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}|B|.

Another application of the inductive formula for box norms lets us write this as

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{00},i_{01},\\
i_{10},i_{11}\in I\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{1i_{00}},\ldots,H_{(s-2)i_{00}},H_{1i_{01}},\ldots,H_{(s-2)i_{01}},\\
H_{1i_{10}},\ldots,H_{(s-2)i_{10}},H_{1i_{11}},\ldots,H_{(s-2)i_{11}},\\
H_{(s-1)i_{01}}-H_{(s-1)i_{00}},H_{(s-1)i_{11}}-H_{(s-1)i_{10}},\\
H_{si_{10}}-H_{si_{00}},H_{si_{11}}-H_{si_{00}},\\
H_{si_{10}}-H_{si_{01}},H_{si_{11}}-H_{si_{01}}\end{subarray}}^{2^{4s-2}}\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}|B|,

which can be expressed more compactly as

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{00},i_{01},\\
i_{10},i_{11}\in I\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{H_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}:\ {\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{2},\ j=1,\ldots,s-2\},\\
\{H_{(s-1)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}-H_{(s-1)i_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2}\ \mathrm{with}\ \epsilon_{1}=\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ \epsilon_{2}=1,\ \epsilon^{\prime}_{2}=0\},\\
\{H_{si_{\underline{\epsilon}}}-H_{si_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{2}\ \mathrm{with}\ \epsilon_{1}=1,\ \epsilon^{\prime}_{1}=0\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{4s-2}}\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}|B|.

We have thus successfully concatenated all of the multisets
H_{(s-1)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
and
H_{si_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
.

At the next stage, we concatenate the multisets
H_{(s-2)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
. Applying Lemma
6.1
and the induction formula for box norms four times (as in the previous stage), each time to an average over
i_{00},i_{01},i_{10},i_{11}
(respectively), we arrive at the inequality

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{{\underline{\epsilon}}}\in I,\\
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{3}\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{H_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}:\ {\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{3},\ j=1,\ldots,s-3\},\\
\{H_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}-H_{ji_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ j=s-2,s-1,s,\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{3}\\
\mathrm{with}\ (\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{s-j})=(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon^{\prime}_{s-j}),\ \epsilon_{s+1-j}=1,\ \epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1-j}=0\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{d^{\prime}}}\gg_{s}A^{-O_{s}(1)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}|B|,

where
d^{\prime}
is the degree of the box norms on the left-hand side.
This time, we have successfully concatenated the multisets
H_{(s-2)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
. At the next step, eight applications of Lemma
6.1
and the induction formula for box norms allow us to concatenate multisets
H_{(s-3)i_{\underline{\epsilon}}}
. Continuing the argument in this manner, we arrive at the conclusion of the proposition after a total of

\displaystyle 1+2+2^{2}+\cdots+2^{s-1}=2^{s}-1

applications of Lemma
6.1
and the induction formula for box norms.
∎

We now present two corollaries of Proposition
6.2
which are better suited for applications in
{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}
.
In what follows, we order the set
\{0,1\}^{s}
lexicographically.
Identifying
\{0,1\}^{s}
with
[2^{s}]
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 6.3

(Concatenation of box norms, version II)
.

Let
s,D\in\mathbb{N}
. There exist positive reals
C=O_{s,D}(1)
and
C^{\prime}=O_{s}(1)
such that the following holds.
Let
A,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
I
be a finite indexing set, and let
H_{ji}\subseteq[\pm AN]^{D}
be finite nonempty multisets for each
i\in I
and
j\in[s]
. Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

\displaystyle\E_{i\in I}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1i},\ldots,H_{si}}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

(46)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t}\in I\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\{H_{jk_{i_{1}}}-H_{jk_{i_{2}}}:\ j\in[s],\ 0<i_{1}<i_{2}\leq t\}}^{2^{d}}\geq\frac{1}{C}A^{-C^{\prime}D}\delta^{C}N^{D},

where
t=2^{s}
and
d
is the degree of the box norms on the left-hand side.

Proof.

Proposition
6.2
immediately implies that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{\underline{\epsilon}}\in I,\\
{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{H_{ji_{\underline{\epsilon}}}-H_{ji_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}}:\ j\in[s]\ {\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{s}\\
\mathrm{with}\ (\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{s-j})=(\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\epsilon^{\prime}_{s-j}),\ \epsilon_{s+1-j}=1,\ \epsilon^{\prime}_{s+1-j}=0\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{d^{\prime}}}\gg_{D,s}A^{-O_{s}(D)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}N^{D},

where
d^{\prime}
is the degree of the box norms on the left-hand side. Ordering
\{0,1\}^{s}
lexicographically and identifying it with
[2^{s}]=[t]
in an order-preserving way, we obtain a subset
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}\subseteq[t]\times[t]
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t}\in I\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{H_{jk_{i_{1}}}-H_{jk_{i_{2}}}:\;j\in[s],\ ({i_{1}},{i_{2}})\in{\mathcal{K}}_{0}\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{d^{\prime}}}\gg_{D,s}A^{-O_{s}(D)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}N^{D}.

Note that
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}\subseteq\{(i_{1},i_{2})\in[t]\times[t]:i_{1}<i_{2}\}
. Our next goal is to replace
{\mathcal{K}}_{0}
by the full set
{\mathcal{K}}=\{(i_{1},i_{2})\in[t]\times[t]:\;i_{1}<i_{2}\}
.
By the assumption on the multisets
H_{ji}
, we have

[N]^{D}-(H_{jk_{1}}-H_{jk_{2}})\subseteq(-2AN,(2A+1)N]^{D},

and so

\mathopen{}\left|\supp([N]^{D}-(H_{jk_{1}}-H_{jk_{2}}))\mathclose{}\right|\leq(4A+1)^{D}N^{D}.

The corollary follows from this observation and repeated applications of Lemma
3.5
(
iii
), the monotonicity property for box norms.
∎

The next corollary (which was stated in Section
2
and which we will now restate for convenience) will let us take the directions in the concatenated box norms to be arbitrarily long sums of the original directions rather than just double sums (as in Corollary
6.3
).
See
2.1

It will be useful later that the indices
i_{1},\ldots,i_{\ell}
in the sum

\displaystyle H_{jk_{i_{1}}}+\cdots+H_{jk_{i_{\ell}}}

in (
16
) are all distinct. The assumption of the symmetry of the multisets
H_{ji}
(meaning that
H_{ji}=-H_{ji}
)) in the corollary is not necessary, and we include it only so that we do not have to keep track of the minus signs in the expressions
H_{jk_{i_{1}}}+\cdots+H_{jk_{i_{\ell}}}
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
\ell
, which we recall ranges over powers of 2. For
\ell=1
, the claim is trivial, and for
\ell=2
, the lower bound

(47)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t_{1}}\in I\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\{H_{jk_{i_{1}}}+H_{jk_{i_{2}}}:\ j\in[s],\ 0<i_{1}<i_{2}\leq t_{1}\}}^{2^{d_{1}}}\gg_{D,s}A^{-O_{s}(D)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}N^{D},

where
t_{1}=2^{s}
and
d_{1}={{t_{1}}\choose{2}}
is the degree of the box norms appearing, follows from Corollary
6.3
and the fact that the sets
H_{ji}
are symmetric. We will now prove the case
\ell=4
, and which will make the induction scheme for larger
\ell
clear.

To prove the result for
\ell=2
, we apply the case
\ell=1
to (
47
), taking
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{t_{1}})\in I^{t_{1}}
in place of
i\in I
. This gives

(48)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{il}\in I:\\
i\in[t_{1}],\;l\in[t_{1}^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{H_{jk_{i_{1}l_{1}}}+H_{jk_{i_{2}l_{1}}}+H_{jk_{i_{1}l_{2}}}+H_{jk_{i_{2}l_{2}}}:\\
j\in[s],\;0<i_{1}<i_{2}\leq t_{1},\;0<l_{1}<l_{2}\leq t_{1}^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{d_{1}^{\prime}}}\gg_{D,s}A^{-O_{s}(D)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}N^{D},

where
t_{1}^{\prime}=2^{d_{1}}
and
d_{1}^{\prime}
is the degree of the box norms appearing. Reindexing
(i,l)\in[t_{1}]\times[t_{1}^{\prime}]
as
i\in[t_{2}]
and using the monotonicity property and the assumption on
H_{ji}
, we argue as in the proof of Corollary
6.3
that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\ldots,k_{t_{2}}\in I\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\{H_{jk_{i_{1}}}+\cdots+H_{jk_{i_{4}}}:\ j\in[s],\ 0<i_{1}<\cdots<i_{4}\leq t_{2}\}}^{2^{d_{2}}}\gg_{D,s}A^{-O_{s}(D)}\delta^{O_{s}(1)}N^{D},

where
d_{2}
is the degree of the box norms appearing. We continue in the same manner for larger
\ell
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

One can phrase our results using the language of weights, as in
[
14
, Lemma 5.5]
. In fact, the appropriate group analogue of
[
14
, Lemma 5.5]
is an immediate consequence of our Proposition
6.2
, since one can approximate a weight to arbitrary accuracy as the uniform distribution on a suitable finite multiset and then take a limit.

7.
Equidistribution estimates for concatenation

The purpose of this section is to obtain upper bounds on the number of solutions to systems of multilinear equations that appear in connection with the manipulations in Section
8
.
These results will culminate in Proposition
7.5
, which is precisely the ingredient needed to complete the proof of Theorem
1.10
.

The starting point for our argument is the following simple lemma.

Lemma 7.1

(Bound on solutions to linear congruences)
.

Let
\ell\geq 2
, let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell}
be positive integers, and let
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then

\displaystyle\max_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-1}\in[\pm M]}\mathbf{1}_{h_{\ell}\mathbb{Z}+t}(h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-1}m_{\ell-1})\ll_{\ell}M^{\ell-1}\frac{\gcd(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}+M^{\ell-2}.

Proof.

First, we observe that if
(m^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{\ell-1})\in[\pm M]^{\ell-1}
is a solution to the congruence

\displaystyle h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-1}m_{\ell-1}\equiv t\pmod{h_{\ell}},

then every other solution in
[\pm M]^{\ell-1}
takes the form
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-1})=(m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,m^{\prime}_{\ell}+m^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})
, where
(m^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,m^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})
is a solution to the homogeneous congruence

\displaystyle h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-1}m_{\ell-1}\equiv 0\pmod{h_{\ell}}

and
m^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in[\pm M]-m^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq[\pm 2M]
.
Therefore, it suffices to prove the
t=0
case of the lemma (at the cost of changing the implicit constant in the conclusion of the lemma).

We proceed by induction on
\ell
. For the base case
\ell=2
, we have

h_{2}\,|\,h_{1}m_{1}\iff\frac{h_{2}}{\gcd(h_{1},h_{2})}\,\Big|\,m_{1},

and hence

\sum_{m_{1}\in[\pm M]}\mathbf{1}_{h_{2}\mathbb{Z}}(h_{1}m_{1})\ll M\frac{\gcd(h_{1},h_{2})}{h_{2}}+1

(where
+1
is the boundary term).

For the induction step, write

\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-1}\in[\pm M]}\mathbf{1}_{h_{\ell}\mathbb{Z}}(h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-1}m_{\ell-1})=\\
\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-2}\in[\pm M]}\sum_{m_{\ell-1}\in[\pm M]}\mathbf{1}_{h_{\ell}\mathbb{Z}-(h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-2}m_{\ell-2})}(h_{\ell-1}m_{\ell-1}).

The crucial observation is that the inner summand vanishes if
h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-2}m_{\ell-2}
is not a multiple of
g:=\gcd(h_{\ell-1},h_{\ell})
. Now suppose that
g
does divide
h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-2}m_{\ell-2}
; the induction hypothesis tells us that this occurs for at most

O_{\ell}\mathopen{}\left(M^{\ell-2}\frac{\gcd(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})}{g}+M^{\ell-3}\mathclose{}\right)

choices of
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-2}
, where we have used the identity

\gcd(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell-2},\gcd(h_{\ell-1},h_{\ell}))=\gcd(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell}).

For each such choice of
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-2}
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{h_{\ell}\mathbb{Z}-(h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-2}m_{\ell-2})}(h_{\ell-1}m_{\ell-1})=\mathbf{1}_{(h_{\ell}/g)\mathbb{Z}-(h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-2}m_{\ell-2})/g}((h_{\ell-1}/g)m_{\ell-1}).

Now the
\ell=2
case of the proposition tells us that the sum over
m_{\ell-1}
has size

O\mathopen{}\left(M\frac{\gcd(h_{\ell-1}/g,h_{\ell}/g)}{h_{\ell}/g}+1\mathclose{}\right)=O\mathopen{}\left(M\frac{g}{h_{\ell}}+1\mathclose{}\right)

since
\gcd(h_{\ell-1}/g,h_{\ell}/g)=1
by the definition of
g
. Combining these two observations and using
\gcd(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\leq g\leq h_{\ell}
, we bound

\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-1}\in[\pm M]}\mathbf{1}_{h_{\ell}\mathbb{Z}}(h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-1}m_{\ell-1})\ll_{\ell}M^{\ell-1}\frac{\gcd(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}+M^{\ell-2},

as desired.
∎

Corollary 7.2

(Bound on solutions to linear equations)
.

Let
\ell\geq 2
, let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell}
be positive integers, and let
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then

\displaystyle\max_{t\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\in[\pm M]}\mathbf{1}(h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell}m_{\ell}=t)\ll_{\ell}M^{\ell-1}\frac{\gcd(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}+M^{\ell-2}.

We will later apply this corollary with

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[\ell]}|h_{i}|\leq H\leq M,\quad\gcd(h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell})\leq\eta^{-1},\quad\textrm{and}\quad h_{\ell}\geq\eta H

(for small positive
\eta
), in which case the right-hand side is
\ll_{\ell}\eta^{-2}M^{\ell-1}H^{-1}
.

Proof.

For each choice of
m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-1}
, there is at most one value of
m_{\ell}
satisfying
h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell}m_{\ell}=t
, so we can bound

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell}\in[\pm M]}\mathbf{1}_{h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell}m_{\ell}=t}\leq\sum_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{\ell-1}\in[\pm M]}\mathbf{1}_{h_{\ell}{\mathbb{Z}}+t}(h_{1}m_{1}+\cdots+h_{\ell-1}m_{\ell-1});

here we have an inequality instead of an equality because on the right-hand side we have thrown out the constraint
m_{\ell}\in[\pm M]
. The result now follows from Lemma
7.1
.
∎

In order to state our more general results, we require a bit of notation. Define

(49)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{t,\ell,r}:=\{(k_{li})_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,i)\in[r]\times[\ell]\end{subarray}}\in[t]^{r\ell}:\;1\leq k_{l1}<\cdots<k_{l\ell}\leq t\;\;\textrm{for\; all}\;\;l\in[r]\}

to be the collection of admissible tuples of indices. For
l\in[r]
and
\eta>0
, also define

(50)

{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\eta}:=\{(h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}\in[t]}\in[\pm H]^{t^{l}}:\;|h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}-h_{lk^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots k^{\prime\prime}_{l}}|\geq\eta H,\\
\gcd(h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}-h_{lk^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots k^{\prime\prime}_{l}},h_{lk^{\prime}_{1}\cdots k^{\prime}_{l}}-h_{lk^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cdots k^{\prime\prime}_{l}})\leq\eta^{-1}\\
\textrm{for\; distinct}\;(k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}),\;(k^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{l}),\;(k^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime\prime}_{l})\in[t]^{l}\}

to be the set of tuples of
h
’s whose pairwise differences are not too small and whose greatest common divisors are not too big. The following lemma, which lets us establish anti-concentration results on many pairs of “scales” simultaneously, will turn out to be the key component of the proof of Proposition
7.5
below.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\eta>0
and
H,M,l,\ell,r,s,t\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfy
H\leq M
,
3\leq\ell\leq t
, and
l\leq r
. Let
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{t,\ell,r}
be as in (
49
) and
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{l,\eta}
be as in (
50
).
Then

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}}((h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}})\cdot\prod_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l-1}\in[\ell]}\;\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\;\prod_{u_{l+1},\ldots,u_{r}\in\{0,1\}}\\
\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{0},n_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}|m_{i_{l}}|\leq H^{u_{l+1}+\cdots+u_{r}}M:\\
i_{l}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{l}\in[\ell]}m_{i_{l}}=n_{0}\Bigr)\cdot\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{l}\in[\ell]}h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{li_{l}}}m_{i_{l}}=n_{1}\Bigr)\\
\ll_{l,\ell,r,s,t}\eta^{-2^{r-l+1}s\ell^{l-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}M^{-2^{r-l+1}s\ell^{l-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-(r-l+1)2^{r-l}s\ell^{l-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}.

Example 7.4
.

We will illustrate the content of this lemma and the method of proof for
r=2
,
l=1
,
t=4
,
\ell=3
,
s=1
before supplying the proof in the general case. Letting

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}=\{(1,2,3),\;(1,2,4),\;(1,3,4),\;(2,3,4)\}\quad\textrm{so\; that}\quad{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}\times{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}

and
|{\mathcal{K}}|=16
, and renaming
h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}
as simply
h_{k_{i}}
, we can express the conclusion of the lemma as

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}\in[\pm H]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\cdot\big(\prod_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}\;\prod_{u_{2}\in\{0,1\}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{0},n_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}}\\
\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}|m_{1}|,|m_{2}|,|m_{3}|\leq H^{u_{2}}M\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}=n_{0})\cdot\mathbf{1}(h_{k_{1}}m_{1}+h_{k_{2}}m_{2}+h_{k_{3}}m_{3}=n_{1})\big)^{4}\\
\ll\eta^{-64}M^{-64}H^{-64},

where the fourth power comes from the fact that we are splitting

\prod\limits_{(k_{1i_{1}},k_{2i_{2}})_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[3]}\in{\mathcal{K}}}=\prod\limits_{(k_{1i_{1}})_{i_{1}\in[3]}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}\;\prod\limits_{(k_{2i_{2}})_{i_{2}\in[3]}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}

and the terms in the product are independent of
k_{2i_{2}}
(so the product over
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
above corresponds to the product over
(k_{1i_{1}})_{i_{1}\in[3]}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
while the fourth power corresponds to the product over
(k_{2i_{2}})_{i_{2}\in[3]}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
).
Our normalized count can then be expanded as

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4}\in[\pm H]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\cdot\big(\prod_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}}\;\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{00},n_{01},n_{10},n_{11}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}}\\
\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}|m_{01}|,|m_{02}|,|m_{03}|\leq M,\\
|m_{11}|,|m_{12}|,|m_{13}|\leq HM\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}\begin{pmatrix}m_{01}+m_{02}+m_{03}=n_{00},\\
m_{11}+m_{12}+m_{13}=n_{01}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\mathbf{1}\begin{pmatrix}h_{k_{1}}m_{01}+h_{k_{2}}m_{02}+h_{k_{3}}m_{03}=n_{10},\\
h_{k_{1}}m_{11}+h_{k_{2}}m_{12}+h_{k_{3}}m_{13}=n_{11}\end{pmatrix}\big)^{4}.

Thus, for each
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
, we are left with bounding the number of solutions to the system

\displaystyle m_{01}+m_{02}+m_{03}=n_{00}\quad\textrm{and}\quad h_{k_{1}}m_{01}+h_{k_{2}}m_{02}+h_{k_{3}}m_{03}=n_{10},

\displaystyle m_{11}+m_{12}+m_{13}=n_{01}\quad\textrm{and}\quad h_{k_{1}}m_{11}+h_{k_{2}}m_{12}+h_{k_{3}}m_{13}=n_{11}.

Using the equations on the left to solve for
m_{03}
and
m_{13}
and substituting the resulting expressions into the equations on the right reduces the system to

\displaystyle(h_{k_{1}}-h_{k_{3}})m_{01}+(h_{k_{2}}-h_{k_{3}})m_{02}

\displaystyle=n_{0}

\displaystyle(h_{k_{1}}-h_{k_{3}})m_{11}+(h_{k_{2}}-h_{k_{3}})m_{22}

\displaystyle=n_{1}

for
n_{0}=n_{10}-h_{k_{3}}n_{00}
and
n_{1}=n_{11}-h_{k_{3}}n_{01}
, and each of these two equations can then be handled separately using Corollary
7.2
and the assumptions on
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.

The exponent
64
comes from the fact that the numbers
n_{00},n_{01},n_{10},n_{11}
lie in the intervals

\displaystyle n_{00}\in[\pm 3M],\quad n_{01},n_{10}\in[\pm 3HM],\quad n_{11}\in[\pm 3H^{2}M]

and hence there are
\asymp H^{4}M^{4}
possibilities for the quadruple
(n_{00},n_{01},n_{10},n_{11})
. Because of the assumption on
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4})
, we can use Corollary
7.2
to ensure that the quadruple
(n_{00},n_{01},n_{10},n_{11})
is roughly equidistributed in the appropriate box as the
m
’s range; in particular, the probability of hitting each individual quadruple
(n_{00},n_{01},n_{10},n_{11})
is

O((M^{-2}\eta^{-1}/(\eta H))\cdot((HM)^{-2}\eta^{-1}/(\eta H)))=O(\eta^{-4}H^{-4}M^{-4}).

Raising this quantity to the power
4
(because
|{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}|=4
), we see that the expression in parentheses above is
O(\eta^{-16}H^{-16}M^{-16})
, and raising this to the power
4
gives the desired exponent
64
.

Proof of Lemma
7.3
.

We let all implicit constants depend on
l,\ell,r,s,t
.
Using the equation
\sum\limits_{i_{l}\in[\ell]}{m}_{i_{l}}=n_{0}
we can substitute
m_{\ell}=n_{0}-\sum\limits_{i_{l}\in[\ell-1]}{m}_{i_{l}}
into the equation

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{l}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{li_{l}}}{m}_{i_{l}}=n_{1},

so that our normalized count is bounded by

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}}((h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}})\cdot\prod_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l-1}\in[\ell]}\;\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\;\prod_{u_{l+1},\ldots,u_{r}\in\{0,1\}}H^{-(u_{l+1}+\cdots+u_{r})}M^{-1}\\
\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}|m_{i_{l}}|\leq H^{u_{l+1}+\cdots+u_{r}}M:\\
i_{l}\in[\ell-1]\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{l}\in[\ell-1]}(h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{li_{l}}}-h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(l-1)i_{l-1}}k_{l\ell}})m_{i_{l}}=n\Bigr),

where we have set
n=n_{1}-n_{0}h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(l-1)i_{l-1}}k_{l\ell}}
. By Corollary
7.2
, the inner max is in turn bounded by

H^{-(u_{l+1}+\cdots+u_{r})}M^{-1}\frac{\gcd(h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(l-1)i_{l-1}}k_{li_{l}}}-h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(l-1)i_{l-1}}k_{l\ell}}:\;i_{l}\in[\ell-1])}{\max(h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(l-1)i_{l-1}}k_{li_{l}}}-h_{lk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(l-1)i_{l-1}}k_{l\ell}}:\;i_{l}\in[\ell-1])}

as long as
\ell\geq 3
and
H\leq M
. Due to our assumption on the set
{\mathcal{H}}
, the
\gcd
in the numerator is at most
\eta^{-1}
and the maximum in the denominator is at least
\eta H
, so we can bound our normalized count by

\displaystyle\ll\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{l-1}\in[\ell]}\;\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\;\prod_{u_{l+1},\ldots,u_{r}\in\{0,1\}}\eta^{-2}M^{-2}H^{-1-2(u_{l+1}+\cdots+u_{r})}

\displaystyle\ll\eta^{-2^{r-l+1}s\ell^{l-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}M^{-2^{r-l+1}s\ell^{l-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-(r-l+1)2^{r-l}s\ell^{l-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}.

∎

The next result, in which we we establish simultaneous anti-concentration for expressions on many scales, is the main estimate of this section. It gives an upper bound on the number of solutions to the systems of multilinear equations that naturally appear while concatenating box norms in the proof of Theorem
1.10
.

Proposition 7.5

(Solutions to systems of multilinear equations)
.

Let
\eta>0
, let
\ell,r,s,t\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
3\leq\ell\leq t
, and let
H,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
H\leq M
. Let
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{t,\ell,r}
be as in (
49
), and for each
l\in[r]
, let
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}:={\mathcal{H}}_{l,\eta}
be as in (
50
). Then

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\;j\in[s],\\
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in[t],\;l\in[r]\end{subarray}}\;\Bigl(\prod_{l\in[r]}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{l}}((h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}\in[t]})\Bigr)\\
\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}}^{u_{r}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)\\
\ll_{\ell,r,s,t}\eta^{-O_{\ell,r,s,t}(1)}M^{-2^{r}s|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-r2^{r-1}s|{\mathcal{K}}|}.

(Here, the superscript
u
’s are exponents, not indices.)

On a first read, the reader may wish to consider the case
t=\ell
, so that
{\mathcal{K}}
consists of only one element,
k_{li_{l}}
can be replaced by
k_{l}
, and the product
\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}
disappears.

In the applications of Proposition
7.5
to Theorem
1.10
,
s
is fixed and
\ell
(the number of terms in each equation) can be taken as large as we want, whereas we have no control over
t
, which grows with
\ell
and will typically be much larger than
\ell
.

Before proving Proposition
7.5
, we illustrate the statement with several examples.

Example 7.6
.

If
s=1
, i.e., we are using Proposition
7.5
to concatenate an average of degree-
1
box norms, then we will always have
t=\ell
, whence
|{\mathcal{K}}|=1
. Suppose that
t=\ell=3
and
r=2
, i.e., we are dealing with a system of equations coming from concatenating an average

\displaystyle\E_{h_{1},h_{2}\in[\pm H]}\left\|f\right\|_{({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{00}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{10}h_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{01}h_{2}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{11}h_{1}h_{2})\cdot[\pm M]}^{2}.

The resulting system consists of equations of the form

h_{11}^{u_{1}}(h_{211}^{u_{2}}m_{11}+h_{212}^{u_{2}}m_{12}+h_{213}^{u_{2}}m_{13})+h_{12}^{u_{1}}(h_{221}^{u_{2}}m_{21}+h_{222}^{u_{2}}m_{22}+h_{223}^{u_{2}}m_{23})\\
+h_{13}^{u_{1}}(h_{231}^{u_{2}}m_{31}+h_{232}^{u_{2}}m_{32}+h_{233}^{u_{2}}m_{33})={n_{u_{1}u_{2}}}

for
(u_{1},u_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
. Since
n_{u_{1}u_{2}}
lies in
[\pm 9H^{u_{1}+u_{2}}M]
, we expect to get a cancellation on the order of

\displaystyle\ll\prod_{u_{1},u_{2}\in\{0,1\}}H^{-(u_{1}+u_{2})}M^{-1}=H^{-4}M^{-4},

and modulo the powers of
\eta
, this is indeed what Proposition
7.5
gives.

Example 7.7
.

Suppose now that
r=1
,
s=4
,
t=6
, and
\ell=4
, so that
|{\mathcal{K}}|=15
. Then we have a system of
30
equations, which we can group into
15
pairs of equations

\displaystyle m_{{\underline{k}}1}+m_{{\underline{k}}2}+m_{{\underline{k}}3}+m_{{\underline{k}}4}=n_{{\underline{k}}0}\quad\textrm{and}\quad m_{{\underline{k}}1}h_{k_{1}}+m_{{\underline{k}}2}h_{k_{2}}+m_{{\underline{k}}3}h_{k_{3}}+m_{{\underline{k}}4}h_{k_{4}}=n_{{\underline{k}}1}

corresponding to the
15
tuples

{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}=\{(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}):\;1\leq k_{1}<k_{2}<k_{3}<k_{4}\leq 6\}.

Each
n_{{\underline{k}}0}
,
n_{{\underline{k}}1}
gives cancellation of order
\asymp M^{-1},\asymp H^{-1}M^{-1}
(respectively), so the total cancellation will be of order
\asymp H^{-15}M^{-30}
(modulo powers of
\eta
, which we ignore here for the simplicity of exposition). We remark that this example does not figure in the proof of Theorem
1.10
(but nonetheless is covered by Proposition
7.5
).

Let us see how the heuristics from the preceding two examples extend to the general case.
For each
(j,{\underline{k}},{\underline{u}})
, we expect the value
n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}
to be distributed fairly uniformly in an interval
[\pm C_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}H^{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}}M]

for some
C_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}>0
; the key uniform distribution property follows from Corollary
7.2
and the fact that the tuples
(h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}}
lie in the sets
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}
.
So for each
(j,{\underline{k}},{\underline{u}})
we get a cancellation of the order of

(51)

\displaystyle\asymp_{r}(H^{-(u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r})}M^{-1}),

which is (up to constants) the probability that a uniformly random element of the interval
[\pm C_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}H^{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}}M]
assumes any given value. The powers of
\eta
in the bound come from restricting the variables
h_{l}
to the admissible ranges
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}
, and using Corollary
7.2
to bound solutions for
h
’s in this range. The final bound then follows upon taking the product of (
51
) over all
j\in[s],{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, and
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
.

Proof of Proposition
7.5
.

We let all implicit constants depend on
\ell,r,s,t
. The proof goes by induction on
r
. The
r=1
case is the content of Lemma
7.3
. The induction step is in general quite notationally complicated, so, to make it more palatable, we first present the case
r=2
.

The case
r=2
.

In this case, our claimed bound takes the form

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}00},\;n_{j{\underline{k}}01},\\
n_{j{\underline{k}}10},\;n_{j{\underline{k}}11}\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}((h_{1k_{1}})_{k_{1}})\cdot\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}((h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{1},k_{2}})\\
\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{2}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}^{u_{2}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)\\
\ll\eta^{-O(1)}M^{-4s|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-4s|{\mathcal{K}}|}.

For each
(j,{\underline{k}})\in[s]\times{\mathcal{K}}
, we have the system of four equations

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}

\displaystyle=n_{j{\underline{k}}00}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}

\displaystyle=n_{j{\underline{k}}10}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}

\displaystyle=n_{j{\underline{k}}01}

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}

\displaystyle=n_{j{\underline{k}}11}.

This system is quadratic in the
h
variables, and our goal is to decouple it into two separate systems that are linear in
h
. We accomplish this by introducing two dummy variables

\displaystyle t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}=\sum_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}=\sum_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}

and renaming
t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}i_{2}}
. Let us mention the motivation for choosing these variables. The above system of four equations has three different scales: The first is at scale
\asymp M
, the second and third are at scale
\asymp HM
, and the fourth is at scale
\asymp H^{2}M
. In order to apply the two-scale result from Lemma
7.3
, we want to think of the fourth equation as having scale
\asymp H(HM)
, where the
HM
here “matches” the scale of the third equation. This is precisely the role played by the second set of dummy variables, which is at scale
\asymp HM
. The first set of dummy variables, at scale
\asymp M
, pertains to the first and second equations, and the two sets of dummy variables are chosen in such a way that they are related at consecutive scales as in Lemma
7.3
.

Thus, solving the original system is equivalent to solving

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}=n_{j{\underline{k}}00}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sum_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}=n_{j{\underline{k}}10}

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}=n_{j{\underline{k}}01}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sum_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}=n_{j{\underline{k}}11},

together with

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\sum_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{i_{1}}k_{i_{2}}}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}\quad\textrm{for\; each}\quad i_{1}\in[\ell],

where the variables
t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}
and
t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}
have ranges
[\pm\ell M]
and
[\pm\ell HM]
, respectively. Converting the expectation over the
m
’s to a sum, we see that our normalized count is bounded by
O(M^{-s\ell^{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|})
times

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}00},\;n_{j{\underline{k}}01},\\
n_{j{\underline{k}}10},\;n_{j{\underline{k}}11}\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}((h_{1k_{1}})_{k_{1}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}\in[\pm\ell M],\\
t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}\in[\pm\ell HM]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Big(\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{2}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}u_{2}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Big(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}((h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{1},k_{2}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\prod_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{2}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}^{u_{2}}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}u_{2}}\Bigr)\Big);

we will deal first with the system involving the variables
h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}
and then with the system involving the variables
h_{1k_{1}}
. Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\prod_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{2}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}^{u_{2}}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}u_{2}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad=\prod_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}\in[\pm M]:\\
i_{2}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}\Bigr)\cdot\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}\Bigr).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}((h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{1},k_{2}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}\in[\pm M],\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\prod_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{2}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}^{u_{2}}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}u_{2}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}((h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{1},k_{2}})\prod_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}\in[\pm M]:\\
i_{2}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}\Bigr)\cdot\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}\Bigr).

Lemma
7.3
applied with
l=r=2
allows us to bound this expression by

O(\eta^{-O(1)}M^{s\ell^{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|-2s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|}).

Similarly, the count of solutions to the system involving the variables
h_{1k_{1}}
is

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}00},\;n_{j{\underline{k}}01},\\
n_{j{\underline{k}}10},\;n_{j{\underline{k}}11}\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}((h_{1k_{1}})_{k_{1}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}0}\in[\pm\ell M],\\
t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}1}\in[\pm\ell HM]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{2}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}u_{2}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}((h_{1k_{1}})_{k_{1}})\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{2}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\max_{n_{0},n_{1}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}u_{2}}\in[\pm\ell H^{u_{2}}M]:\\
i_{1}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}u_{2}}=n_{0}\Bigr)\cdot\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}u_{2}}=n_{1}\Bigr),

and this can be bounded by

O(\eta^{-O(1)}M^{2s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|-4s|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|-4s|{\mathcal{K}}|})

using the case
l=1,r=2
of Lemma
7.3
. Note that the big centered equations had an
inequality
rather than an
equality
because we interchanged the
\max
with the sums and products.

Combining these two bounds and the normalising factor
O(M^{-s\ell^{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|})
, we bound the overall normalised count by

\displaystyle\ll M^{-s\ell^{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|}\cdot\mathopen{}\left(\eta^{-O(1)}M^{s\ell^{2}|{\mathcal{K}}|-2s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|}\mathclose{}\right)\cdot\mathopen{}\left(\eta^{-O(1)}M^{2s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|-4s|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|-4s|{\mathcal{K}}|}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle=\eta^{-O(1)}M^{-4s|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-4s|{\mathcal{K}}|},

as claimed.

The case
r>2
.

We handle the case
r>2
similarly. For
l\in[r]
,
i_{1},\ldots,i_{l}\in[\ell]
,
j\in[s]
,
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
,
{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, we define the auxiliary variable

\displaystyle t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{l}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=\sum\limits_{i_{l+1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[\ell]}h_{(l+1)k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(l+1)i_{l+1}}}^{u_{l+1}}\cdots h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}}^{u_{r}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}

at scale
H^{u_{l+1}+\cdots+u_{r}}M
.
Notice that
t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{l}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}
depends on only the coordinates
u_{l+1},\ldots,u_{r}
of
{\underline{u}}
and that the
t
’s satisfy the relation

(52)

\displaystyle t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{l}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=\sum_{i_{l+1}\in[\ell]}h_{(l+1)k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(l+1)i_{l+1}}}^{u_{l+1}}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{l+1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}.

Iterating (
52
) through intermediate scales, we see that for each
j\in[s]
and
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\in[\pm M]:\\
i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}}^{u_{r}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}|\ll H^{u_{2}+\cdots+u_{r}}M:\\
i_{1}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}|\ll H^{u_{3}+\cdots+u_{r}}M:\\
i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}^{u_{2}}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\cdots\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}|\leq M:\\
i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r-1}\in[\ell]}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{r}\in[\ell]}h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}}^{u_{r}}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=t_{i_{1}\ldots i_{r-1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr),

where we note that
t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}
. Hence our normalized count is bounded by
O(M^{-s\ell^{r}|{\mathcal{K}}|})
times

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\;j\in[s],\\
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}((h_{1k_{1}})_{k_{1}})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}|\ll H^{u_{2}+\cdots+u_{r}}M:\;j\in[s],\\
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;u_{2},\ldots,u_{r}\in\{0,1\},\;i_{1}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Big(\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},k_{2}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{2}}((h_{2k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{1},k_{2}})\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}|\ll H^{u_{3}+\cdots+u_{r}}M:\;j\in[s],\\
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;u_{3},\ldots,u_{r}\in\{0,1\},\;i_{1},i_{2}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Big(\prod_{i_{1}\in[\ell]}\;\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{2},\ldots,u_{r}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{2}\in[\ell]}h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}}^{u_{2}}t_{i_{1}i_{2}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=t_{i_{1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\cdots\times\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{rk_{1}\cdots k_{r}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{r}}((h_{rk_{1}\cdots k_{r}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}})\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}|\leq M:\;j\in[s],\\
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\prod_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r-1}\in[\ell]}\;\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{r}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{r}\in[\ell]}h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}}^{u_{r}}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr).

As in the case
r=2
, we have decoupled our original system of degree-
r
multilinear equations into
r
smaller systems, each consisting of a pair of multilinear equations at consecutive scales. We will next use Lemma
7.3
to bound the counts of solutions for these subsystems individually, starting from the system involving the variables
h_{rk_{1}\cdots k_{r}}
. The count of solutions to this system can be rephrased as

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{rk_{1}\cdots k_{r}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{r}}((h_{rk_{1}\cdots k_{r}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}})\prod_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r-1}\in[\ell]}\;\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}|\leq M:\\
i_{r}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\ \prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{r}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{r}\in[\ell]}h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}}^{u_{r}}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr),

and bounded by
\ll\eta^{-O(1)}M^{s\ell^{r}|{\mathcal{K}}|-2s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}
using the case
l=r
of Lemma
7.3
. Similarly, the count corresponding to the system involving the variables
h_{(r-1)k_{1}\cdots k_{r-1}}
can be rephrased as

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{(r-1)k_{1}\cdots k_{r-1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r-1}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{r-1}}((h_{(r-1)k_{1}\cdots k_{r-1}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r-1}})\prod_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r-2}\in[\ell]}\;\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{r}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}|\ll H^{u_{r}}M:\\
i_{r-1}\in[\ell]\end{subarray}}\ \prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{r-1}\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\Bigl(\sum\limits_{i_{r-1}\in[\ell]}h_{(r-1)k_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{(r-1)i_{r-1}}}^{u_{r-1}}t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-1}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{r-2}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\Bigr)

and bounded by

\displaystyle\ll\eta^{-O(1)}M^{2s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|-s\ell^{r}|{\mathcal{K}}|-4s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|-4s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}

with the help of the case
l=r-1
of Lemma
7.3
. Continuing in this manner and combining the results, we can bound our initial normalized count by

\displaystyle\ll M^{-s\ell^{r}|{\mathcal{K}}|}\cdot\prod_{l\in[r]}\eta^{-O(1)}M^{2^{r-l}s\ell^{l}|{\mathcal{K}}|-2^{r-l+1}s\ell^{l-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad H^{(r-l)2^{r-l-1}s\ell^{l}|{\mathcal{K}}|-(r-l+1)2^{r-l}s\ell^{l-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|},

where in each
l
-multiplicand, the negative exponents of
\eta,M,H
come from an application of Lemma
7.3
to bound the count of solutions to the system involving the variables
h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}
and the positive exponents come from counting the admissible values of the variables
t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{l}j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}
. The telescoping nature of the exponents leads to cancellations: The exponents of
M
and
H
cancel out as

\displaystyle-s\ell^{r}|{\mathcal{K}}|+(s\ell^{r}|{\mathcal{K}}|-2s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|)+\cdots+(2^{r-1}s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|-2^{r}s|{\mathcal{K}}|)=-2^{r}s|{\mathcal{K}}|,

\displaystyle-s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|+(s\ell^{r-1}|{\mathcal{K}}|-4s\ell^{r-2}|{\mathcal{K}}|)+\cdots+((r-1)2^{r-2}s\ell|{\mathcal{K}}|-r2^{r-1}s|{\mathcal{K}}|)=-r2^{r-1}s|{\mathcal{K}}|,

respectively, yielding the desired final bound

\displaystyle\ll\eta^{-O(1)}M^{-2^{r}s|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-r2^{r-1}s|{\mathcal{K}}|}.

∎

The last result in this section ensures that the set
{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\eta}
fills almost all of
[\pm H]^{t^{l}}
when
\eta>0
is small.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
\eta>0
,
H,l,t\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{l,\eta}
be as in (
50
). Then all but an
O_{l,t}(\eta)
-proportion of the elements of
[\pm H]^{t^{l}}
lie in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof.

Setting
k=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{l})
and
h_{k}=h_{lk_{1},\ldots,k_{l}}
, we can rewrite

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}=\{(h_{k})_{k\in[t^{l}]}\in[\pm H]^{t^{l}}:\;

\displaystyle\gcd(h_{k}-h_{k^{\prime\prime}},h_{k^{\prime}}-h_{k^{\prime\prime}})\leq\eta^{-1},

\displaystyle|h_{k}|\geq\eta H\;\textrm{for all distinct}\;k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}\in[t^{l}]\}.

We will show that there are very few elements of
[\pm H]^{t^{l}}
failing each condition for membership in
{\mathcal{H}}
, and then we will use the union bound to conclude that
[\pm H]^{t^{l}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}
is small. For the gcd condition, we estimate

\displaystyle\E_{h,h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}\in[\pm H]}\mathbf{1}\mathopen{}\left(\gcd(h-h^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime}-h^{\prime\prime})\geq\eta^{-1}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\E_{h,h^{\prime}\in[\pm 2H]}\mathbf{1}\mathopen{}\left(\gcd(h,h^{\prime})\geq\eta^{-1}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{H^{2}}\sum_{g=\eta^{-1}}^{2H}\sum_{h,h^{\prime}\in[\pm 2H]}\mathbf{1}\mathopen{}\left(g|h,h^{\prime}\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{H^{2}}\sum_{g=\eta^{-1}}^{2H}(H/g)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{g=\eta^{-1}}^{2H}\frac{1}{g^{2}}\ll\eta.

For the size condition, we note that
|h|<\eta H
for an
O(\eta)
-fraction of
h\in[\pm H]
. The conclusion of the lemma now follows from the union bound.
∎

8.
Concatenation along polynomials

In this section, we combine the conclusions of the previous two sections in order to complete the proof of Theorem
1.10
. First, we specialize the results of Section
6
to averages of polynomial box norms.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
D,d,r,s\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
d\leq r
, and let
\ell
be a power of
2
. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{\ell,r,s}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{D,\ell,r,s}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
\delta>0
and
H,M,N,V\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfy
H^{d}MV\leq N
, and let
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[{\underline{h}}]
be polynomials of degree at most
d
with coefficients of size at most
V
. Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

(53)

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M],\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[r],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}=1}^{\ell}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}})\cdot[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{w}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D},

where
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{t,\ell,r}
and
w
is the degree of the box norms appearing.

Proof.

The proof consists of several applications of Corollary
2.1
. First, we apply it with the indexing set
{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}
to obtain a natural number
t_{1}=O_{\ell,s}(1)
such that

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}_{1},\ldots,{\underline{h}}_{t_{1}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}}_{k_{11}})\cdot[\pm M]+\cdots+{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}}_{k_{1\ell}})\cdot[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;1\leq k_{11}<\cdots<k_{1\ell}\leq t_{1}\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{w_{1}}}\gg_{D,\ell,s}\delta^{O_{\ell,s}(1)}N^{D},

where
w_{1}
is the degree of the box norms appearing.

For the second application of Corollary
2.1
, we write
{\underline{h}}_{k}=(h_{1k},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k})
and take the indexing set to be the set of tuples
(\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{11},\ldots,\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1t_{1}})\in[\pm H]^{(r-1)t_{1}}
. Then Corollary
2.1
, applied separately for each fixed choice of
h_{11},\ldots,h_{1t_{1}}
, gives
t_{2}=O_{\ell,s}(1)
for which

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]^{r-1}:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}],\;k_{2}\in[t_{2}]\end{subarray}}\;\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{11}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{11}k_{21}})\cdot[\pm M]+\cdots+{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{11}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{11}k_{2\ell}})\cdot[\pm M]\\
+\cdots+{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1\ell}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{1\ell}k_{21}})\cdot[\pm M]+\cdots+{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1\ell}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{1\ell}k_{2\ell}})\cdot[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;1\leq k_{11}<\cdots<k_{1\ell}\leq t_{1},\;1\leq k_{21}<\cdots<k_{2\ell}\leq t_{2}\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{w_{2}}}\\
\gg_{D,\ell,s}\delta^{O_{\ell,s}(1)}N^{D},

where
w_{2}
is the degree of the box norms appearing.

For the next step, we similarly write
\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{1k_{1}k_{2}}=(h_{2k_{1}k_{2}},\tilde{h}_{2k_{1}k_{2}})
and take our indices to be the tuples
(\tilde{h}_{2k_{1}k_{2}})_{k_{1}\in[t_{1}],k_{2}\in[t_{2}]}\in[\pm H]^{(r-2)t_{1}t_{2}}
. Applying Corollary
2.1
separately for each
h_{1k_{1}},h_{2k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]
, with
k_{1}\in[t_{1}],k_{2}\in[t_{2}]
, we obtain
t_{3}=O_{\ell,s}(1)
for which

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{1k_{1}k_{2}}\in[\pm H]:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}],\;k_{2}\in[t_{2}]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{2k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\in[\pm H]^{r-2}:\\
k_{1}\in[t_{1}],\;k_{2}\in[t_{2}],\;k_{3}\in[t_{3}]\end{subarray}}\\
\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}=1}^{\ell}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},h_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}},\tilde{{\underline{h}}}_{2k_{1i_{1}}k_{2i_{2}}k_{3i_{3}}})\cdot[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;1\leq k_{l1}<\cdots<k_{l\ell}\leq t_{l},\;l\in[3]\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{w_{3}}}\\
\gg_{D,\ell,s}\delta^{O_{\ell,s}(1)}N^{D},

where
w_{3}
is the degree of the box norms appearing. Continuing in this fashion
r-2
more times, we obtain
t_{3},\ldots,t_{r}=O_{\ell,r,s}(1)
such that

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[r],\;k_{l}\in[t_{l}]\end{subarray}}\;\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}=1}^{\ell}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}})\cdot[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;1\leq k_{l1}<\cdots<k_{l\ell}\leq t_{l},\;l\in[r]\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{w_{r}}}\gg_{D,\ell,r,s}\delta^{O_{\ell,r,s}(1)}N^{D},

where
w_{r}
is the degree of the box norms appearing.

Although this inequality is just as useful as the conclusion of the proposition, it will later be notationally convenient to replace the
t_{l}
’s with a single parameter. Setting
t=\max(t_{1},\ldots,t_{r})
and noting that the coordinates of the polynomial

\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}=1}^{\ell}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}})

are at most
O_{d,r,\ell}(VH^{d})
for all valid choices of the
h
’s, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}=1}^{\ell}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}})\cdot[\pm M]\subset[-AN,AN]^{D}

for some
A=O_{d,\ell,r,s}(1)
; it is here that we are using the assumption on the coefficients of
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}
and the bound
H^{d}MV\leq N
. We then deduce the result from the monotonicity property of box norms (Lemma
3.5
(
iii
)).
∎

We will finally use Proposition
8.1
, together with the equidistribution results from Section
7
, to prove Theorem
1.10
, which we restate for convenience.

Theorem

(Theorem
1.10
)
.

Let
d,r,s,D\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
d\leq r
. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{r,s}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{r,s,D}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
H,M,N,V\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\delta>0
satisfy

\displaystyle C^{-1}\delta^{-C}\leq H\leq M\quad\textrm{and}\quad H^{d}MV\leq N,

and let
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}]
be multilinear polynomials
{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum\limits_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j{\underline{u}}}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}
of degree at most
d
and with coefficients at most
V
. Then for all
1
-bounded functions
f:{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]^{D}
, the bound

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\left\|f\right\|_{{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M],\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]}^{2^{s}}\geq\delta N^{D}

implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{1}^{t},\ldots,E_{s}^{t}}^{2^{st}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N^{D},

where

\displaystyle E_{j}=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{d}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j{\underline{u}}}\cdot[\pm H^{|{\underline{u}}|+1}].

Proof.

The proof amounts to combining the conclusions of Propositions
8.1
and
7.5
. We will apply these results with
\ell=4
since we need
\ell
to be a power of 2 (as required by Proposition
8.1
), and we want it to be at least 3 (in order to apply Proposition
7.5
). We let all implicit constants depend on
D,r,s
20
20

20

and also on
d
, which, however, is bounded by
r
and note that the powers of
\delta
appearing in the proof will be independent of
D
. We also let
{\mathcal{K}}={\mathcal{K}}_{t,4,r}
, which we recall from Section
7
is the set of admissible tuples of indices

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}=\{(k_{li})_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,i)\in[r]\times[4]\end{subarray}}\in[t]^{4r}:\;1\leq k_{l1}<\cdots<k_{l4}\leq t\;\;\textrm{for\; all}\;\;l\in[r]\}.

By Proposition
8.1
, there is some positive integer
t=O(1)
(independent of
D
) for which

(54)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[r],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\left\|f\right\|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[\ell]}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}})\cdot[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}\end{subarray}}^{2^{w}}\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{D},

where
w
is the degree of the box norms appearing. We will now make use of our equidistribution estimates. It is necessary first to restrict to “generic” tuples of the
h
’s.

Recall from (
50
) the definition of the set of tuples
{\mathcal{H}}_{l,\eta}\subset[\pm H]^{t^{l}}
, which by Lemma
7.8
contains all but an
O(\eta)
-proportion of the elements of
[\pm H]^{t^{l}}
. Write
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}={\mathcal{H}}_{l,\eta}
and take
\eta=c\delta^{1/c}
for some
c>0
sufficiently small that the contribution of tuples
(h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}}\in[\pm H]^{l^{t}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}_{l}
is at most half (say) of the lower bound in (
54
). Removing such “bad” tuples of
h
’s and expanding (
54
), we obtain

(55)

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[r],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{l\in[r]}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{l}}((h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}})\mathclose{}\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\mu_{M}((m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}})_{j,{\underline{k}},i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}})\\
\sum_{x}\Delta_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}{\mathbf{C}}_{j}(h_{1k_{1i_{1}}},\ldots,h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}})m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}\end{subarray}}\;f(x)\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{D}.

For each
j\in[s]
,
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}
,
{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, we introduce dummy variables

(56)

\displaystyle n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}=\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}}^{u_{r}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}

at scale
H^{u_{1}+\cdots+u_{r}}M
, and we define

{\mathcal{N}}({\underline{n}})=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}\in[\pm M]:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]\end{subarray}}\;\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\in[\pm H]:\\
l\in[r],\;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in[t]\end{subarray}}\;\mathopen{}\left(\prod_{l\in[r]}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{l}}((h_{lk_{1}\cdots k_{l}})_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{l}})\mathclose{}\right)\\
\prod_{j\in[s]}\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}\mathopen{}\left(\sum\limits_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\in[4]}h_{1k_{1i_{1}}}^{u_{1}}\cdots h_{rk_{1i_{1}}\cdots k_{ri_{r}}}^{u_{r}}m_{j{\underline{k}}i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}=n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\mathclose{}\right).

In words, the kernel
{\mathcal{N}}({\underline{n}})
is the probability that (
56
) is satisfied when the
m
’s are chosen (independently, uniformly) at random from
[\pm M]
and the tuples of
h
