’s are chosen (independently, uniformly) at random from the sets
{\mathcal{H}}_{l}
.
Inserting the definition of
{\mathcal{N}}({\underline{n}})
into (
55
) and applying the triangle inequality gives us the neat bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\ll H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{N}}({\underline{n}})\cdot\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\Delta_{\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\end{subarray}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j{\underline{u}}}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}:\;j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}}f({\mathbf{x}})\mathclose{}\right|\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{D}.

The kernel
{\mathcal{N}}
is supported on the tuples

(n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}})_{j,{\underline{k}},{\underline{u}}}
satisfying
n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\ll H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M
, of which there are

\displaystyle\ll\prod_{j\in[s]}\prod_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}}\prod_{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M\ll M^{2^{r}s|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{r2^{r-1}s|{\mathcal{K}}|}.

It is at this point that we crucially incorporate the bound

(57)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}({\underline{n}})\ll\delta^{-O(1)}M^{-2^{r}s|{\mathcal{K}}|}H^{-r2^{r-1}s|{\mathcal{K}}|}

provided by Proposition
7.5
to get the estimate

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}\ll H^{|{\underline{u}}|}M:\\
j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}},\;{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{\mathbf{x}}\Delta_{\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}\end{subarray}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j{\underline{u}}}n_{j{\underline{k}}{\underline{u}}}:\;j\in[s],\;{\underline{k}}\in{\mathcal{K}}\}}f({\mathbf{x}})\mathclose{}\right|\gg\delta^{O(1)}N^{D}.

From here, we use the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality to remove the absolute value and introduce Fejér kernels, and then use Lemma
3.5
(
vi
) to trim the boxes (as in the end of the proof sketch of Proposition
5.1
). To conclude the proof, we use Lemma
3.5
to replace the differencing with respect to
[\pm N]^{D}
by a differencing with respect to one of the boxes
E_{j}
; by the monotonicity of box norms, we can also increase the multiplicity of every other
E_{i}
by
1
, and we conclude the theorem with
t+1
in place of
t
.
∎

9.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

We conclude by deriving Theorem
1.6
from Proposition
4.9
(the output of PET) and Theorem
1.10
(the concatenation result).
Assume that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left|\sum_{{\mathbf{x}}}\E_{z\in[K]}f_{0}({\mathbf{x}})\cdot f_{1}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{1}(z))\cdots f_{\ell}({\mathbf{x}}+{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}(z))\mathclose{}\right|\geq\delta N^{D}.

Proposition
4.9
gives us
r,s=O_{d,\ell}(1)
and nonzero
r
-variable multilinear polynomials
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{C}}_{s}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{D}[{\underline{h}}]
(depending only on
{\mathbf{P}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{P}}_{\ell}
) of degree at most
d-1
, such that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-1\end{subarray}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}){\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}

for some indices
w_{j{\underline{u}}}\in[0,\ell]
(with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}:=\mathbf{0}
), for which

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r}}\left\|f_{1}\right\|_{{{\mathbf{C}}_{1}({\underline{h}})}\cdot[\pm M],\ldots,{{\mathbf{C}}_{s}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm M]}}^{2^{s}}\gg_{d,D,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}N^{D}

holds for all
\delta^{-O_{d,\ell}(1)}\ll_{d,D,\ell}H\leq M\ll_{d,D,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}K
.
Furthermore, by property (
iii
) of Definition
4.5
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}(|{\underline{u}}|+1)}
is the leading coefficient of
{\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}}
, which implies that

\displaystyle\deg({\mathbf{P}}_{1}-{\mathbf{P}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}})=|{\underline{u}}|+1.

By Theorem
1.10
(with
d-1
in place of
d
), there exists
t_{0}=O_{d,\ell}(1)
such that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{E_{10}^{t_{0}},\ldots,E_{s0}^{t_{0}}}^{2^{st_{0}}}\gg_{d,D,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}N^{D}

for the boxes

\displaystyle E_{j0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r},\\
|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-1\end{subarray}}d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}!\cdot({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}})\cdot[\pm H^{d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}}]

as long as
H^{d-1}MV\leq N
. We now set
H=M\asymp_{d,D,\ell}\delta^{c}K
for a suitably large constant
c=c_{d,\ell}>0
,
which, together with the assumption on the size of
K
, ensures that the aforementioned conditions on
H,M
are satisfied. Using parts (
iv
)-(
vi
) of Lemma
3.5
, we perform the following operations on each box
E_{j0}
: Remove all of the directions making up
E_{j0}
except for one direction corresponding to a (nonzero) highest-degree monomial; enlarge
H
to
K
(at the loss of the factor
O_{d,D,\ell}(\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)})
in the lower bound); and remove the factor
d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}!
. In total, we replace each box
E_{j0}
by

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}})\cdot[\pm K^{d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}}]

for some
w_{j{\underline{u}}}\neq 1
. The theorem now follows, with
t=t_{0}\ell
, from the monotonicity property of box norms, where we have implicitly used the bound

\displaystyle|({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{w_{j{\underline{u}}}d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}})\cdot K^{d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}}|\ll_{D}VK^{d_{1w_{j{\underline{u}}}}}\ll_{d,D,\ell}VK^{d}\leq N.

10.
One more PET result

In the last section, we prove one more result required in our companion paper
[
20
]
.

Proposition 10.1
.

Let
d,r\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
d\geq 2
, and let
T>0
. There exist a positive integer
t=O_{d,r}(1)
and a positive real
C=O_{d,r,T}(1)
such that the following holds. Let
P\in{\mathbb{Z}}[z]
be a polynomial of degree
d
with coefficients of size at most
V
, and let
\delta>0
and
K,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
satisfy
C\delta^{-C}\leq K\leq T(N/V)^{1/d}
. Then for every
1
-bounded function
f:{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on
[N]
, the bound

(58)

\displaystyle\sum_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}}\mu_{K}({\underline{h}})\sum_{x}\E_{z\in[K]}\prod_{{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{r}}{\mathcal{C}}^{|{\underline{\epsilon}}|}f(x+P(z+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}))\geq\delta N

implies that

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{U^{t}(\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}])}^{2^{t}}\geq\frac{1}{C}\delta^{C}N,

where
\beta_{d}
is the leading coefficient of
P
.

We remark that the bounds in the preceding proposition are uniform in the choice of
\beta_{d}
: in particular,
\beta_{d}
can depend on
N
. This uniformity is crucially explored in
[
20
]
.

Proof.

Write
P(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\beta_{i}z^{i}
. Setting

\displaystyle P_{\underline{\epsilon}}(z,{\underline{h}}):=P(z+{\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}})-P({\underline{\epsilon}}\cdot{\underline{h}}),

we first show that the family

{\mathcal{Q}}:=(P_{\underline{\epsilon}}:\;{\underline{\epsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{r})

(ordered so that
P_{\underline{1}}
comes first) descends from
{\mathcal{P}}=(P-P(0))
. Indeed,
{\mathcal{Q}}
is normal, for which it is crucial that
d\geq 2
, since otherwise
P_{\underline{\epsilon}}(z,{\underline{h}})=\beta_{1}z
for every
{\underline{\epsilon}}
, as the contributions of
{\underline{h}}
are absorbed into the term not depending on
z
.
Moreover, a simple computation gives that

\displaystyle P_{\underline{\epsilon}}(z,{\underline{h}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{d}z^{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{u}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{r}:\;|{\underline{u}}|\leq d-i,\\
\supp({\underline{u}})\subseteq\supp({\underline{\epsilon}})\end{subarray}}{{|{\underline{u}}|+i}\choose{{\underline{u}}}}\beta_{|{\underline{u}}|+i}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}},

and hence the formula (
29
) holds with
w_{{\underline{\epsilon}}{\underline{u}}}=\mathbf{1}(\supp({\underline{u}})\subseteq\supp({\underline{\epsilon}}))
and
w_{\underline{u}}=0
. This immediately implies property (
i
) from Definition
4.5
. One also sees that for distinct
{\underline{\epsilon}},{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{r}
, the leading coefficient of

P_{\underline{\epsilon}}-P_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}
as a polynomial in
z
is
d\beta_{d}({\underline{\epsilon}}-{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime})\cdot{\underline{h}}
. In particular, the leading coefficient of
P_{\underline{\epsilon}}-P_{{\underline{\epsilon}}^{\prime}}
is a multilinear homogeneous polynomial of degree
1
. This gives property (
ii
) of Definition
4.5
, and property (
iii
) follows since
\beta_{d}
is the leading coefficient of
P
.
Hence
{\mathcal{Q}}
indeed descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
. This allows us to use Proposition
4.8
to find positive integers
r^{\prime}=O_{d,r}(1)
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{r^{\prime}}
such that
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}=\partial_{m_{r^{\prime}},\ldots,m_{1}}{\mathcal{Q}}
is linear in
z
and has size
s:=|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}|\ll_{d,r}1
.

Since Proposition
4.8
also gives that
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
descends from
{\mathcal{P}}
, the members of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
are multilinear homogeneous polynomials of degree
d-1
(homogeneity follows from remark (
iv
) below Definition
4.5
). Letting
{\underline{h}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r^{\prime\prime}})
from now on with
r^{\prime\prime}:=r+r^{\prime}
, we can enumerate the elements of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle b_{1}({\underline{h}})z,\ldots,b_{s}({\underline{h}})z,

where

\displaystyle b_{j}({\underline{h}})=\sum_{{\underline{u}}\in I_{j}}d!\beta_{d}{\underline{h}}^{\underline{u}}

and the sets
I_{j}\subseteq\{{\underline{u}}\in\{0,1\}^{r^{\prime\prime}}:\;|{\underline{u}}|=d-1\}
for
j\in[s]
are distinct and nonempty.

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz and van der Corput inequalities
r^{\prime}
times to (
58
) much like
21
21

21

Technically, the expression (
58
) differs from the one in Proposition
4.9
in that in (
58
), we have an extra averaging over
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
upfront. However, the argument for obtaining the bound in terms of an average of box norms for the averaged expression in (
58
) goes exactly the same way as in Proposition
4.9
. Check e.g. the proof of
[
23
, Corollary 4.3]
starting from (14) to see why such averaged counting operators are no harder to analyze than ones without an additional average over
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
upfront.
The applications of the Cauchy-Schwarz and van der Corput inequalities give us new shifting parameters
h_{r+1},\ldots,h_{r^{\prime\prime}}
; hence the polynomial parameters
{\underline{h}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{r^{\prime\prime}})
come from combining the “old”
h_{1},\ldots,h_{r}
with the “new”
h_{r+1},\ldots,h_{r^{\prime\prime}}
.
in the proof of Proposition
4.9
, we deduce the bound

\displaystyle\E_{{\underline{h}}\in[\pm H]^{r^{\prime\prime}}}\left\|f\right\|_{b_{1}({\underline{h}})\cdot[\pm H],(b_{1}({\underline{h}})-b_{2}({\underline{h}}))\cdot[\pm H],\ldots,(b_{1}({\underline{h}})-b_{s}({\underline{h}}))\cdot[\pm H]}^{2^{s}}\gg_{d,D,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}N^{D},

valid for all
H\ll_{d,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}K
. Theorem
1.10
combined with Lemma
3.5
(
v
) gives a positive integer
t=O_{d,r}(1)
for which

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{U^{t}(d!\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm H^{d}])}^{2^{t}}\gg_{d,r}\delta^{O_{d,r}(1)}N.

Properties (
iii
) and (
vii
) of Lemma
3.5
allow us to replace the progression
d!\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm H^{d}]
with
\beta_{d}\cdot[\pm N/\beta_{d}]
upon taking
H\asymp_{d,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}K\asymp_{d,\ell}\delta^{O_{d,\ell}(1)}N/V
, giving the claimed result.

∎
